NDA/BLA Multi-Disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 216956
Velsipity (etrasimod)

1 Executive Summary
Product Introduction
Etrasimod is an orally bioavailable sphingosine 1-phosphate (S1P) receptor modulator that
binds with high affinity to S1P receptors 1, 4, and 5. S1P receptor modulation partially and
reversibly sequesters specific lymphocytes in lymph nodes, thus reducing activated
lymphocytes in the tissue. The proposed indication is for the treatment of moderately to
severely active ulcerative colitis (UC) in adults.
S1P signaling is involved in multiple immune functions. The mechanism by which etrasimod
exerts therapeutic effects involves lymphocyte retention in lymphoid tissues and the reduction
of lymphocyte migration to sites of inflammation including the intestine. As UC is an immunemediated inflammatory disease, the retention of lymphocytes in the lymphoid tissue has the
potential to prevent recruitment of inflammatory cells, local release of proinflammatory
cytokines, and ongoing damage to the colonic mucosa. Reduction of this inflammatory
response may allow a decrease in disease activity and subsequent healing of the mucosa.
Etrasimod is a new molecular entity (NME) for UC. Other S1P modulators have been approved
for UC (e.g., Zeposia [ozanimod]), multiple sclerosis, psoriasis, and graft-versus-host disease.
The proposed dosing for etrasimod in UC is 2 mg orally once daily.

Conclusions on the Substantial Evidence of Effectiveness
The multidisciplinary review team recommends approval of this original NDA for Velsipity
(etrasimod) for the treatment of moderately to severely active UC in adults.
The Applicant submitted the results of two adequate and well-controlled phase 3 trials
(APD334-301 and APD334-302), which formed the basis of the substantial evidence of
effectiveness. A clinically meaningful and statistically significant benefit was demonstrated on
the coprimary endpoints for APD334-301 and primary endpoint for APD334-302. Multiple
secondary endpoints supported the primary efficacy results in each trial. Thus, the Applicant
has met the evidentiary standard for efficacy to support approval of etrasimod for the
proposed indication.
The safety profile was acceptable and generally consistent with known adverse reactions for
drugs in the same class. Identified risks will be mitigated through labeling.
The overall benefit-risk assessment supports approval as described in the Benefit-Risk
Assessment below.

Version date: October 12, 2018
Reference ID: 5259863

NDA/BLA Multi-Disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 216956
Velsipity (etrasimod)

Benefit-Risk Assessment
Benefit-Risk Summary and Assessment
Moderately to severely active ulcerative colitis (UC) is a serious chronic disease associated with morbidity and mortality when inadequately
treated. Multiple therapies are approved for the treatment of moderately to severely active UC; however, there remains a need for novel
therapies as not all patients will respond or have continued response to any given treatment.
Etrasimod demonstrated efficacy in inducing clinical remission after 12 weeks of treatment with 2 mg daily, and in maintaining clinical
remission when continued over 40 weeks of treatment. Efficacy was demonstrated in two adequate and well-controlled investigations:
•

APD334-301: A 52-week, randomized, double-blinded, placebo-controlled “treat-through” trial

•

APD334-302: A 12-week, randomized, double-blinded, placebo-controlled induction trial

In the 52-week, “treat-through” trial, etrasimod demonstrated efficacy in achieving the coprimary endpoints of clinical remission at Week 12
and Week 52, compared to placebo. In the 12-week induction trial, etrasimod demonstrated efficacy in achieving the primary endpoint of
clinical remission at Week 12, compared to placebo. In both trials, efficacy was demonstrated on multiple clinically relevant secondary
endpoints at Week 12 (APD334-301 and APD334-302) and Week 52 (APD334-301), compared to placebo. Overall, the results provided
substantial evidence of effectiveness for etrasimod in the treatment of moderately to severely active UC in adults. Therefore, the Applicant has
met the evidentiary standard for efficacy to support approval of etrasimod for the proposed indication.
The safety profile of etrasimod 2 mg once daily for up to 52 weeks was acceptable and supports approval of the dosing regimen. The safety
database included data from the 52-week phase 3 trial APD334-301, the 12-week phase 3 trial APD334-302, and 12-week phase 2 trial APD334003. The size of the safety database was adequate to support approval of the proposed dosing regimen. However, the size of the controlled 52week trial does not allow for a definitive characterization of the potential for rare, serious safety concerns (e.g., mortality, malignancy,
progressive multifocal leukoencephalopathy [PML], cryptococcal meningitis) that have been observed in S1P receptor modulators, including
ozanimod (ZEPOSIA) that is approved for the treatment of moderately to severely active UC. There are several known potential risks related to
the S1P receptor modulator class which were observed in the etrasimod development program (i.e., infections, bradyarrhythmia and
atrioventricular conduction delays, liver injury, macular edema, increased blood pressure, and respiratory effects), which were consistent with
other S1P receptor modulators. The most common adverse reactions observed in UC subjects treated with etrasimod during the 52-week trial
Version date: October 12, 2018

NDA/BLA Multi-Disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 216956
Velsipity (etrasimod)

2 Therapeutic Context
Analysis of Condition
Ulcerative colitis (UC) is a chronic gastrointestinal inflammatory disorder that involves the
colonic surface mucosa, including the infiltration of neutrophils and other inflammatory cells
into the epithelium and submucosa of the colon. The etiology of UC is multifactorial, but likely
includes a dysregulated mucosal immune response against commensal non-pathogenic bacteria
of the colon, resulting in bowel inflammation. Onset of disease most commonly occurs between
15 and 40 years of age. Prevalence estimates per 100,000 persons in North America range from
139.8 (Quebec, Canada) to 286.3 (Minnesota, USA), while in Europe they range from 2.42
(Romania, 2003) to 505 (Norway, 1991-94), including 412 in Germany (2010), 340 in Hungary
(2013), and 90.8 in the UK (1989). Globally, the prevalence of UC continues to rise, particularly
in North America and Europe, and is expected to increase in newly industrialized countries in
Africa, Asia, and South America (Ng et al. 2017).
The clinical course of UC is characterized by a lifelong course of remissions and exacerbations.
Patients with UC suffer from recurrent episodes of diarrhea, rectal bleeding, weight loss,
abdominal pain, fever, and are at an increased risk of perforated bowel, toxic megacolon, and
colorectal cancer. The estimated risk of colorectal cancer is approximately 2% after 10 years,
5% to 10% after 20 years, and 12% to 30% after 30 to 35 years of UC (Bernstein et al. 2001;
Eaden, Abrams, and Mayberry 2001; Feuerstein and Cheifetz 2014).
Patients have a 10% cumulative risk of colectomy 5 years after diagnosis, and 15% at 10 years
after diagnosis (Fumery et al. 2018) However, with colectomy, there is a 50% risk of continued
inflammation in the residual intestinal pouch (pouchitis); after 10 years, approximately 12% of
patients experience pouch failure and require conversion to a permanent ileostomy (Zezos and
Saibil 2015; Mark-Christensen et al. 2018). Surgical complications of proctocolectomy with
ileostomy include stenosis, prolapse, and other abdominal/pelvic sequelae including small
bowel obstruction, fistula, infection, persistent pain, unhealed perineal wound, sexual and
bladder dysfunction, and infertility.
Patients with UC may also experience extra-intestinal manifestations including primary
sclerosing cholangitis or eye, joint, or skin manifestations. Improved intestinal
disease activity in UC is associated with an improvement in extra-intestinal manifestations.

Analysis of Current Treatment Options
The goals of UC treatment include decreasing mucosal inflammation, reducing signs and
symptoms, reducing long-term corticosteroid use, reducing colon cancer risk, and improving
patient quality of life.

Version date: October 12, 2018
Reference ID: 5259863

26

NDA/BLA Multi-Disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 216956
Velsipity (etrasimod)
Treatment of UC is challenging due to its multifactorial nature and incompletely understood
pathogenesis. UC is also a chronic disease, and in general, most drugs that are initiated for
induction of remission are continued as maintenance therapy, if they are effective (Feuerstein
et al. 2020).
Therapeutic options for the treatment of moderately to severely active UC include a variety of
pharmacologic therapies such as tumor necrosis factor (TNF)-α antagonists (infliximab,
adalimumab, golimumab), anti-integrin agents (vedolizumab), Janus kinase (JAK) inhibitors
(tofacitinib, upadacitinib), interleukin (IL) 12/23 antagonist (ustekinumab), sphingosine 1phosphate (S1P) receptor modulator (ozanimod), and other immunomodulators (thiopurines,
methotrexate). However, a proportion of patients who have initially responded to these
therapies may subsequently lose response and experience recurrence of UC symptoms. Recent
systematic reviews and meta-analyses have reported that the estimated incidence rates of loss
of response (LOR) to vedolizumab was 39.8 (per 100 person-years of follow-up) in patients with
UC (Peyrin-Biroulet et al. 2019) and the loss of response to anti-TNF agents was approximately
25% in one year and in 33% in 3 years in patients with IBD (Guberna et al. 2021).
Although multiple approved therapies are available, there remains a need for novel therapies
for patients who have had an inadequate response, intolerance, or loss of response to available
therapies and would benefit from the availability of additional therapeutic options.
The currently approved systemic therapies for the treatment of moderately to severely active
UC are summarized in Table 1.
Table 1. Summary of Systemic Therapies for The Treatment of Moderately to Severely Active

3 Regulatory Background
U.S. Regulatory Actions and Marketing History
At the time of this review, etrasimod has not been authorized for marketing in any country
outside of the U.S.
On October 14, 2023, Pfizer, Inc. submitted a new drug application (NDA) for new molecular
entity (NME), etrasimod. Etrasimod (also referred to as APD334, APD334 L-arginine or
AR401959) is a S1P receptor modulator. The proposed indication for etrasimod is the treatment
moderately to severely active UC in adults at a recommended dose of 2 mg orally once daily.

Financial Disclosure
Etrasimod was originally developed by Arena Pharmaceuticals Inc. (Arena), which was acquired
by Pfizer Inc. (Pfizer) on March 11, 2022. The two covered clinical studies (APD334-301 and
APD334-302) were conducted, completed, and achieved database lock before the completion
of this acquisition. Per guidance received from FDA in Pre-NDA meeting minutes (Reference ID
4992019) dated May 31, 2022, since the acquisition occurred within 1 year of conclusion of the
studies, both Arena and Pfizer are considered sponsors for the covered clinical studies. As such,
the financial disclosures were provided pertaining to both entities (collectively “Applicant”).
Financial disclosure information was self-disclosed by investigators. All investigators were
assessed for equity interest, significant payments of other sorts, other compensation by the
Sponsor, and propriety interest. Self-disclosures pertaining to Arena have been collected from
97% of investigators. Per FDA’s request communicated in the same Pre-NDA meeting minutes,
the Applicant has made efforts to collect updated financial disclosure information from
investigators identifying both Arena and Pfizer as sponsors. As of the disclosure cutoff date of
September 6, 2022, for this NDA, 33% of investigators have provided the requested updates;
due diligence to obtain these updates will continue for 1 year after study completion for each
covered clinical study.
Seven investigators had reportable financial interests of any significant payments from the
Applicant for the covered study, APD334-301, such as a grant to fund ongoing research,
compensation in the form of equipment, retainer for ongoing consultation, or honoraria. The
seven investigators represent 1% (7/793) of the investigators for APD334-301. Given the total
number of investigators, the potential impact of this financial interest was not expected to
influence the trial outcome.
Five investigators had reportable financial interests of any significant payments from the
Applicant for the covered study, APD334-302, such as a grant to fund ongoing research,
compensation in the form of equipment, retainer for ongoing consultation, or honoraria. The
five investigators represent 0.5% (5/933) of the investigators for APD334-302. Given the total
number of investigators, the potential impact of this financial interest was not expected to
influence the trial outcome.
A detailed summary of financial disclosures is included in the Appendix Section 16.2.

Product Quality
Drug Substance
Etrasimod arginine is the arginine salt of the active moiety etrasimod and is a synthetic small
molecule sphingosine 1-phosphate receptor modulator. Etrasimod has not been previously
approved or marketed in the United States and therefore, it is classified as a New Molecular
Entity (NME).
Etrasimod arginine is a white, off-white, to light brown crystalline solid. It has a melting point of
203.5C -208.1C, a pKa of 4.5, and a log P of 6.8 – 9.0. It is non-hygroscopic with a solubility of
1.38mg/mL in water at pH of 8.9. To date, one thermodynamically stable crystalline form has
been identified for etrasimod arginine. The drug substance as free acid has one chiral center,
and the R-configuration has been confirmed by single crystal X-ray technique.
Etrasimod arginine is the L-arginine salt of (R)-2-(7-(4-cyclopentyl-3-(trifluoromethyl)benzyloxy)1,2,3,4-tetrahydrocyclopenta[b]indol-3-yl) acetic acid. It has a molecular formula of
C32H40F3N5O5 (C26H26F3NO3 as free acid), a molecular weight of 631.69 g/mol (457.48
g/mol as free acid), and the chemical structure as shown in Figure 1.

Drug Product
The drug product, Velsipity (etrasimod) Tablets, 2 mg is indicated for the treatment of
moderately to severely active ulcerative colitis in adults. The recommended dosage is one
tablet taken orally once daily. The tablet should be swallowed as a whole with or without food.
Velsipity (etrasimod) tablets are green, round, film-coated immediate release tablets debossed
with “ETR” on one side and “2” on the other side. Each tablet contains 2.762 mg of etrasimod
arginine equivalent to 2 mg of etrasimod as the active ingredient and magnesium stearate,
mannitol, microcrystalline cellulose, and sodium starch glycolate, with a film coating containing
FD&C blue #1/brilliant blue FCF aluminum lake, FD&C blue #2/indigo carmine aluminum lake,
FD&C yellow #5/tartrazine aluminum lake, macrogol 4000 JP/PEG 3350, polyvinyl alcohol
(partially hydrolyzed), talc, and titanium dioxide as inactive ingredients.
Velsipity (etrasimod) tablets are supplied as 30 tablets in a child resistant 100 mL high density
polyethylene (HDPE) white, round bottle with 4 g silica gel as desiccant integrated directly into
the 45 mm white polypropylene (PP) closure.
Velsipity (etrasimod) tablets are manufactured in accordance with cGMP requirements by Penn
Pharmaceutical Services Limited, United Kingdom and it is tested and released against a
specification that assures the identity, strength, purity, and quality of the drug product at
release and throughout its proposed expiration dating period of 24 months. The proposed

5 Nonclinical Pharmacology/Toxicology
Executive Summary
From a nonclinical perspective, no approvability issues have been identified for etrasimod at
the proposed clinical dose of 2 mg/day.
Nonclinical studies conducted with etrasimod include pharmacology studies, safety
pharmacology studies, pharmacokinetics, repeat-dose toxicology studies of up to six months
duration in rats and nine months duration in dogs, a standard genotoxicity battery, two-year
carcinogenicity studies in mice and rats, a standard battery of developmental and reproductive
toxicity studies in rats and rabbits, and an in vitro phototoxicity study.
Etrasimod is a sphingosine-1-phosphate receptor subtype 1, 4, and 5 (S1P1,4,5) agonist with no
significant pharmacological activity at S1P2 or S1P3 receptors. Etrasimod agonism of S1P1
reduces lymphocyte egress from lymph nodes, resulting in decreased circulating lymphocytes.
In a standard battery of nonclinical safety pharmacology studies, etrasimod had no significant
effects on hERG channel current in vitro, cardiovascular function in dogs, CNS parameters in
rats, or respiratory function in rats. In an in vitro assay using human atrial cardiomyocytes,
etrasimod activated IKAch channels at concentrations less than the predicted steady state Cmax
and Ctrough values at the clinical etrasimod dose of 2 mg/day; this finding may provide an
underlying mechanism for the transient etrasimod-induced decrease in heart rate observed in
clinical trials.
In repeat-dose toxicity studies, mortalities, decreased body weight gain, and histopathological
findings in the liver and thymus were observed in rats at exposures > 1000-fold the exposure at
the maximum recommended human dose (MRHD) of 2 mg/day (based on AUC comparison). No
adverse effects were observed in rats or dogs at exposures up to 738- and 446-fold,
respectively, the exposure at the MRHD (based on AUC comparison). Dose-dependent
decreases in circulating lymphocytes were observed in both rats and dogs and correlated with
lymphoid depletion in the lymph nodes and spleen of rats only; these findings were attributed
to the pharmacological activity of etrasimod and not considered adverse.
Etrasimod did not demonstrate genotoxic potential in a standard battery of in vitro and in vivo
(b) (4)
studies. Process-related impurity
was considered positive for mutagenicity in the
(b) (4)
bacterial reverse mutation assay and is controlled in the
manufacturing product at
(b) (4)
<
µg/day in accordance with ICH M7(R1), option 2.
In a two-year carcinogenicity study in mice, a statistically significant positive dose-response in
the incidences of hemangiosarcoma malignant tumor (multicentric neoplasm) and combined

Version date: October 12, 2018
Reference ID: 5259863

41

NDA/BLA Multi-Disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 216956
Velsipity (etrasimod)
hemangioma/hemangiosarcoma were observed at exposures ≥ 46-fold the exposure at the
MRHD (based on AUC comparison). In a two-year carcinogenicity study in rats, no evidence of
carcinogenic potential was observed at exposures up to 88- and 198-fold the exposure at the
MRHD in males and females, respectively (based on AUC comparison). The Executive
Carcinogenicity Assessment Committee concurred with these conclusions. Notably, these
findings are consistent with those observed in studies with other approved S1P receptor
modulators.
No adverse effects of etrasimod were observed on male or female fertility parameters at doses
up to 42 and 121 times, respectively, the exposure at the MRHD (based on AUC comparison). In
an embryo-fetal development study in rats, no maternal toxicity was observed at up to 23-fold
the exposure at the MRHD, but a developmental NOAEL could not be determined based on the
observed dose-dependent increase in the incidence of post-implantation loss (primarily early
resorptions), decreased number and proportion of viable fetuses, and dose-dependent
incidence of fetal malformations (aortic vessel abnormalities and septal defects) at exposures ≥
6 times the exposure at the MRHD. In an embryo-fetal development study in rabbits, no
maternal toxicity was observed at up to 12 times the exposure at MRHD. Based on the findings
of increased post-implantation loss, decreased litter proportions of viable fetuses, and
increased fetal malformations (aortic arch defects and fused sternebrae) at exposures 7 and 11
times the exposure at the MRHD, the NOAEL for developmental toxicity in rabbits was
considered the low dose of 2 mg/kg/day (approximately the same exposure as MRHD). In a preand post-natal development study in rats, maternal toxicity including the premature
termination of one female during delivery, increased post-implantation loss, increased number
of females with stillborn pups, increased number of stillborn pups per litter, decreased viability
index, and decreased lactation index was observed at exposures 10 and 24 times the exposure
at the MRHD; thus, the NOAEL for maternal toxicity was considered the low dose of 0.4
mg/kg/day (approximately 2 times the exposure at the MRHD). Based on findings of increased
perinatal mortality, adverse clinical signs and findings of decreased body weight/body weight
gain during the preweaning period, as well as reduced number of implantations and increased
rate of pre-implantation loss correlated with a decrease in the number of live embryos per
litter, a developmental NOAEL could not be determined (with effects occurring at exposures
less than that predicted at the MRHD).
No novel excipients are used in the formulation of the etrasimod drug product, and all
excipients are used at acceptable levels that are consistent with those used in other FDA
approved, orally administered products. No leachables are expected given the solid tablet
formulation. Elemental impurities in the etrasimod drug substance meet the allowable limits
established by ICH Q3D, option 1, for oral administration. All impurities, including those with
genotoxic potential, are controlled in accordance with ICH Q3A and/or ICH M7(R1) as
appropriate. Thus, there are no safety concerns regarding excipients, impurities, or extractables
and leachables from the drug product or container closure system.

Pharmacology
Etrasimod is a sphingosine-1-phosphate receptor subtype 1, 4, and 5 (S1P1,4,5) agonist with no
significant pharmacological activity at S1P2 or S1P3 receptors. At clinically relevant
concentrations, etrasimod had no significant binding or activity when tested against panels of
other receptors, ion channels, transporters, and enzymes. Etrasimod agonism of S1P1 reduces
lymphocyte egress from lymph nodes, resulting in decreased circulating lymphocytes. In
nonclinical species, etrasimod produced a dose-dependent decrease in blood lymphocytes, with
EC50 values of 25 ng/mL in rats and dogs and 45 ng/mL in mice and monkeys (approximately 4.5
and 2.5 times lower, respectively, than the steady state Cmax at the clinical etrasimod dose of 2
mg/day; 113 ng/mL).
A standard battery of nonclinical safety pharmacology studies did not demonstrate significant
safety concerns regarding acute etrasimod administration. Etrasimod inhibited hERG signaling
in vitro, with a maximal inhibitory effect of 59% at the highest tested dose of 3 µM; a specific
IC50 value could not be determined based on the study design but is likely between 1000-3000
nM (460-1370 ng/mL; 4-12 times the steady state Cmax at the MRHD). In an in vitro assay using
human atrial cardiomyocytes, etrasimod activated IKAch channels (EC50: 29.9 nM or 18.9 ng/mL;
6 times lower than the steady state Cmax at the MRHD). This finding may suggest an underlying
mechanism for the transient drug-induced decrease in heart rate observed in clinical trials with
etrasimod in various indications. A single oral dose of etrasimod did not produce acute CNS or
clinically relevant respiratory effects in rats (NOELs: 350 mg/kg/day) or cardiovascular effects in
dogs (NOEL: 350 mg/kg/day).

Distribution
• Etrasimod is highly bound (> 95%) to plasma proteins in multiple

• No selective association of etrasimod with the cellular component

• Etrasimod is primarily portioned into the extracellular component
of blood in multiple species6

In vitro
• The disappearance half-life of etrasimod was >60 minutes in liver

approximately 30 minutes in microsomes from rabbit8
• Metabolites identified using human liver microsomes included M1

as well as M4 and M5 (glucuronide conjugates of M2 and M3)9
• Metabolites identified using hepatocytes from various species
were 29 in total, with the most abundant including M1, M3, and
M6, as well as hydroxyl glucuronides M14, M15, and M1710
• M3 and M6 were both observed in hepatocytes from rat, dog, and
monkey10
• No unique human metabolites identified in vitro9,10
• Glucuronidation of etrasimod is primarily catalyzed by UGT1A7 in
liver microsomes from humans as well as mouse, rat, dog, and
monkey11
In vivo
• Primary human metabolites M3 and M6 were detected in the
plasma and feces of rats3 and feces of dogs5
• Metabolism of etrasimod in dogs involved oxidation,
dehydrogenation, and conjugation via sulfate or glucuronidation5
Excretion
Rats
• Elimination primarily via the bile/feces (> 80% within 48 hours, >
95% within 168 hours post-dose)3
Dogs
• Elimination primarily via the feces, with 68% recovered by 96
hours post-dose and > 81% through 168 hours post-dose5

Toxicology
General Toxicology
Repeat-dose toxicity studies of up to six months duration in rats and nine months duration in
dogs were conducted to support the chronic use of etrasimod in humans. Mortalities,
decreased body weight gain, and histopathological findings in the liver and thymus were
observed in rats at exposures > 1000-fold the exposure at the MRHD, based on AUC
comparison. No adverse effects were observed in rats or dogs at exposures up to 738- and 446fold, respectively, the exposure at the MRHD.

Study TX10013: APD334 L-Arginine: Oral Bone Marrow Micronucleus Test in Rats
Key Study Findings:
• Etrasimod was positive for inducing bone marrow micronuclei in female rats at the highest

GLP compliance: Yes
Test system: Sprague-Dawley rats
Study is valid: Yes
Carcinogenicity
Etrasimod was positive for carcinogenicity in mice but negative for carcinogenicity in rats.
In a two-year carcinogenicity study in mice, a statistically significant positive dose-response in
the incidences of hemangiosarcoma malignant tumor (multicentric neoplasm) and combined
hemangioma/hemangiosarcoma were observed in males and females at 6 and 20 mg/kg/day
compared to controls. These doses correspond to 46- and 134-fold, respectively, the exposure
at the MRHD of 2 mg/day (based on AUC comparison). The Executive Carcinogenicity
Assessment Committee concurred with these findings and considered the study to be
adequately conducted.
In a two-year carcinogenicity study in rats, no carcinogenic findings were observed in males or
females administered up to 20 mg/kg/day for 86 to 89 weeks, after which mortalities precluded
continued conduct of the study. This dose corresponds to 88- and 198-fold the exposure at the
MRHD in males and females, respectively (based on AUC comparison). The Executive
Carcinogenicity Assessment Committee concurred with these findings and considered the study
to be adequately conducted.

Version date: October 12, 2018
Reference ID: 5259863

Embryo-Fetal Development
Dose-Range Finding Studies in Rats and Rabbits
Two dose-range finding studies were conducted with etrasimod to determine the doses for the
definitive embryo-fetal development (EFD) study in rats. In the first study using doses of 25, 75,
150, and 250 mg/kg/day (Study TX14016), severe maternal toxicity and decreased intrauterine
survival were observed such that, among all treated groups, there was only a single viable fetus
from the 25 mg/kg/day group and no others. The single fetus in the 25 mg/kg/day group (121
times the clinical dose, based on HED) was observed with a meningocele.
In the second study using doses of 2, 5, 10, and 20 mg/kg/day (Study TX14025), increased postimplantation loss with corresponding lower numbers/proportions of viable fetuses was
observed at ≥ 5 mg/kg/day. A meningocele was observed in a single fetus each in the 5 and 10
mg/kg/day groups, and spina bifida was observed in one fetus at 10 mg/kg/day. These findings
occurred at > 21 times the exposure at the clinical dose, based on AUC (based on the available
AUC data at 4 mg/kg/day) or 24 times the clinical dose, based on HED.
While these findings suggest the potential of etrasimod exposure during organogenesis to
result in neural defects, the toxicological significance of these findings is unclear due to the
sporadic nature of the findings, lack of clear dose effect, and overall developmental failure at
higher doses. However, the presence of these findings is unsurprising, as S1P receptors play an
essential role in embryogenesis. Based on the totality of findings in these studies, the doses in
the definitive EFD study in rats were set to 1, 2, and 4 mg/kg/day.
In the dose-range finding study in rabbits using doses of 2, 25, 75, and 150 mg/kg/day (Study
TX14018), moribundity or abortion were observed in 5/6 females at 75 mg/kg/day and all
Version date: October 12, 2018
Reference ID: 5259863

56

NDA/BLA Multi-Disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 216956
Velsipity (etrasimod)
females at 150 mg/kg/day (726 and 1452 times the clinical dose, respectively, based on HED).
The single surviving female at 75 mg/kg/day was observed to have increased post-implantation
loss. At 25 mg/kg/day (242 times the clinical dose, based on HED), increased post-implantation
loss and a lower number of viable fetuses were observed. No adverse effects were observed at
2 mg/kg/day (19 times the clinical dose, based on HED, or less than the clinical dose, based on
AUC). Based on these findings, the doses in the definitive EFD study in rabbits were set to 2, 10,
and 20 mg/kg/day.

Key Study Findings
• Based on the premature termination of one female during delivery (4 mg/kg/day), increased
post-implantation loss (4 mg/kg/day), increased number of females with stillborn pups (2
and 4 mg/kg/day), increased number of stillborn pups per litter (2 mg/kg/day), decreased

of pre-implantation loss correlating with a decrease in the number of live embryos per litter
at 4 mg/kg/day, a developmental NOAEL could not be established (with effects occurring at
exposures less than that expected at the clinical dose).

• Viability index was decreased at 2 and 4 mg/kg/day, while
lactation index was decreased at 4 mg/kg/day.
F1 Generation
Preweaning
• An increased number of pups were found dead or found
missing and presumed to be cannibalized, predominantly
between PND 0-4, at 2 and 4 mg/kg/day.
• Clinical observations from birth through PND 4 included cold
to touch, suspected dehydration (based on skin turgor),
discolored skin, skin pallor, and swelling at 4 mg/kg/day and
no milk band in the stomach at 2 and 4 mg/kg/day.
• After culling (PND 4-21), clinical signs included suspected
dehydration (based on skin turgor) and thin appearance at 4
mg/kg/day.
• Decreased maternal feed consumption during lactation
corresponded to decreased pup body weights and body
weight gain at 2 and 4 mg/kg/day. Pup body weights at the
end of the preweaning period (PND 21) were 10%, 12%, and
20% lower than controls at 0.4, 2, and 4 mg/kg/day,
respectively, while total body weight gains were 11%, 14%,
and 23% lower than controls, respectively.
• Macroscopic findings in prematurely terminated or stillborn
pups included full body edema at 4 mg/kg/day and no milk in
the stomach at 2 and 4 mg/kg/day.
Postweaning
• A reduced number of implantations and increased rate of preimplantation loss were observed at 4 mg/kg/day and
correlated with a decrease in the number of live embryos per
litter in this group.

Other Toxicology Studies
Phototoxicity
Etrasimod did not demonstrate phototoxic potential in an in vitro neutral red uptake
phototoxicity assay using BALB/c mouse fibroblasts.
Excipients and Leachables
Etrasimod is orally administered at a dose of 2 mg/day. The etrasimod drug product is a round,
immediate release, film-coated tablet available in a 2 mg form only. There are no novel
excipients or excipients of human or animal origin used in the manufacturing of the etrasimod

Velsipity (etrasimod)

6 Clinical Pharmacology
Executive Summary
Etrasimod is a sphingosine 1-phosphate (S1P) receptor modulator, which binds to S1P receptors
1, 4, and 5. It is a new molecular entity with a proposed indication for the treatment of
moderately to severely active ulcerative colitis (UC) in adults.
In support of this application, the safety and effectiveness of etrasimod were assessed in phase
3 trials APD334-301 and APD334-302 in subjects 16 years of age and older with moderately to
(b) (4)
severely active UC.
The clinical pharmacology review
evaluated the appropriateness of the general dosing instructions, dosage for specific patient
populations, drug-drug interaction (DDI), as well as population PK (PopPK), and exposureresponse (E-R) analyses.
Overall, the proposed oral dosage at 2 mg daily (QD) for the general adult patient population is
supported by a significantly higher proportion of subjects achieving clinical remission at Weeks
12 and 52 compared to placebo as well as an acceptable safety profile. No dosage adjustment is
recommended for patients with mild to severe renal impairment or mild to moderate hepatic
impairment. However, use of etrasimod in patients with severe hepatic impairment is not
recommended.
Co-administration of etrasimod with the following drugs is not recommended:
• Drugs that are moderate or strong inhibitors of both CYP2C9 and CYP3A4.
•

Drugs that are combined CYP3A4 (strong), CYP2C8 (moderate) and CYP2C9 (moderate)
inducer.

•

Drugs that are moderate or strong CYP2C8 or CYP3A4 inhibitor in patients who are known
to be CYP2C9 poor metabolizers.
Recommendations

The Office of Clinical Pharmacology has reviewed this NDA submission and found it acceptable
for approval for the treatment of adult patients with moderately to severely active UC from a
clinical pharmacology standpoint.

NDA/BLA Multi-Disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 216956
Velsipity (etrasimod)

Summary of Clinical Pharmacology Assessment
Pharmacology and Clinical Pharmacokinetics
Pharmacology
Etrasimod is a S1P receptor modulator, which binds with high affinity to S1P receptors 1, 4, and
5. Etrasimod partially and reversibly blocks the capacity of lymphocytes to egress from
lymphoid organs, reducing the number of lymphocytes in the peripheral blood, thereby
lowering the number of activated lymphocytes in the tissue. However, the mechanism by which
etrasimod exerts therapeutic effects in UC is not fully known.
Clinical Pharmacokinetics
Clinical pharmacokinetics of etrasimod have been characterized in healthy subjects and
subjects with UC and briefly summarized below. Overall, the pharmacokinetics of etrasimod is
similar in healthy subjects and subjects with UC.
Absorption
Following oral administration, the Tmax of etrasimod is approximately 4 hours (ranging 2 – 8
hours). Following single dose administration from 0.1 mg to 5 mg, the Cmax and AUC for
etrasimod increase proportionally by 59 and 55-fold, respectively, with a 50-fold increase in
dose. Following the multiple dose administration from 0.7 mg QD to 2 mg QD, the Cmax and
AUCtau at steady-state increased more than dose proportionally by 3.67 and 3.63-fold,
respectively, with a 2.86-fold increase in dose. Steady state was reached by 7 days following 2
mg QD dosing, with a mean Cmax of 113 ng/mL and AUCtau of 2162 h*ng/mL.
Food effect
No clinically significant differences in the Cmax and AUC of etrasimod were observed following
administration with a high-fat, high-calorie meal, compared to the fasted conditions (< 2%).
Food intake can result in delayed absorption (the median Tmax) by 2 hours.
Distribution
The mean apparent volume of distribution of etrasimod (Vz/F) is 66 L. Human plasma proteins
binding of etrasimod is approximately 97.9%.
Elimination
The mean plasma elimination t1/2 of etrasimod is approximately 30 hours. The T1/2 of the two
most abundant metabolites, M3 and M6, ranged from 46 to 83 hours and 52 to 70 hours,
respectively.

Version date: October 12, 2018
Reference ID: 5259863

66

NDA/BLA Multi-Disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 216956
Velsipity (etrasimod)
Metabolism
Etrasimod is extensively metabolized by multiple enzymes via oxidation, dehydrogenation,
glucuronidation, sulfation and a combination of these reactions, though unchanged etrasimod
is the main circulating component in plasma as shown in Figure 2. In vitro assays indicate that
etrasimod is metabolized via CYP2C8 (38%), CYP2C9 (37%), and CYP3A4 (22%), and with minor
contribution (1%) via CYP2C19 and CYP2J2. The two most abundant circulating metabolites of
etrasimod, AR503641 (M3) and AR504344 (M6), accounted for <20% of the parent AUC as well
as <10% of total radioactivity in human plasma. In vitro studies indicated that M3 was formed
primarily by CYP2C9*1 and to a lesser extent by CYP2C8 and CYP2C19. M3 was further
metabolized to M6.
Figure 2. Metabolic Pathways for Etrasimod and Metabolites in Human

Source: Clinical Study Report APD334-107, Figure 5

Etrasimod has one chiral center. Analysis of cross-subject pooled plasma samples obtained
from Study APD334-005 indicated that etrasimod does not undergo any in vivo
stereoconversion to its opposite enantiomer, AR401967.
Excretion
Following a single oral dose of radiolabeled etrasimod 2 mg, approximately 82% of the
administered radioactivity was recovered in feces and 4.9% in urine over 336 hours. 57.2% of
the total administered radioactivity was recovered in feces by 144 hours post-dose. In feces,
11.2% was unchanged etrasimod and 41% was metabolites. Unchanged etrasimod was not
detected in urine.

Version date: October 12, 2018
Reference ID: 5259863

67

NDA/BLA Multi-Disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 216956
Velsipity (etrasimod)
PK in Subjects with Ulcerative Colitis
The PK of etrasimod was characterized in subjects with UC by population PK analysis using
sparse PK samples collected in phase 2 and phase 3 trials.
In subjects with UC, the model estimated mean apparent total clearance (CL/F) of etrasimod
after oral administration was 1.06 L/h, mean apparent volume of distribution at steady state
(Vss/F) was 70 L for a 70 kg subject, and the mean effective half-life (t1/2,eff) was 33.9 hours.
PopPK estimated that subjects with UC would have slightly higher Cmax,ss and AUCss (about 10%)
compared to healthy subjects of the same body weight, following 2 mg QD dosing. Hence,
disease status is not expected to have a clinically significant effect on etrasimod PK exposure in
subjects with UC.
Pharmacodynamics
In subjects with UC, mean lymphocyte counts decreased to approximately 50% of baseline at 2
weeks after treatment initiation and the lower lymphocyte counts were maintained during
treatment. After discontinuing etrasimod, the median time for peripheral blood lymphocytes to
return to the normal range was 2.6 weeks, with approximately 90% of subjects in the normal
range within 4.7 weeks in phase 3 trials.
General Dosing and Therapeutic Individualization
General Dosing
The recommended dosage regimen for adult patients with moderate to severe UC is 2 mg QD
without regards of food.
The effectiveness of etrasimod in adult patients was supported by the efficacy results from the
phase 3 trials APD334-301 and APD334-302. A greater proportion of subjects treated with
etrasimod 2 mg QD achieved clinical remission at Week 12 compared to placebo (27.4% versus
7.5% in APD334-301; 26.2% versus 15.2% in APD334-302) during the initial treatment period.
Similarly, a greater proportion of subjects treated with etrasimod 2 mg QD achieved clinical
remission at Week 52 compared to placebo (32.5% versus 6.7%) in APD334-301. APD334-301
was a treat-through design in which all patients continued assigned treatment without
rerandomization of responders following assessment of early response at Week 12.
The Applicant has proposed a dosage of 2 mg QD in adults
PK, clinical efficacy, and safety data are available from only 1
pediatric subject (16 years-old, female, body weight of 42 kg) received etrasimod 2 mg QD.
Although adolescents aged 16 to less than 18 years of age were allowed to enroll in phase 3
trials APD334-301 and APD334-302, a total three adolescent patients were enrolled, and two
subjects received placebo. No other clinical trials enrolled adolescent subjects.

Therapeutic Individualization
Renal Impairment
No dose adjustment is needed in patients with renal impairment. Based on a dedicated renal
impairment study, renal impairment has no clinically important effects on the PK of etrasimod.
The effect of dialysis on etrasimod was not assessed.
Hepatic Impairment
No dosage adjustment is needed for patients with mild to moderate hepatic impairment (ChildPugh A and B). Use of etrasimod in patients with severe hepatic impairment (Child-Pugh C) is
not recommended based on 57% higher etrasimod exposure compared with subjects with
normal liver function given multiple doses higher than 2 mg have not been studied.
Drug-Drug Interactions
Effects of Other Drugs on Etrasimod:
•
•

•

In vitro data suggest that etrasimod is mainly metabolized via CYP2C8, CYP2C9, and CYP3A4,
and with only minor contribution from CYP2C19 and CYP2J2.
CYP2C8 or CYP3A4 inhibitor alone: Only moderate effect of a strong inhibitor of a single CYP
enzyme was observed. Overall, the risk for clinically relevant DDIs appears low with
concomitant medications that inhibit only CYP2C8 or CYP3A4.
o CYP3A4: Itraconazole (a strong inhibitor of CYP3A4) had little impact on the Cmax of
etrasimod (increased by 6%) but increased AUC0-inf of etrasimod by 32%.
o CYP2C8: Gemfibrozil (a strong inhibitor of CYP2C8) had little impact on the Cmax of
etrasimod (increased by 12%) but increased AUC0-inf of etrasimod by 36%. Thus, no dose
adjustment is warranted.
Drugs that are both CYP2C9 and CYP3A4 inhibitors: The AUC of etrasimod increased by 84%
when co-administered with fluconazole (a moderate inhibitor of CYP2C9 and CYP3A4, and
strong inhibitor of CYP2C19). While fluconazole inhibits multiple CYP enzymes, it appears
that the effect of fluconazole is mainly by inhibition of CYP2C9 given a strong CYP3A4

Version date: October 12, 2018
Reference ID: 5259863

69

NDA/BLA Multi-Disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 216956
Velsipity (etrasimod)

•

inhibitor (itraconazole) increased AUC by 32%, although additive effects of moderate
inhibition of both CYP2C9 and CYP3A4 cannot be ruled out. Thus, co-administration of
etrasimod with a drug that is a dual inhibitor of CYP2C9 and CYP3A4 (e.g., fluconazole) is not
recommended.
Rifampin: The AUC of etrasimod decreased by 49% when co-administered with rifampin (a
moderate inducer of CYP2C8 and CYP2C9 and a strong inducer of CYP2C19 and CYP3A4).
Thus, co-administration of etrasimod with a drug that is a combined CYP3A4 (strong),
CYP2C8 (moderate) and CYP2C9 (moderate) inducer (i.e., rifampin) is not recommended.

Additionally, administration of etrasimod in patients who are CYP2C9 poor metabolizers and
receiving CYP2C8 or CYP3A4 moderate or strong inhibitors is not recommended. In patients
who are intermediate CYP2C9 metabolizers, the effect on etrasimod exposure when coadministered with CYP3A4 or CYP2C8 inhibitors is not known.
Effects of Etrasimod on Other Drugs:
DDI study with oral contraceptive:
Co-administration of etrasimod with an oral contraceptive (containing 30 μg ethinylestradiol
[EE] and 150 μg levonorgestrel [LVG]) increased Cmax,ss and AUCtau,ss of EE by 12% and 24%,
respectively, and increased Cmax,ss and AUCtau,ss of LVG by 22% and 32%, respectively. These
changes are not expected to be clinically relevant.
No notable differences in mean concentration-time profiles for follicle stimulating hormone
(FSH), luteinizing hormone (LH), estradiol, progesterone, and sex hormone binding globulin
(SHBG), as well as the incidents of Hoogland scores were observed between when oral
contraceptive was administered alone and co-administration of etrasimod and oral
contraceptive. Steady-state plasma PK parameters for etrasimod appear generally consistent
with previous findings for etrasimod when given alone, in a cross-study comparison. Thus, the
review team agrees with the Applicant’s proposal that no dosing adjustment is necessary when
etrasimod is co-administered with oral contraceptive.
No dosage adjustment based on other intrinsic or extrinsic factors is needed for adult subjects
with UC. See details in Section 6.3.2.
Outstanding Issues
There are no outstanding issues that would preclude the approval of etrasimod for the
currently proposed indication in adult patients with moderately to severely active UC from a
clinical pharmacology perspective.

Distribution
The mean apparent volume of distribution of etrasimod (Vz/F) is 66 L.
Human plasma proteins binding of etrasimod is approximately 97.9%.
Based on the in vitro studies, etrasimod is not a substrate or inhibitor of
human ABC transporters (P-gp, BCRP, and BSEP), and SLC transporters
(OATP1B1, OATP1B3, OAT1/3, OCT1, OCT2, and MATE1/2 K).
Elimination
Fecal (hepatobiliary) excretion was the predominant route of elimination for
etrasimod.
Following a single oral dose of radiolabeled etrasimod 2 mg, approximately
82% of the radioactivity was recovered in feces and 4.9% in urine over 336
hours.
Total administered radioactivity was recovered in urine and feces through the
last collection interval (336 hours postdose) with 3.00% of the total
administered radioactivity recovered in urine within the first 72 hours
postdose and 57.2% of the total administered radioactivity recovered in feces
by 144 hours postdose. Total of 11, 21, and 9 metabolites were observed in
plasma, urine, and feces, respectively. In feces, metabolites M3 (AR503641)
and M36 (APD334-oxy sulfate) accounted for a mean of 22.1% and 18.9% of
the dose (total administered radioactivity), respectively, and unchanged
etrasimod accounted for a mean of 11.2% of the dose. Unchanged etrasimod
was not detected in urine.
No major circulating metabolites were observed. The two most abundant
circulating metabolites of etrasimod, AR503641 (M3) and AR504344 (M6)
accounted for <20% of the parent AUC as well as <10% of total radioactivity
in human plasma.

Drug Information
The mean plasma elimination t1/2 of etrasimod is approximately 30 hours.
Etrasimod is extensively metabolized by multiple enzymes via oxidation,
dehydrogenation, glucuronidation, sulfation and a combination of these
reactions, though unchanged etrasimod is the main circulating component in
plasma. In vitro assays indicate that etrasimod is mainly metabolized via
CYP2C9, CYP2C8, and CYP3A4.
Primary excretion
Fecal excretion was the predominant route of excretion. See Mass balance
pathways (% dose)
results above.
Intrinsic Factors and Specific Populations
Body weight
In population PK analysis, body weight was a significant covariate of
etrasimod PK with lower etrasimod exposure associated with higher body
weight. The Ctrough,ss for subjects with body weights of 50 kg and 110 kg were
estimated to be 1.20 and 0.779 times, respectively, that of a 70 kg subject.
Age/sex/race/ethnicity
The PK of etrasimod would not be expected to be significantly affected by
age, sex, or race/ethnicity.
Renal impairment
Etrasimod Cmax and AUC were comparable between subjects with severe
renal impairment/ESRD and subjects with normal renal function.
Hepatic impairment
Etrasimod AUC parameters were 13%, 29%, and 57% higher in subjects with
mild, moderate, and severe hepatic impairment (Child-Pugh A, B, and C,
respectively), respectively, compared with subjects with normal liver function.
Drug Interaction Liability Effects of Etrasimod on Other Drugs
Inhibition/induction of
In vitro data suggested that the major human enzymes involved in the
metabolism
metabolism of etrasimod are CYP2C8, CYP2C9, and CYP3A4 and to a
lesser extent CYP2C19 and CYP2J2.
Etrasimod (10 μM) was not a direct or time-dependent inhibitor of CYP1A2,
CYP2B6, CYP2C9, CYP2C19, CYP2D6 or CYP3A4/5. Etrasimod is not an
inducer of human CYP1A2, CYP2B6, and CYP3A4 based on the fold-change
method as mRNA evaluations. Etrasimod was not an inhibitor of UGT1A3,
UGT1A4, UGT1A9, UGT2B7, or UGT2B17 in vitro.

transporter systems

No clinically meaningful effects of etrasimod on the steady-state Cmax and
AUC of oral contraceptive (30 μg ethinylestradiol and 150 μg levonorgestrel)
were observed when co-administered with etrasimod 2 mg QD.
In vitro studies suggest that etrasimod is not an inhibitor of human ABC
transporters (P-gp, BCRP, and BSEP) or SLC transporters (OATP1B1,
OATP1B3, OAT1/3, OCT1, OCT2, and MATE1/2 K).

Source: Data combined from NDA 216956 submission.
Abbreviations: SD = standard deviation; UC = ulcerative colitis; QD = once daily; PK = pharmacokinetics; AUCτ,ss = area under the
concentration time curve during one dosing interval at steady state; CL/F = apparent total body clearance of drug calculated after
extra-vascular administration; Cmax,ss = maximum observed drug concentration during a dosing interval at steady state; t1/2 = halflife of elimination phase; V/F = apparent total volume of distr bution

Based on PopPK analysis, disease status is only expected to have a minimal effect on etrasimod
PK exposure in subjects with UC. Healthy subjects have slightly smaller (within 10%) mean
etrasimod Cmax,ss and AUCss compared to UC patient of the same body weight, following 2 mg
QD dosing.

NDA/BLA Multi-Disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 216956
Velsipity (etrasimod)
Heart Rate Reduction
As a class, S1P1 agonists are known to affect heart rate especially following the first dose.
A dose-dependent reduction in heart rate (HR) by etrasimod was observed with the maximum
decline in HR from predose baseline observed from 2 to 4 hours postdose across clinical
studies. Of note, median Tmax is 4 hours. Maximal mean HR decrease from baseline ranged
from −3.3 to −19.4 bpm in healthy subjects following the first 2 mg dose of etrasimod. In
subjects with UC, maximal mean HR change from baseline ranged from −7.3 to −9.4 bpm
following the first 2 mg dose of etrasimod in phase 2 and 3 trials.
Of note, several different etrasimod dose titration regimens were evaluated in phase 1 study
APD334-110 and compared with dosing regimen without up-titration. Under dose titration
regimens, i.e., initiating with a low (0.25-0.5 mg) starting dose followed by up-titration to 2 mg
over a period of one week or less, as expected minimal HR-lowering effect was observed after
the first dose on Day 1. The maximal HR-lowering effect was delayed and observed at 2 mg at
the end of titration. Additionally, similar findings were also described using population PK/PD
HR modeling as shown in Figure 3. The model estimated that while the 2 mg QD with titration
regimen reduced the HR effect on the first day of treatment, the model predicted nadir,
reached after 5 days of treatment, was similar to that of the 2 mg QD without titration regimen
nadir reached on Day 1.
As such, etrasimod dosages of 1 mg QD and 2 mg QD without dose titration were used in phase
2 trials and subsequently 2 mg QD without dose titration was used in phase 3 trials in subjects
with UC.

Version date: October 12, 2018
Reference ID: 5259863

Clinical Pharmacology Questions
Does the Clinical Pharmacology Program Provide Supportive Evidence of Effectiveness?
The primary evidence of effectiveness of 2 mg etrasimod QD was supported by a significantly
higher proportion of adult subjects achieving clinical remission at Week 12 and Week 52
compared to placebo in the placebo-controlled phase 3 trials. See Section 8.1 of this multidisciplinary review for the related efficacy data.
For adolescent subjects aged 16 years and older, there were very limited data from the phase 3
trials for this age group. Overall, there was only one pediatric subject that received etrasimod 2
mg QD in phase 3 trials APD334-301 and APD334-302.
Is the Proposed Dosing Regimen Appropriate for the General Patient Population for Which
the Indication is Being Sought?
Yes. The proposed oral dosage of 2 mg QD for the general adult patient population is
appropriate.

Version date: October 12, 2018
Reference ID: 5259863

76

NDA/BLA Multi-Disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 216956
Velsipity (etrasimod)
Dose-Response Relationship
In a phase 2 dose-ranging study APD334-003, etrasimod at 1 mg and 2 mg QD, or placebo, were
studied in subjects with moderately to severely active UC for 12 weeks. Both doses showed
numerically better efficacy based on the primary efficacy endpoint of the study (i.e., change
from baseline in the modified Mayo score) compared to placebo at Week 12. Only the 2-mg
dose was statistically significantly different from placebo for the primary efficacy endpoint. In
addition, there was a dose-dependent increase in the proportion of subjects who achieved
endoscopic improvement, and greater decrease from baseline in rectal bleeding and stool
frequency subscores and the total Mayo score between 1 mg and 2 mg doses.
The effects of etrasimod on lymphocyte counts were also assessed in this phase 2 study.
Reduction of circulating lymphocyte counts was observed in both etrasimod dose groups at
Week 12. Numerically greater reductions in lymphocyte counts from baseline were observed
for the 2 mg dose compared to the 1 mg dose. The mean percent changes from baseline in
absolute lymphocyte count (ALC) versus time profiles are presented in Figure 5. It should be
noted that the clinical relevance of reduction in circulating lymphocyte counts with UC is not
fully understood.
Figure 5. Mean (SE) Percent Change from Baseline in Lymphocyte Count

Source: Phase 2 trial APD334-003 report, Figure 5.
Abbreviations: SE = standard error

Although the data described above are supportive of the proposed 2 mg dose for induction, no
dose exploration was performed for etrasimod for the treatment beyond week 12 in the phase
2 study and doses higher than 2 mg were not studied.

Version date: October 12, 2018
Reference ID: 5259863

77

NDA/BLA Multi-Disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 216956
Velsipity (etrasimod)
Applicant-proposed secondary endpoints (endoscopic improvement, symptomatic remission,
and mucosal healing)1 at Week 12. There appeared to be a trend toward greater efficacy
response with higher etrasimod exposure. Overall, exposure-response analysis estimated a
higher proportion of patients achieving improvements in efficacy response in the etrasimodtreated group as compared to placebo.
Of note, the overall incidence of treatment-emergent adverse events (TEAEs) appeared to be
higher in the etrasimod group (71.3%) compared to placebo (56.3%) in the phase 3 trial
APD334-301, although most TEAEs were Grade 1 or Grade 2 in severity. The incidence of severe
TEAEs, serious TEAEs, TEAEs leading to study drug discontinuation were similar between the
two treatment groups in the phase 3 trial APD334-301. Additionally, the overall incidence of
TEAEs were similar between the etrasimod group (47.1%) and placebo (46.6%) in phase 3 trial
APD334-302.
Overall, the proposed dosage of 2 mg QD is considered appropriate for the treatment of adult
patients with moderately to severely active UC based on the efficacy data in the phase 3 clinical
trials. See Section 8 of this multidisciplinary review for the related efficacy and safety data.
Is an Alternative Dosing Regimen or Management Strategy Required for Subpopulations
Based on Intrinsic Patient Factors?
Etrasimod is not recommended in patients with severe hepatic impairment. No dosage
adjustment is needed based on other intrinsic factors.
Hepatic Impairment
Based on a dedicated hepatic impairment study, after single dose administration of 2 mg, mean
Cmax of etrasimod was generally similar between subjects with hepatic impairment and matched
subjects with normal liver function; however, mean AUC0-inf were 13%, 29%, and 57% higher in
subjects with mild, moderate, and severe hepatic impairment, respectively, compared with
subjects with normal liver function. Mean T1/2 increased as hepatic function decreased, ranging
from 43.9 to 59.5 hours in normal matched controls and for 55.7 hours in subjects with mild
hepatic impairment to 76.5 hours for subjects with severe hepatic impairment.
The geometric mean AUC of M3 increased 3-fold and M6 increased 2-fold in the severe hepatic
impairment group compared to matched-control.
The summary of PK parameters for etrasimod is presented in Table 13.

There was an unexpected decreasing trend in %fu with decreasing liver function (i.e., %fu of
4.35%, 3.84%, and 2.59% for subjects with mild, moderate, and severe hepatic impairment,
respectively), while the mean %fu remained similar across the matched healthy control subjects
(%fu ranging 4.67% to 5.11%). The unbound AUC0-inf in subjects with severe hepatic impairment
(AUC0-inf,u of 70.8 ng·h/mL) appeared lower compared to that in matched subjects with normal
liver function (AUC0-inf,u of 81.1 ng·h/mL).The reasons for this slight decreasing trend in %fu is
unknown.
Overall, the differences in AUC0-inf for subjects with mild and moderate hepatic impairment
compared to healthy controls are not expected to be clinically meaningful to warrant dose
adjustment. However, etrasimod is not recommended in patients with severe hepatic
impairment, considering the extent of increase in AUC (57% higher) as well as lack of data from
phase 3 trials on subjects with severe hepatic impairment.
Renal Impairment
Based on a dedicated renal impairment study, renal impairment does not have clinically
significant effects on the PK of etrasimod. The mean Cmax and AUC of etrasimod were similar
between subjects with severe renal impairment/end-stage renal disease (ESRD) and subjects
with normal renal function as shown in Table 14. Of note, the effect of hemodialysis on the PK
of etrasimod has not been evaluated.

Patients ≥65 Years of Age
Based on population PK analysis, steady state PK estimates (Cmax,ss, AUCss, and Ctrough,ss) of
etrasimod in subjects over 65 years of age were similar (within 5%) to younger adult subjects.
Thus, dose reduction for UC patients aged 65 years and older is deemed not necessary based on
the available data.
Body Weight
Dose adjustment based on body weight is not needed for adult patients with UC.
In population PK analysis, covariate effects for body weight were included on both clearance
and volume terms. Model estimated the Cmax,ss, AUCss, and Ctrough,ss values for etrasimod in a
typical 50 kg subject are 125%, 123%, and 120%, respectively, of those from a reference 70 kg
subject. Model predicted Cmax,ss, AUCss, and Ctrough,ss values for etrasimod in a typical 110 kg
subject are 74.1%, 75.7%, and 77.9%, respectively, of those from a reference 70 kg subject.
Hence, the effect of body weight on etrasimod PK is not expected to be clinically meaningful to
warrant dose adjustment across the typical body weight range in adults. Of note, body weight
ranged 41.4 kg to 138 kg in subjects with UC who received 2 mg QD dosing in phase 3 trials
APD334-301 and APD334-302.
Pharmacogenetics
Etrasimod is extensively metabolized by multiple CYP enzymes in the liver. In vitro studies
demonstrated that etrasimod was predominantly metabolized by CYP2C8, CYP2C9, CYP3A4,
and with minor contribution via CYP2C19 and CYP2J2 (1% each).
The CYP2C9 gene is highly polymorphic. Individuals could be categorized into normal
metabolizers (NM), intermediate metabolizers (IM), and poor metabolizers (PM) based on
CYP2C9 genotypes (*1, *2, or *3). The Applicant studied metabolism by using an in vitro
recombinant CYP2C9 enzyme test. However, the Applicant did not submit a pharmacogenetic
report or data for CYP2C9 genotypes from in vivo studies in the original submission package.
The conversion of CYP2C9 genotype to phenotype relies on categorizing alleles into functional
status based on assigned “activity value” ranging from 0-1 (e.g., 0 for no function, 0.5 for
Version date: October 12, 2018
Reference ID: 5259863

81

NDA/BLA Multi-Disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 216956
Velsipity (etrasimod)
decreased function, and 1.0 for normal function), which are summarized into an “activity score
(AS)” for each diplotype (see Table 15). The CYP2C9 phenotype frequencies differ across
ancestrally diverse populations (see Table 16).

Clinical drug-drug interaction (DDI) studies with fluconazole (a moderate inhibitor of both
CYP2C9 and CYP3A4; study APD334-009) and gemfibrozil (a strong inhibitor of CYP2C8; study
APD334-009) increased the AUC of etrasimod by 84% and 36%, respectively. Itraconazole (a
strong CYP3A4 inhibitor; study APD334-116) increased the AUC of etrasimod by 32%.
Rifampin (a strong inducer of CYP3A4 and moderate inducer of CYP2C8 and CYP2C9; study
APD334-009) decreased the AUC of etrasimod by 49%. Based on the results obtained from the
DDI study with fluconazole (refer to additional study information below), a similar magnitude of
increase in etrasimod exposure is expected for CYP2C9 poor metabolizers with coadministration of moderate or strong inhibitors of CYP2C8 or CYP3A4.
As the results obtained from the DDI studies suggested a potential increase of exposure in
subjects with genetically decreased CYP2C9 enzyme activity, an Information Request (IR) was
sent to the Applicant (December 13, 2022) requesting available pharmacogenetic data for
CYP2C9. According to the Applicant’s response received on January 4, 2023, a CYP2C9
pharmacogenetic study was not conducted for the following reasons:

Version date: October 12, 2018
Reference ID: 5259863

82

NDA/BLA Multi-Disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 216956
Velsipity (etrasimod)
1. The clinical DDI data were consistent with the in vitro data, indicating involvement of three
CYP enzymes and suggests that a single enzyme doesn’t dominate the oxidative metabolism
of etrasimod.
2. Inter-subject variability across all the etrasimod PK studies was low to moderate (about 27%
for CL/F based on a population PK analysis) with no evidence of outlying clusters of subjects
with higher exposure.
3. The treatment emergent adverse events (TEAEs) were generally mild to moderate across
phase 1 and UC studies.
The review team agrees with the above rationales provided by the Applicant. However,
considering the expected increase in etrasimod exposure for IM or PM CYP2C9 metabolizers
with co-administration of CYP2C8 or CYP3A4 moderate or strong inhibitors, an additional IR was
sent on March 6, 2023, requesting recommendations for dosing approaches for patients who
are CYP2C9 IMs or PMs and receiving CYP2C8 or CYP3A4 moderate or strong inhibitors. The
Applicant’s response was received on March 20, 2023. The Applicant added the following
(b) (4)
language to the label,

The proposed dose recommendation was based on the justification outlined below:
1. The Applicant conducted a review of the published literature on the impact of CYP2C9
allelic variants on in vivo drug clearance. For different CYP2C9 substrates, decreases in
clearance in IMs relative to CYP2C9*1/*1 ranged from 3% (torsemide), 49% (celecoxib),
and ~68% (S-warfarin). In PMs, clearances are 9% to 33% or as high as ~91% (S-warfarin
in CYP2C9*3/*3 subjects), compared to the wild-type. The Applicant indicated that the
observed range of impact on clearance for CYP2C9 IM phenotype with concomitant
administration of an inhibitor of CYP3A4 or CYP2C8 is expected to result in <50%
decrease in etrasimod clearance (<2-fold increase in AUC), relative to wild-type.
Whereas in PMs, the decrease in clearance is unlikely to substantially exceed 50%, with
an increase in exposure that is 2-fold or slightly higher.
2. In the presence of fluconazole, while CYP2C9 and CYP3A4 pathways are moderately
inhibited, a significant extent of CYP2C8 activity is available to metabolize etrasimod.
Similarly, in IM or PM of CYP2C9 patients taking CYP3A4 or CYP2C8 inhibitor, the
remaining uninhibited metabolic pathways are expected to be fully active. Therefore,
the overall inhibition of metabolic activity of etrasimod in PM patients is not expected to
substantially exceed 50%, compared to wild type.
3. Based on the Applicant’s population PK model, post hoc steady state AUCss values were
generated for each subject receiving etrasimod 2 mg in the UC phase 3 trials. As shown
in Figure 7, for the etrasimod single dose, AUCinf in the presence of fluconazole (blue
box plot) is within the distribution of AUCss (red box plot) as determined in subjects with
Version date: October 12, 2018
Reference ID: 5259863

83

NDA/BLA Multi-Disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 216956
Velsipity (etrasimod)
UC from the phase 3 trials. According to the Applicant, this magnitude of increase in
etrasimod AUC is not expected to be a significant safety concern.
Figure 7. Etrasimod AUC for Etrasimod Co-Administered with Fluconazole Compared to Predicted
Exposure in UC Subjects Using a Population Pharmacokinetic Model

Source: The Applicant PopPK analysis.

The Applicant concluded that the increase in etrasimod exposure in patients with IM or PM
CYP2C9 status taking concomitant moderate or strong CYP3A4 or CYP2C8 inhibitors is expected
to be in a similar range as that observed in the fluconazole DDI study. Therefore, the
recommendation for PM patients should be consistent with recommendation for fluconazole.
Due to uncertainty in the range of potential changes in etrasimod exposure, the effect of
etrasimod exposure with concomitant use of CYP3A4 or CYP2C8 inhibitors is unknown in
CYP2C9 IM patients.
In general, the review team agrees with the Applicant’s justification. Pharmacogenomic labeling
recommendations will be included in the etrasimod label.
Are There Clinically Relevant Food-Drug or Drug-Drug Interactions, and What is the
Appropriate Management Strategy?
The proposed dosing instruction to administer etrasimod with or without food is appropriate.

Version date: October 12, 2018
Reference ID: 5259863

84

NDA/BLA Multi-Disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 216956
Velsipity (etrasimod)
Drug-Drug Interactions
•

Co-administration of etrasimod with drugs that are both moderate CYP2C9 and moderate or
strong CYP3A4 inhibitors (e.g., fluconazole) is not recommended.

•

Co-administration of etrasimod with a drug that is a combined CYP3A4 (strong), CYP2C8
(moderate) and CYP2C9 (moderate) inducer (e.g., rifampin) is not recommended.

•

Co-administration of etrasimod with drugs that are CYP2C8 or CYP3A4 inhibitor in patients
who are known to be CYP2C9 poor metabolizers is not recommended. In patients who are
intermediate metabolizers of CYP2C9 substrates, the effect on etrasimod exposure when
co-administered with CYP3A4 or CYP2C8 inhibitors is not known.

Food Effect
The effect of food on etrasimod PK was evaluated in study APD334-114 using the proposed
TBM formulation. No significant food effect on the PK of etrasimod was observed when a single
dose of 2 mg etrasimod was administered with a high-fat meal as compared to the fasted state.
Geometric mean Cmax and AUC were similar between the fed state and the fasted state (Table
17). Mean Tmax was delayed by 2 hours in the fed state (6 h) compared to the fasted state (4 h).

DDI Study with Fluconazole, Gemfibrozil, and Rifampin
A clinical drug interaction study (APD334-009) was conducted to evaluate the effects of
fluconazole, gemfibrozil, or rifampin on the PK of etrasimod in healthy adult subjects.
Co-administration of fluconazole 200 mg QD (400 mg on the first day, followed by 200 mg QD
for 14 days) with a single dose of etrasimod 1 mg resulted in an increase of 12% in mean Cmax
and an increase of 84% in mean AUC0-inf of etrasimod as shown in Table 18.

NDA/BLA Multi-Disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 216956
Velsipity (etrasimod)
In addition to the PK assessment, the effect of etrasimod on PD measures of ovarian activity
(i.e., follicle stimulating hormone (FSH), luteinizing hormone (LH), estradiol, and progesterone,
sex hormone binding globulin (SHBG), and transvaginal ultrasound (TVUS)/Hoogland scores)
were explored in this study. Graphical assessment suggested that co-administration of
etrasimod and OC did not show clinically meaningfully altered hormone concentration levels of
FSH, LH, estradiol, and progesterone compared to OC administration alone. A similar increasing
trend in SHBG levels was observed in both OC administration alone and co-administration of
etrasimod and OC treatment, which is expected for monophasic OC containing both EE and LVG
(Abdalla, Shabaan, and Stanczyk 1992). The incidents of Hoogland scores also showed similar
trends for both OC treatments.
Smoking Status
Population PK analysis estimated that steady state PK estimates (Cmax,ss, AUCss, and Ctrough,ss) of
etrasimod in a typical tobacco user were similar (within 2%) to those from a reference male
non-user of tobacco of the same body weight.
Immune System Effects After Stopping Etrasimod
As lymphocyte count is reported to return to the normal range in 90% of subjects within 4 to 5
weeks of stopping etrasimod in subjects with UC, use of immunosuppressants within this period
may lead to an additive effect on the immune system. Monitoring of patients receiving
concomitant immunosuppressants for infectious complications during treatment and for 5
weeks after the last dose of etrasimod will be recommended in the label.
Bridge Between the “To-Be-Marketed” and Clinical Trial Formulations
Since the formulation used in the phase 3 trials APD334-301 and APD334-302 is different from
the to-be-marketed formulation (2 mg oral tablets), a relative bioavailability study (APD334114) was conducted to bridge the clinical trial formulation to the to-be-marketed formulation.
Bioequivalence criteria were met for systemic exposures between these two formulations.
Geometric mean Cmax and AUC were comparable between the formulation used in the phase 3
trials and the to-be-marketed formulation as shown in Table 23.

Review Strategy
The clinical development program for etrasimod that supported the determination of safety
and effectiveness included one 12-week phase 2 trial (APD334-003), one 12-week phase 3 trial
(APD334-302), and one 52-week phase 3 trial (APD334-301).
Efficacy
The determination of effectiveness was based on the two phase 3 trials, APD334-301 and
APD334-302. No pooling of data across trials was conducted for the assessment of efficacy. The
two trials are summarized as follows:
•

APD334-301: A phase 3, multicenter, randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled trial to
evaluate the efficacy and safety of etrasimod 2 mg for the treatment of moderately to
severely active UC. The trial was a “treat-through” design for a duration of 52 weeks with an
initial treatment period of 12 weeks, at which time endpoints were assessed (i.e., Week 12).
Subjects then continued their blinded, randomized treatment for an additional 40 weeks, at
which time endpoints were assessed (i.e., Week 52). After Week 12, subjects who met the
prespecified definition of UC worsening 2 were eligible to leave APD334-301 and enter the
open-label extension study (APD334-303).

•

APD334-302: A phase 3, multicenter, randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled trial to
evaluate the efficacy and safety of etrasimod 2 mg for the treatment of moderately to
severely active UC. The trial duration was 12 weeks.

During the review, several issues were identified which resulted in the primary efficacy analyses
being conducted in a redefined population (“FDA Preferred Analysis Population”) as explained
below.
Disease Severity Criteria
APD334-301 and APD334-302 defined moderately to severely active UC as a modified Mayo
Score (mMS) of 4 to 9, including an endoscopic subscore (ES) of at least 2 and rectal bleeding
subscore (RBS) of at least 1. Prior to completion of the Applicant’s phase 3 program, the
Division communicated to the Applicant that the Division’s recommended definition of
moderately to severely active UC includes subjects with a mMS of 5 to 9 and an ES of at least 2.
The Division raised concerns that a mMS of 4 may result in the enrollment of subjects with mild
symptoms (e.g., a subject with a SFS of 1, RBS of 1, and ES of 2), in whom achieving the

Subjects with disease worsening were defined as having at least 1 of the following criteria: 1) absolute rectal
bleeding subscore (RBS) ≥ 2 on 2-consecutive days and confirmation of endoscopic subscore (ES) ≥ 2, 2) absolute
RBS + stool frequency subscore (SFS) ≥ 4 on 2-consecutive days and confirmation of ES ≥ 2 or 3) absolute RBS ≥ 2 or
RBS + SFS ≥ 4 (in any order) on 2-consecutive days and confirmation of ES ≥ 2.

2

Version date: October 12, 2018
Reference ID: 5259863

93

NDA/BLA Multi-Disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 216956
Velsipity (etrasimod)
definition of remission (which may include a SFS of 1) may not represent a clinically meaningful
improvement. The Applicant performed the requested additional analyses using the Division’s
recommended definition of moderately to severely active UC, and the results of analyses in this
population are the focus of this multidisciplinary review.
Pediatric Subjects
APD334-301 and APD334-302 enrolled subjects 16 to 80 years of age inclusive,

Safety
The primary safety analyses were performed on the 52-week trial APD334-301, and on pooled
safety data from the 12-week trials APD334-302 and APD334-003. The pooling of the data from
APD334-302 and APD334-003 for subjects that received the same dose of etrasimod or placebo
was appropriate as the study design, demographics, baseline disease characteristics, and prior
and concomitant therapy were similar between the two trials. Pooling of the 12-week trials
provided an assessment of adverse events in a larger safety database, as compared to
evaluating each study individually.
The safety of etrasimod was assessed in the safety population, which included all randomized
subjects who received at least one dose of study treatment. For trials APD334-301 and APD334302, the safety population is the same as the FAS because all randomized subjects received at
least one dose of study treatment.
Additionally, The Applicant submitted pooled safety data from the integrated summary of
safety (ISS). The ISS consisted of safety data from the placebo-controlled parent studies

Velsipity (etrasimod)
APD334-301, APD334-302, and APD334-003, as well as their open-label extension studies
APD334-303 (for APD334-301 and APD334-302) and APD334-005 (for APD334-003). The ISS also
included data from the open-label portion of an ongoing placebo-controlled trial conducted in
East Asia, ES101002. The open-label extension studies are described below.
•

Safety Information from Open-Label Studies
o ES101002: A phase 3, multicenter, randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled trial to
evaluate the efficacy and safety of etrasimod 2 mg for the treatment of moderately to
severely active UC in East Asia. This trial was ongoing at the time of the NDA submission;
however, data from the open-label portion of the trial were included. Subjects that
were nonresponders at Week 12 and those that experienced disease worsening during
ES101002 were eligible to enter the open-label treatment period with a treatment
duration of up to 40 weeks.
o APD334-005: An open-label extension study for subjects that completed APD334-003.
All subjects who completed APD334-003 and met the eligibility criteria for the APD334005 extension study had the option to enroll and receive open-label etrasimod 2 mg
once daily for 34 weeks, which was later extended to 40 weeks under Protocol
Amendment 2.
o APD334-303: An open-label extension study for subjects that completed APD334-301,
APD334-302, and APD334-308. Subjects from the ongoing studies APD334-210 (a 52week study in moderately active UC) and APD334-203 (a 12-week study in Japanese
subjects with moderately to severely active UC) were also eligible to enroll in APD334303. All subjects received open-label etrasimod 2 mg once daily for up to 5 years.

Given the open-label nature of the data from ES101002, APD334-005, and APD334-303, results
were not considered to provide substantial evidence of effectiveness. Additionally, APD334-308
was not submitted as part of the original NDA submission and consisted of summary safety
information; as such, data from APD334-308 was not considered to provide substantial
evidence of effectiveness. However, safety data were reviewed to assess for rare events given
the extended duration of the open-label studies.

8 Statistical and Clinical and Evaluation
Review of Relevant Individual Trials Used to Support Efficacy
APD334-301
Trial Design
APD334-301 was a multicenter, randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled trial to evaluate
the efficacy and safety of etrasimod 2 mg over 52 weeks. The trial population included subjects
with a modified Mayo Score (mMS) of 4 to 9 who had an inadequate response to, loss of
response to, or intolerance to at least 1 of the following: oral 5-amniosalicylic acid (5-ASA)
compounds, corticosteroids, thiopurines, biologic therapy (TNF blocker, vedolizumab, antiinterleukin [anti-IL] 12/23), or Janus kinase (JAK) inhibitors. A total of 433 subjects, including 1
adolescent, from 37 countries were randomized in a 2:1 ratio to receive either etrasimod 2 mg
once daily or matching placebo once daily for up to 52 weeks. The trial included an initial 12week treatment period, after which, subjects who met the predefined definition for UC
worsening2 were eligible to enter into the open-label extension trial (APD334-303). All other
subjects were to continue on their randomized treatment for an additional 40 weeks. Subjects
that met the predefined definition for UC worsening during this 40-week treatment period
could switch to the open-label extension trial. Randomization was stratified by prior treatment
status (i.e., naïve to biologic or JAK inhibitor therapy at trial entry [yes/no]), baseline
corticosteroid use (yes/no), and baseline disease activity (mMS: [4-6] or [7-9]). The overall trial
design for APD334-301 is shown in Figure 8.
Figure 8. Trial Design for APD334-301

Source: Applicant’s clinical study report for APD334-301, Figure 1 (page 22).
Abbreviation: JAK, Janus kinase; UC, ulcerative colitis

Velsipity (etrasimod)
Trial Endpoints
The co-primary efficacy endpoints comparing etrasimod to placebo consisted of:
•

The proportion of subjects achieving clinical remission at Week 12

•

The proportion of subjects achieving clinical remission at Week 52

Clinical remission was evaluated using the mMS and was defined as a stool frequency subscore
(SFS) of 0 or a SFS of 1 with a ≥1 point decrease from baseline, rectal bleeding subscore (RBS) of
0, and endoscopic subscore (ES) of 0 or 1 (excluding friability). The Applicant’s definition of
clinical remission differed from the Agency’s preferred definition of clinical remission of SFS of 0
or 1 (i.e., no requirement for a ≥1 point decrease from baseline), RBS of 0, and ES of 0 or 1
(excluding friability). The multiplicity-controlled secondary endpoints and definitions are shown
in Table 26 and were subject to overall type I error control (tested based on a gatekeeping
approach). Refer to Section 16.4.1 for additional details on the multiple testing procedure.
