Statistical Analysis Plan
Populations
The Applicant’s primary efficacy analyses were based on subjects in the full analysis set (FAS)
with baseline mMS of 5 to 9 including an ES of at least 2. The FAS consisted of all randomized
(b) (4)
subjects who received at least 1 dose of trial treatment.
The single adolescent subject was excluded from the efficacy analysis population,
resulting in the “FDA Preferred Analysis Population.” The Division’s efficacy analyses (in
addition to confirming the Applicant’s analyses) focus on efficacy in the FDA preferred analysis
population, defined as all adult subjects in the FAS with baseline mMS of 5 to 9 including an ES
of at least 2. The safety population included all randomized subjects (including adolescents and

Version date: October 12, 2018
Reference ID: 5259863

97

NDA/BLA Multi-Disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 216956
Velsipity (etrasimod)
subjects with mMS of 4) who received at least 1 dose of trial treatment, which is used for
safety-related analysis.
Sample Size
The trial was powered based on assumed clinical remission rates at Week 52 of 23.5% for
etrasimod versus 10% for placebo. A sample size of 420 (280 etrasimod, 140 placebo) was
expected to provide 93.4% power to demonstrate that etrasimod is superior to placebo in
achieving the clinical remission at Week 52, as assessed using a 2-group Fisher’s exact test with
a 1-sided significance level of 0.025. With this sample size, there was 96% power to detect a
difference of 12.5% in the other primary endpoint of clinical remission at Week 12, assuming a
placebo rate of 6%. The lower bound of overall power for both primary endpoints were at least
90%.
Calculation of SFS and RBS
The coprimary efficacy endpoints were based on the mMS which contains three subscore
components: SFS, RBS, and ES. On visits when the mMS was calculated, SFS and RBS were
derived by averaging the scores from the 3 most recent consecutive days within the 7 days
prior to the day of bowel preparation (excluding the day of bowel preparation) and rounding to
the nearest integer. On visits without endoscopy, SFS and RBS were derived by averaging the
scores from the 3 most recent consecutive days within the 7 days prior to the date of visit and
rounding to the nearest integer. If 3 consecutive days were not available, then the average of
the 2 most recent consecutive days within the 7 days would be used. Sensitivity analyses were
conducted that considered subjects with only 2 consecutive days available as non-responders.
The outcomes of these analyses were consistent with the primary efficacy results.
Analyses of Efficacy Endpoints
For assessments of the coprimary endpoints and multiplicity-controlled secondary endpoints,
the estimated difference in proportions between arms and the corresponding 95% confidence
interval (CI) were computed based on the stratified risk difference using the Cochran-MantelHaenszel (CMH) weights. The analyses adjusted for the following randomization stratification
factors: naïve to biologic or JAK inhibitor therapy at trial entry (yes/no), baseline corticosteroid
use (yes/no), and baseline disease activity (mMS: [4-6] or [7-9]).
Estimands and Intercurrent Events
The estimand corresponding to the co-primary efficacy endpoint of clinical remission at Week
12 evaluated the difference in the proportion of subjects in clinical remission at Week 12 for
etrasimod 2 mg versus placebo in subjects with baseline mMS of 5 to 9 in the FAS population.
For the intercurrent events of 1) premature treatment discontinuation for worsening of
disease, lack of efficacy, or adverse event related to UC, 2) initiation of a rescue medication for
UC, 3) an increase in dose over baseline levels in subjects’ existing UC medication, or 4) rescue
medical procedure during the trial, subjects were considered non-responders. The other coVersion date: October 12, 2018
Reference ID: 5259863

98

NDA/BLA Multi-Disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 216956
Velsipity (etrasimod)
primary endpoint at Week 52 and multiplicity-controlled secondary endpoints used the same
estimand as the co-primary endpoint at Week 12.
Handling of Missing Data
For analyses of the coprimary efficacy endpoints and multiplicity-controlled secondary
endpoints, missing data were imputed using non-responder imputation (NRI). Sensitivity
analyses for all multiplicity-controlled endpoints included multiple imputation under missing at
random (MAR), tipping point analysis, multiple imputation with Copy Reference (CR) under
missing not at random (MNAR), and a hybrid imputation approach with multiple imputation
used to handle missing endoscopy data due to the impact of the COVID-19 pandemic, and NRI
for missing data not due to the COVID-19 pandemic.
Multiplicity Adjustment
A gatekeeping multiple testing procedure was specified to control the overall Type I error rate
at 2-sided alpha of 0.05 for all coprimary endpoints and major secondary endpoints. Refer to
Figure 29 in the Appendix Section 16.4.1 for the details on the gatekeeping procedure.

The original study protocol for APD334-301 had four amendments. A summary of changes for
each amendment are described in Table 27.

Protocol Violations/Deviations
A total of 212 (52%) subjects had at least 1 important protocol deviation, including 145 (53%)
subjects in the etrasimod group and 67 (50%) subjects in the placebo group. The most
frequently reported (> 5% subjects) important protocol deviations by category were “Study
Procedures” (e.g., tests were not performed during the trial due to COVID-19, subjects did not
return IP bottle due to non-compliance), “Laboratory Assessment”, “Eligibility and Entry
Criteria” (e.g., tests were not performed during screening visit due to COVID-19, subjects were
randomized into the trial despite meeting exclusion criteria due to an oversight), Concomitant
Medication” (e.g., baseline corticosteroid dose was changed, corticosteroids were note tapered
after Week 12), and “Visit Schedule.” The most common protocol deviations in both cohorts
were “Study Procedures and Laboratory Assessment”, followed by “Eligibility and Entry
Criteria” in the etrasimod group, and “Concomitant medication” in the placebo group. The
number and distribution of protocol deviations were similar across both groups.

Treatment Compliance, Concomitant Medications, and Rescue Medication Use
Overall compliance was calculated as the total number of tablets taken divided by the total
number of tablets expected during the treatment period. Subjects who took less than 80% or
more than 120% of investigational drug during the entire treatment period were considered
non-compliant. Treatment compliance was 98% for the etrasimod and placebo groups,
respectively.
Subjects must have had inadequate response to, loss of response to, or intolerance to at least 1
prior approved therapy for UC, including oral 5-ASAs, corticosteroids, thiopurines, biologics,
and JAK inhibitors. Oral 5-ASA, AZA, 6-MP, oral corticosteroids, or medicinal probiotics were
permitted at screening and as per the inclusion criteria (Trial APD334-301 Protocol Section 4.1).
Concomitant treatments for UC were required to be stable prior to and during the trial and
Version date: October 12, 2018
Reference ID: 5259863

103

NDA/BLA Multi-Disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 216956
Velsipity (etrasimod)
could not be initiated during screening or the treatment periods. Immunosuppressive agents
such as oral AZA and 6-MP were to be discontinued ≥ 2 weeks prior to randomization.
The majority of subjects received at least 1 concomitant medication for UC during the trial (89%
in the etrasimod group and 88% in the placebo group), the majority of which were oral 5-ASAs.
Efficacy Results – Co-Primary Endpoints
A total of 433 subjects were in the FAS population, among which 409 subjects had baseline
mMS of 5 to 9, including an ES of at least 2. The Applicant’s pre-specified primary analysis was
performed on subjects in the FAS population with baseline mMS of 5 to 9, including an ES of at
(b) (4)
least 2, which included one adolescent subject.
The adolescent subject was excluded from the efficacy analysis population, resulting in a
total of 408 subjects (i.e., the “FDA Preferred Analysis Population”). Unless otherwise specified,
all efficacy results in this review are based on the FDA Preferred Analysis Population defined as
all adult subjects in the FAS with baseline mMS of 5 to 9, including an ES of at least 2.
The Applicant’s definition of clinical remission was different from the FDA preferred definition
of clinical remission. Refer to Section 8.1.1 for details. The review team recommended the FDA
preferred definition for assessment of the coprimary endpoint and for labeling, because the
definition corresponds to the Agency’s currently recommended definition of clinical remission
as described in the draft guidance for industry Ulcerative Colitis: Developing Drugs for
Treatment (April 2022). 3 Unless otherwise specified, the FDA preferred definition of clinical
remission is used in all applicable efficacy analyses.
On December 18, 2022, FDA sent an information request to the Applicant, asking the Applicant
to provide new analyses on all multiplicity-controlled endpoints based on adult subjects in the
FAS with baseline mMS of 5 to 9, including an ES of at least 2, and based on the FDA preferred
definition of clinical remission. On Jan 11, 2023, the updated results were submitted and
verified by the review team.
The primary efficacy results demonstrate superiority of etrasimod 2 mg compared to placebo in
achieving clinical remission at Week 12 and Week 52, as summarized in Table 31. The estimated
difference between etrasimod compared to placebo in the percentage of subjects achieving
clinical remission was 20.1% at Week 12 and 25.7% at Week 52, with p-values less than 0.0001
for both comparisons.
The percentage of subjects with missing data was 9.9% for clinical remission at Week 12 and
44.5% for clinical remission at Week 52 in the etrasimod 2 mg group. The percent missing was
11.2% for clinical remission at Week 12 and 73.1% for clinical remission at Week 52 in the

due to trial discontinuation, with the main reasons for discontinuation being adverse events or
withdrawal by subject. For clinical remission at Week 52, most of the missing data were due to
trial discontinuation with the main reason for discontinuation being disease worsening in both
arms. A higher dropout rate was observed in the placebo arm compared to the etrasimod 2 mg
arm. A non-responder imputation approach was used to handle missing data regardless of the
reason for dropout. As UC is a chronic condition and etrasimod is intended as a chronic
treatment, subjects who prematurely discontinued treatment would not experience a longterm benefit from the trial treatment. Therefore, non-responder imputation was considered
appropriate for handling missing data due to dropout.

Discussion of Primary and Secondary Endpoints Proposed for Labeling
Clinical Remission
The Applicant’s proposed definition of clinical remission differs from the FDA preferred
definition of clinical remission due to the requirement for subjects to demonstrate at least 1point decrease from baseline in the SFS. The efficacy results for clinical remission using the FDA
preferred definition and the Applicant’s definition were similar and both were statistically
significant. To align with the Division’s current thinking, the team concluded that the efficacy
endpoint of clinical remission based on the FDA preferred definition will be included in the label
to replace the Applicant’s prespecified coprimary endpoints of clinical remission (which

Version date: October 12, 2018
Reference ID: 5259863

107

NDA/BLA Multi-Disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 216956
Velsipity (etrasimod)
includes a requirement of at least 1-point decrease from baseline in the SFS).
Endoscopic Improvement
The Applicant’s proposed definition of endoscopic improvement aligns with the Division’s
recommended definition. Achievement of resolution of gross inflammation that can be
visualized on endoscopy is a clinically relevant outcome of interest, as patients who achieve
endoscopic improvement have been shown to have better outcomes than those with continued
active inflammation. Therefore, this endpoint will be included in labeling.
Symptomatic Remission
The Applicant defined symptomatic remission as a SFS of 0 (or 1 with at least 1-point decrease
from baseline), and RBS of 0 on the mMS. Therefore, symptomatic remission is the same as
clinical remission (based on the Applicant’s definition) without the endoscopy assessment.
There is no consensus on the definition of the term “symptomatic remission” and the concept is
redundant to the primary endpoint given that improvement in stool frequency and rectal
bleeding are incorporated in clinical remission. Therefore, the endpoint of symptomatic
remission will not be included in labeling. However, there was a trend toward more subjects
experiencing improvement in SFS at Week 2 and RBS at Week 4. This information is useful to
clinicians to help characterize the anticipated onset of improvement in symptoms. Therefore,
this information will be included descriptively in labeling. Refer to the Discussion of Additional
Endpoints below.
Histologic-Endoscopic Mucosal Improvement
The endpoint definition for “mucosal healing” represents an important clinical concept,
incorporating both improvement of the endoscopic appearance of the mucosa (ES ≤1 excluding
friability) and improvement in histologic abnormalities, including absence of any neutrophilic
inflammation (Geboes score <2.0). This combination represents an important clinical endpoint
and will be reflected in labeling. However, there is no standard definition for “mucosal healing”
and the definition varies depending on the source. There are also uncertainties regarding the
assessment of the histologic component of this endpoint, including the potential limitation of
obtaining biopsies from a single segment of the colon, as the degree of histologic inflammation
may vary by location of the biopsies. Although improvement in histologic findings may be
observed in the location from which the biopsy is taken, there is uncertainty as to whether
other locations in the colon have also improved. Therefore, the endpoint will be described in
labeling as histologic-endoscopic mucosal improvement, rather than “mucosal healing.”
The Applicant utilized a definition of ES of 0 or 1 (excluding friability) and a Geboes score of
<2.0, which represents no neutrophils in the epithelial crypts or lamina propria, no increase in
eosinophils, and no crypt destruction, erosions, ulcerations, or granulation tissue.
The Applicant proposed that the clinical relevance of this definition was supported by
literature. For example, the Applicant described that the improvement, and resolution, of
Version date: October 12, 2018
Reference ID: 5259863

108

NDA/BLA Multi-Disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 216956
Velsipity (etrasimod)
inflammation in UC, as measured by a histologic assessment and endoscopic measures
[respectively], compared to endoscopic measures alone, is associated with lower rates of
clinical relapse, corticosteroid use, hospitalization, and colectomy . Furthermore, the Applicant
stated that a “using very conservative standards (i.e., Geboes < 2.0, indicating neutrophil
absence in the epithelium and no increase detected in the lamina propria) is supported by
International Consensus and Position Papers .”
Although the review team agrees with this justification, there were some important limitations
to the histologic evaluation in this program. Thirty-three subjects (approximately 8%) entered
the trial with a Geboes score of <2.0 on the baseline biopsy, thus meeting the histologic
component of the endpoint definition. Therefore, it was unclear whether achieving histologicendoscopic mucosal improvement represents an improvement for subjects with a Geboes score
of <2.0 at baseline. The review team conducted a sensitivity analysis in which this endpoint was
reanalyzed only in the subgroup of subjects with a baseline Geboes score of ≥2.0. Results were
consistent with those obtained in the FDA preferred analysis population, and therefore the
team concluded that it is reasonable to include this endpoint in the label.
Additionally, although investigators were instructed to perform biopsies on the mucosa “most
representative of the inflammation seen in the region,” there may be variation in histologic
findings due to sampling. UC is generally considered to involve continuous segments of the GI
tract; however, discontinuous patterns of inflammation may also be observed, particularly in
subjects with a prior history of treatment (Bernstein et al. 1995). Furthermore, the clinical
significance of achieving “histologic improvement” (in addition to endoscopic improvement)
has not been well characterized and is limited by the various definitions used in the literature to
define this concept (Battat et al. 2019). Due to these limitation, the label will indicate that the
relationship between any histologic-endoscopic mucosal improvement endpoint and disease
progression and longer-term outcomes beyond 52 weeks has not been assessed.
Discussion of Additional Endpoints
Clinical Response
Clinical response at Week 12 is a clinically meaningful endpoint. Although clinical response at
Week 12 was not multiplicity-controlled, the etrasimod group had a numerically higher
proportion of clinical responders in comparison with those treated with placebo, with an
observed p-value less than 0.001. Clinical response at Week 52 is less clinically important than
clinical response at Week 12 as subjects were expected to achieve clinical remission at Week
52, which has a more stringent definition. Therefore, language describing only the results of
clinical response at Week 12 will be included in labeling.
Stool Frequency and Rectal Bleeding Subscores
Individual analyses of the SFS and RBS of the mMS in APD334-301 showed evidence that
subjects treated with etrasimod reported symptomatic relief after only a few weeks of dosing
(Figure 9 and Figure 10). Subjects in the etrasimod treatment group showed a nominally
Version date: October 12, 2018
Reference ID: 5259863

109

NDA/BLA Multi-Disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 216956
Velsipity (etrasimod)
significant and greater mean reduction in SFS as early as Week 2 (nominal p-value = 0.0258) and
in RBS as early as Week 4 (nominal p-value = 0.0007) compared to the placebo group based on
the estimates from a mixed-effect model with repeated measures (MMRM). The improvement
from baseline in SFS and RBS was sustained in the etrasimod group starting from Week 20.

Endoscopic Remission
The Applicant’s definition of endoscopic remission is consistent with the Division’s preferred
definition of endoscopic remission. Although the endoscopic remission endpoint was not
multiplicity-controlled, this endpoint demonstrated a nominally significant effect and
represents a clinically relevant endpoint that is informative to prescribers as part of their
clinical practice. Therefore, the results of endoscopic remission (at Week 12 and both Weeks 12
and 52) will be included descriptively in labeling.
Endoscopic Remission and Geboes Histologic Score < 2.0
This endpoint is similar to histologic-endoscopic mucosal improvement but included a more
stringent definition of resolution of endoscopic findings, characterized by normal appearing
mucosa (ES = 0). However, this endpoint was not pre-specified in the protocol or in the SAP.
Therefore, this endpoint will not be included in labeling.

111

NDA/BLA Multi-Disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 216956
Velsipity (etrasimod)
Efficacy Results – Secondary or Exploratory COA (PRO) Endpoints
Bowel Urgency and Abdominal Pain
Bowel urgency is a common symptom of UC and is associated with reduced quality of life
(Dubinsky et al. 2022). The Applicant assessed bowel urgency severity with the Urgency
Numeric Rating Scale (NRS) patient-reported outcome (PRO) measure, a single-item PRO
measure assessing severity of a subject’s urgency (the sudden or immediate need) to have a
bowel movement within the past 24 hours using an 11-point NRS ranging from 0 (“No urgency”)
to 10 (“Worst possible urgency”).
Abdominal pain is another common symptom of UC that is an important treatment objective
for patients. The Applicant assessed abdominal pain with the Abdominal Pain NRS PRO
measure, a single item PRO measure assessing severity of a subject’s abdominal pain within the
past 24 hours using an 11-point NRS ranging from 0 (“No Pain”) to 10 (“Pain as Bad as Can
Imagine”).
The Urgency NRS and Abdominal Pain NRS were collected at baseline, Week 12, and Week 52.
An ad hoc analysis of the proportion of subjects with “absence of urgency” defined as an
Urgency NRS score of 0 and “absence of abdominal pain” defined as an Abdominal Pain NRS
score of 0 was performed at Week 12 and Week 52. Of note, no data were collected between
these timepoints.
A greater proportion of subjects treated with etrasimod 2 mg reported an Urgency NRS of 0
than placebo at Week 12 (19.3% vs 7.4%) and Week 52 (18.6% vs 8.1%). A greater proportion of
subjects treated with etrasimod 2 mg reported an Abdominal Pain NRS of 0 than placebo at
Week 12 (26.6% vs 13.3%) and Week 52 (21.5% vs 7.4%).
Although bowel urgency and abdominal pain are common and important symptoms of UC, the
results of the Urgency NRS and Abdominal Pain NRS are not recommended for inclusion in
labeling due to the ad hoc nature of the analyses (i.e., not multiplicity controlled and performed
after unblinding). Additionally, given the recall period of “the past 24 hours” for the bowel
urgency and abdominal pain assessments, data collected at baseline, Week 12, and Week 52
would only be representative of patients’ experiences in the past 24 hours at each respective
assessment time point. Therefore, the results are difficult to interpret as UC is a chronic disease
that can relapse and remit.
Inflammatory Bowel Disease Questionnaire
The inflammatory bowel disease questionnaire (IBDQ) is an instrument used to assess healthrelated quality of life. The Division had previously determined that IBDQ is not appropriate to
support labeling claims. The instrument combines many concepts and impacts, and some of the
questions are double-barreled or otherwise unclear. Therefore, the results of the IBDQ are not
recommended for inclusion in labeling.

Version date: October 12, 2018
Reference ID: 5259863

112

NDA/BLA Multi-Disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 216956
Velsipity (etrasimod)
Additional Analyses Conducted on the Individual Trial
Efficacy results were investigated by prior biologic or JAK inhibitor exposure status for
multiplicity-controlled endpoints in APD334-301, as summarized in Table 34. Although the trial
was not powered to detect a significant difference for each subgroup, the treatment
differences were consistent with the primary analysis. The etrasimod group consistently
showed higher remission and response rates compared to the placebo group across all
subgroups. In general, the observed remission and response rates were higher in the subgroup
with no prior biologic/JAK inhibitor exposure versus the subgroup with prior biologic/JAK
inhibitor exposure for both etrasimod and placebo, which was expected because patients with
prior biologic/JAK inhibitor exposure are typically more refractory to treatment. The subgroup
with no prior exposure to biologics/JAK inhibitors generally had a larger treatment difference
between etrasimod and placebo, though the estimated treatment differences for endoscopic
improvement and mucosal healing endpoints were similar across subgroups.

The subgroup analyses for efficacy conducted by use of corticosteroids at baseline for the coprimary endpoints were presented in Table 35. The etrasimod group showed higher remission

APD334-302
Trial Design
Trial APD334-302 was a multicenter, randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled trial to
evaluate the efficacy and safety of etrasimod 2 mg, compared with placebo, over a 12-week
induction period. The trial population included subjects with a modified Mayo Score (mMS) of 4
to 9 who had an inadequate response to, loss of response to, or intolerance to at least 1 of the
following: oral 5-amniosalicylic acid (5-ASA) compounds, corticosteroids, thiopurines, biologic
therapy (TNF blocker, vedolizumab, anti-IL 12/23), or Janus kinase (JAK) inhibitors. A total of
354 subjects, including 2 adolescent subjects, from 39 countries were randomized in a 2:1 ratio
to receive either etrasimod 2 mg once daily or matching placebo once daily for up to 12 weeks.
Randomization was stratified by prior treatment status (i.e., naïve to biologic or JAK inhibitor
therapy at trial entry [yes/no]), baseline corticosteroid use (yes/no), and baseline disease
activity (mMS: [4-6] or [7-9]). The overall trial design for APD334-302 is shown in Figure 11.

Version date: October 12, 2018
Reference ID: 5259863

NDA/BLA Multi-Disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 216956

Trial Endpoints
The primary efficacy endpoint was the proportion of subjects achieving clinical remission at
Week 12. The Applicant’s definition was the same as APD334-301; refer to Section 8.1.1 for
additional details. The multiplicity-controlled secondary efficacy endpoints are shown in Table
36 and were subject to overall type I error control (tested based on a prespecified gatekeeping
approach). Refer to Section 16.4.1 for additional details on the multiple testing procedure.
Table 36. Multiplicity-Controlled Secondary Efficacy Endpoints for Study APD334-302
Secondary Endpoint
Definition
Endoscopic improvement at Week 12
ES ≤ 1 (excluding friability)
Symptomatic remission at Week 12
SFS = 0 (or = 1 with a ≥ 1 point decrease from baseline)
and RBS of 0
Mucosal healing at Week 12
ES of ≤ 1 (excluding friability) with histologic remission
measured by a Geboes Index score < 2.0
Source: Review team’s table
Abbreviation: ES, endoscopic subscore; SFS, stool frequency subscore; RBS, rectal bleeding subscore

Statistical Analysis Plan
Populations
The Applicant’s primary efficacy analyses were based on subjects in the FAS with baseline mMS
of 5 to 9. The FAS consisted of all randomized subjects who received at least 1 dose of trial
(b) (4)
treatment.
The two adolescent subjects were excluded
from the efficacy analysis population, resulting in the “FDA Preferred Analysis Population.” The
safety population included all randomized subjects (including adolescents and subjects with
mMS of 4) who received at least 1 dose of trial treatment, which is used for safety-related
analysis.

Version date: October 12, 2018
Reference ID: 5259863

117

NDA/BLA Multi-Disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 216956
Velsipity (etrasimod)
Sample Size
Based on a 2-sided Fisher’s exact test with a 1-sided significance level of 0.025, and a 2:1
randomization ratio, a sample size of 330 subjects (220 etrasimod, 110 placebo) was expected
to provide at least 90% power to detect a difference of 12.5% in the primary endpoint of clinical
remission between the etrasimod treatment group and the placebo treatment group, assuming
a 6.0% remission rate for the placebo group.
Calculation of SFS and RBS
Similar to APD334-301, the primary efficacy endpoint for APD334-302 was evaluated using the
mMS, which contains three components: SFS, RBS, and ES. For the SFS and RBS, the average of
the most recent 3 consecutive days within the 7 days prior to the day of bowel preparation
(excluding the day of bowel preparation) was rounded to the nearest integer to calculate the
weekly score for each subject. If 3 consecutive days were not available, then the average of the
2 most recent consecutive days within the 7-day period was used. Sensitivity analyses were
conducted that considered subjects with only two consecutive days as non-responders. The
outcomes of these analyses were consistent with the primary efficacy results.
Analyses of Efficacy Endpoints
For assessments of the primary endpoint and multiplicity-controlled secondary endpoints, the
estimated difference in proportions between arms and the corresponding 95% confidence
interval were computed based on the stratified risk difference using the Cochran-MantelHaenszel (CMH) weights. The analyses adjusted for the following randomization stratification
factors: naïve to biologic or JAK inhibitor therapy at trial entry (yes/no), baseline corticosteroid
use (yes/no), and baseline disease activity (mMS: [4-6] or [7-9]).
Estimands and Intercurrent Events
Similar to APD334-301, the estimand corresponding to the primary efficacy endpoint of clinical
remission at Week 12 evaluated the difference in the proportion of subjects in clinical remission
at Week 12 for etrasimod 2 mg versus placebo in subjects with baseline mMS of 5 to 9 in the
FAS population. For the intercurrent events of 1) premature treatment discontinuation for
worsening of disease, lack of efficacy, or adverse event related to UC, 2) initiation of a rescue
medication for UC, 3) an increase in dose over baseline levels in subjects’ existing UC
medication, or 4) rescue medical procedure during the trial, subjects were considered nonresponders.
Handling of Missing Data
For analyses of the efficacy endpoint and multiplicity-controlled secondary endpoints, missing
data were imputed using non-responder imputation (NRI). Sensitivity analyses for all
multiplicity-controlled endpoints included multiple imputation under missing at random (MAR),
tipping point analysis, multiple imputation with Copy Reference (CR) under missing not at

Version date: October 12, 2018
Reference ID: 5259863

118

NDA/BLA Multi-Disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 216956
Velsipity (etrasimod)
random (MNAR), and a hybrid imputation with multiple imputation used to handle missing
endoscopy data due to the impact of the COVID-19 pandemic and NRI used for missing data not
due to the COVID-19 pandemic.
Multiplicity Adjustment
A gatekeeping multiple testing procedure was specified to control the overall Type I error rate
at 2-sided alpha of 0.05, for all co-primary endpoints and major secondary endpoints. Refer to
Figure 30 in Section 16.4.1 for the details of the gatekeeping procedure.

The original study protocol for APD334-302 had three amendments. A summary of changes for
each amendment are described in Table 37.

Trial Results (APD334-302)
Patient Disposition
The “FDA Preferred Analysis Population” consisted of a total of 333 subjects, 221 in the
etrasimod 2 mg group and 112 in the placebo group, 89% (N=197) of whom completed the trial
in the etrasimod group and 88% (N=99) in the placebo group. The disposition of subjects in the
“FDA Preferred Analysis Population” was similar to the FAS.
The most frequently reported reason for trial discontinuation in the etrasimod group was due
to an adverse event. In the placebo group, the most frequently reported reason for trial
discontinuation was withdrawal by subject. Table 38 summarizes subject disposition by
treatment group.

Protocol Violations/Deviations
A total of 71 (21%) subjects had at least 1 important protocol deviation, including 50 (23%)
subjects in the etrasimod group and 21 (19%) subjects in the placebo group. The most
frequently reported (> 5% subjects) important protocol deviations by category were “Study
Procedures” (e.g., tests or assessments were not performed during the trial), “Laboratory
Assessment”, “Eligibility and Entry Criteria” (e.g., subjects were randomized into the trial
despite meeting exclusion criteria due to an oversight.), and “Visit Schedule.” The most
common protocol deviations in both cohorts were “Study Procedures.” The number and
distribution of protocol deviations were similar across both groups.
Table of Demographic Characteristics
Baseline demographic characteristics from the FDA preferred analysis population are presented
in Table 39. Overall, the baseline demographics were similar to APD334-301. The majority of
patients were male (57% and 63% in etrasimod and placebo, respectively) and most were

15kg/m2 to 39kg/m2 in the placebo group. Demographic characteristics were generally similar
among treatment groups.
Similar to APD334-301, the majority of subjects were enrolled outside the U.S. However, as
there are no well-established differences in UC disease severity or response to treatment by
different ethnic groups, and the disease activity scores and documentation of prior treatment
failure were standardized by the protocol, it is reasonable to generalize the result to the U.S.
population.

Velsipity (etrasimod)
Treatment Compliance, Concomitant Medications, and Rescue Medication Use
Overall compliance was calculated as the total number of tablets taken divided by the total
number of tablets expected during the treatment period. Subjects who took less than 80% or
more than 120% of investigational drug during the entire treatment period were considered
non-compliant. Treatment compliance was 99.6% for the etrasimod and 98.3% for the placebo
group, respectively.
Subjects must have had inadequate response to, loss of response to, or intolerance to at least 1
prior approved therapy for UC, including oral ASAs, corticosteroids, thiopurines, biologics, and
JAK inhibitors. Oral 5-ASA, AZA, 6-MP, oral corticosteroids, or medicinal probiotics were
permitted at screening and as per the inclusion criteria (Trial APD334-302 Protocol Section 4.1).
Concomitant treatments for UC were required to be stable prior to and during the trial and
could not be initiated during screening or the treatment periods. Immunosuppressive agents
such as oral AZA and 6-MP were to be discontinued ≥ 2 weeks prior to randomization.
The majority of subjects received at least 1 concomitant medication for UC during the trial (93%
in the etrasimod group and 89% in the placebo group), the majority of which were oral 5-ASAs.
Efficacy Results – Primary Endpoint
As discussed in Section 8.1.2, unless otherwise specified, all efficacy results in this review are
based on the FDA Preferred Analysis Population defined as all adult subjects in the FAS with
baseline mMS of 5 to 9. The FDA preferred definition of clinical remission was used in the
analyses of clinical remission related endpoints.
The Applicant’s efficacy results were confirmed by the review team. As shown in Table 41, the
etrasimod treatment group had a statistically higher proportion of subjects achieving clinical
remission at Week 12 (26.2%) in comparison with those treated with placebo (15.2%), p-value =
0.010.

Of note, the treatment difference observed for APD334-302 for clinical remission at Week 12
was smaller than the treatment difference observed for APD334-301 for clinical remission at

Version date: October 12, 2018

NDA/BLA Multi-Disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 216956
Velsipity (etrasimod)
the placebo group (15.2%) in APD334-302 versus the placebo group (7.5%) in APD334-301.
However, baseline demographic and disease characteristics were similar between APD334-301
and APD334-302. In response to an information request by the review team, the Applicant
analyzed potentially contributing factors, but did not identify any covariates that fully explained
the difference observed between the clinical remission rates of the two trials. The difference in
placebo response was thus attributed to the intrinsic variability of the placebo response rate,
which has been described to range from 5% to 25% in the literature (Jairath et al. 2017).
Twenty-two subjects (10%) in the etrasimod group and 11 subjects (9.8%) in the placebo group
had missing values. Most missing data was due to trial dropout with main reason due to
adverse events for the etrasimod group and withdrawal by subject for the placebo group.
Similar to APD334-301, non-responder imputation was applied to handle missing data. The
results from the pre-specified missing data sensitivity analyses (described in Section 8.1.3)
supported the conclusions of the primary analysis.

Discussion of Primary and Secondary Endpoints Proposed for Labeling
Symptomatic Remission
As previously discussed in this document, symptomatic remission is not considered appropriate
for inclusion in labeling because symptomatic remission is redundant to clinical remission.
However, as discussed in Section 8.1.2 for APD334-301, information related to improvements in
SFS, and RBS is useful to clinicians to help characterize the anticipated onset of improvement in
symptoms. Refer to the Discussion of Additional Endpoints below.
The consideration for other multiplicity-controlled endpoints (i.e., endoscopic improvement
and histologic-endoscopic mucosal improvement) is similar to those described for APD334-301.
Refer to Section 8.1.2.
Discussion of Additional Endpoints
Stool Frequency and Rectal Bleeding Subscores
Individual analyses of the SFS and RBS of the mMS in APD334-302 provided evidence that
subjects treated with etrasimod reported symptomatic relief within a few weeks of treatment
(Figure 12 and Figure 13). In comparison to the placebo group, subjects in the etrasimod
treatment group exhibited a nominally significantly greater mean reduction in SFS (nominal pvalue = 0.0128) and RBS (nominal p-value = 0.0107) as early as Week 2 based on estimates
derived from an MMRM.

For APD334-302, there was a trend toward more subjects experiencing improvement in SFS and
RBS at Week 2. Given that it was not until Week 4 that a significant difference between
etrasimod and placebo for the RBS was observed in APD334-301, the review team
recommended aligning the timepoint between APD334-301 and APD334-302 for the RBS due to
the importance of replication to ensure observed results are not due to change. Therefore,
decreases RBS being observed as early as Week 4 will be descriptively included in labeling.
The considerations for other endpoints that are not controlled for multiplicity (i.e., clinical
response, endoscopic remission, and endoscopic remission and Geboes histologic score <2.0)
are similar to those outlined for APD334-301. Refer to Section 8.1.2 for discussion regarding the
endpoints proposed for labeling.
Efficacy Results – Secondary or Exploratory COA (PRO) Endpoints
Refer to Section 8.1.2. for discussion regarding the Urgency NRS and Abdominal Pain NRS.
Similar to APD334-301, the Urgency NRS and Abdominal Pain NRS were collected at baseline
and Week 12. An ad hoc analysis of the proportion of subjects with “absence of urgency”
defined as an Urgency NRS score of 0 and “absence of abdominal pain” defined as an

Version date: October 12, 2018
Reference ID: 5259863

127

NDA/BLA Multi-Disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 216956
Velsipity (etrasimod)
Abdominal Pain NRS score of 0 was performed at Week 12. Of note, no data were collected
between these timepoints.
A greater proportion of subjects treated with etrasimod 2 mg reported an Urgency NRS of 0
than placebo at Week 12 (21.2% vs 11.6%). A greater proportion of subjects treated with
etrasimod 2 mg reported an Abdominal Pain NRS of 0 than placebo at Week 12 (31.5% vs
17.9%).
Similar to APD334-301, although bowel urgency and abdominal pain are common and
important symptoms of UC, the results of the Urgency NRS and Abdominal Pain NRS are not
recommended for inclusion in labeling due to the ad hoc nature of the analyses (i.e., not
multiplicity controlled and performed after unblinding). Refer to Section 8.1.2 for further
discussion of these endpoints.
Additional Analyses Conducted on the Individual Trial
The efficacy results for multiplicity-controlled endpoints in APD334-302 were analyzed based
on prior biologic or JAK inhibitor exposure status, as presented in Table 44. While the trial was
not designed to detect a significant difference for each subgroup, the treatment differences
observed were in line with the primary analysis. Across all subgroups, the etrasimod groups
consistently demonstrated numerically higher rates of remission and response compared to the
placebo. The subgroup of subjects without prior exposure to biologic or JAK inhibitors exhibited
numerically higher remission and response rates than those with prior exposure.

Assessment of Efficacy Across Trials
Subpopulations
Subgroup analyses were conducted to assess the potential for differences in the treatment
effect at Week 12 and at Week 52 for various demographic characteristics. Overall, the
etrasimod group consistently showed higher clinical remission rates compared to placebo
across all subgroups, as detailed in Table 46, Table 47, and Table 48. In APD334-301, female
subjects within the etrasimod group had a clinical remission rate approximately 17% higher at
Week 12 compared to the male subjects; however, the difference was not evident in APD334302. In the placebo group of APD334-302, female subjects had around 10% clinical remission
rate higher at Week 12 compared to male subjects; however, the discrepancy was not evident
in APD334-301. Such variations might be attributed to the constrained sample sizes of the
subgroups.

Conclusions on Effectiveness
Overall, the submission contains substantial evidence of effectiveness for etrasimod 2 mg daily
based on data from two adequate and well-controlled investigations. The results of the 52week trial (APD334-301) were statistically and clinically significant compared to placebo on the
primary and multiple major secondary endpoints. Similarly, the results for the 12-week trial
(APD334-302) were statistically and clinically significant compared to placebo on the primary
Version date: October 12, 2018
Reference ID: 5259863

132

NDA/BLA Multi-Disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 216956
Velsipity (etrasimod)
and multiple major secondary endpoints. The results of both trials were robust to subgroup
analyses, including for subjects with prior biologic or JAKi failure, baseline corticosteroid use,
and mMS greater than 5, supporting that clinical benefit was observed among the trial
population. The submitted evidence supports the conclusion that etrasimod is effective in the
treatment of moderately to severely active UC in adult patients.

Review of Safety
Safety Review Approach
The safety evaluation of etrasimod for the treatment of moderately to severely active UC
focused on data from the randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled trials APD334-301,
APD334-302, and APD334-003, and is supplemented by additional data from an integrated
summary of safety (ISS). The ISS was a pool of controlled data from studies in subjects with UC
including APD334-301, APD334-302, and APD334-003 as well as uncontrolled data from the
open-label extension studies APD334-303, APD334-005, and ES101002.
APD334-301 was a phase 3, multicenter, randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled trial to
evaluate the efficacy and safety of etrasimod 2 mg for the treatment of moderately to severely
active UC. Eligible subjects were randomized in a 2:1 ratio to receive either etrasimod 2 mg
once daily or matching placebo once daily for up to 52 weeks. After Week 12, subjects who met
the prespecified definition of UC worsening were eligible to enter the open-label extension
study (APD334-303).
Safety data from APD334-302 and APD334-003 were pooled (“12-Week Safety Pool”) because
the two study designs and populations were sufficiently similar, to better assess the adverse
event profile for the 12-week trials. APD334-302 was a phase 3, multicenter, randomized,
double-blind, placebo-controlled trial to evaluate the efficacy and safety of etrasimod 2 mg for
the treatment of moderately to severely active UC. Eligible subjects were randomized in a 2:1
ratio to receive either etrasimod 2 mg once daily or matching placebo once daily for up to 12
weeks. APD334-003 was a phase 2, multicenter, randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled
trial to evaluate the efficacy and safety of etrasimod 2 mg and 1 mg for the treatment of
moderately to severely active UC. Eligible subjects were randomized in a 1:1:1 ratio to receive
either etrasimod 2 mg once daily, etrasimod 1 mg once daily, or matching placebo for up to 12
weeks. The 1 mg etrasimod arm of APD334-003 was not pooled because this dose was not
evaluated in APD334-301 or APD334-302. Due to the differences in randomization ratios,
pooled analysis results were adjusted for the study size.
The ISS consisted of controlled and uncontrolled data from studies in subjects with UC, as
described previously. Since much of the data in the ISS was uncontrolled, the ISS was not
considered to provide substantial evidence of effectiveness or safety. However, safety data
were reviewed to assess for rare events given the extended duration of treatment for the openlabel studies.

Version date: October 12, 2018
Reference ID: 5259863

133

NDA/BLA Multi-Disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 216956
Velsipity (etrasimod)
Clinical trial data were analyzed using JMP and JMP Clinical software. All safety assessments
and conclusions are those of the clinical review team unless otherwise specified. The clinical
review team recoded several adverse event terms using logical groupings in order to better
ascertain the safety profile of etrasimod; refer to Table 107 in Section 16.5 of the Appendix. The
clinical review team did not identify any major data quality or integrity issues that precluded
performing a thorough safety review.
Review of the Safety Database
Overall Exposure
The overall safety database that was the focus of the safety review consisted of 891 subjects as
described in Table 49. A total of 577 subjects received etrasimod 2 mg. A total of 3 adolescent
subjects were enrolled in the overall safety database. Data from these adolescent subjects were
included in the safety analyses; however, due to the limited data, the efficacy analyses were
focused on adult subjects, and it was not recommended to include adolescents in the proposed
indication.

Subjects contributed exposure from APD334-301
Abbreviations: N, number of subjects in treatment arm

Adequacy of the Safety Database
A significant dropout rate was observed during the 52-week treat-through trial APD334-301.
The most common reason for study discontinuation was disease worsening, which was
predefined in the protocol to ensure subjects who were not receiving benefit could leave the
trial to receive additional treatment. Refer to Section 8.1.2 for details regarding subject
disposition. Additional safety data from open label studies (ES101002, APD334-005, and
APD334-303) and the Japanese study APD334-308 provided supportive safety information. This
supportive safety information included longer-term data from subjects that completed 12
weeks of treatment in APD334-302 and APD334-003 and data from additional subjects enrolled
in ES101002 and APD334-308. Refer to Section 7.2 for additional details.
Given the duration of exposures in the trials, the safety database is deemed sufficient to
characterize the safety of etrasimod in adults with moderately to severely active UC when used
through 52 weeks. It is acknowledged that a pre-market database in UC contains limited data to
fully assess the potential for rare events, particularly those of long latency (e.g., malignancy).
Nevertheless, the size and scope of the submitted safety database is acceptable to inform a
risk-benefit assessment for the proposed indication of the treatment of moderately to severely
active UC.
Adequacy of Applicant’s Clinical Safety Assessments
Issues Regarding Data Integrity and Submission Quality
No major issues regarding data integrity or submission quality were identified.
Categorization of Adverse Events
A treatment emergent adverse event (TEAE) was defined as any adverse event that started or
worsened in severity on or after the first dose of study treatment. Terms are coded using
MedDRA version 24.1. Serious adverse events (SAEs) were appropriately defined consistent
with 21 CFR 312.32(a) and reported as required. The baseline period was the screening period.
All AEs, including SAEs, were monitored until the subject discontinued and/or completed the
study. The adverse event reporting period for safety surveillance began when the subject was
initially included in the study (date of first signature of informed consent) and continued up to
Version date: October 12, 2018
Reference ID: 5259863

135

NDA/BLA Multi-Disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 216956
Velsipity (etrasimod)
30 days after the last administration of study treatment. If an adverse event was not resolved
or stabilized by that time, the Applicant in consultation with the Investigator decided whether
to continue to monitor the adverse event or closeout the event in the database if no further
follow-up was necessary. Any SAE suspected to be related to the study treatment was reported
whenever it occurred, irrespective of the time elapsed since the last administration of study
treatment. Any SAE that was ongoing when the subject completed the study or discontinued
the study was followed by the Investigator until the event resolved, stabilized, or returned to
baseline status.
All AEs were graded as mild, moderate, or severe as per the Investigator’s judgement. The
causal relationship between the investigational drug and the AE was characterized by the
Investigator as related or not related, according to definitions outlined in the study protocol.
Adverse events of special interest (AESIs) were specified as being of special interest based on
prior experience with other agents acting via a similar mechanism; these included bradycardia,
heart conduction abnormalities (2nd degree and higher AV block), elevated blood pressure,
macular edema, serious or opportunistic infection, pulmonary effects, hepatic effects, posterior
reversible encephalopathy syndrome (PRES), and malignancy. Within the category of
infections, severe infections, opportunistic infections (including progressive multifocal
leukoencephalopathy), and herpes simplex and herpes zoster were considered AESIs. Refer to
Section 8.2.4.4 for AESI definitions used in the trials.
Routine Clinical Tests
For each subject, the Applicant collected laboratory tests, radiologic tests, electrocardiograms
(ECGs), and ophthalmoscopy according to the Schedule of Activities defined in the study
protocol.
Screening samples for complete blood count (CBC) with differential, platelet count, lymphocyte
counts, T lymphocytes, B lymphocytes, natural killer lymphocytes (TBNK), serum chemistry,
virology, thyroid panel, coagulation, urinalysis, high-sensitivity C-reactive protein (hs-CRP), TB
screen, and stool sample were obtained and reviewed prior to randomization. Safety endpoints
included the incidence and severity of laboratory abnormalities and change from baseline in
laboratory values. Refer to Table 108 in Section 16.5 of the Appendix for details on the
laboratory tests performed during the trials.
All laboratory tests with values considered clinically significantly abnormal during participation
in the study or within 30 days after the last dose of study treatment were repeated until the
values return to normal or baseline or are no longer considered clinically significant by the
Investigator or Medical Monitor. AE included clinically relevant adverse changes in laboratory
parameters over the course of the study.
Total white blood cell (WBC), neutrophil, lymphocyte, and TBNK counts were available for
review prior to randomization. Since lymphopenia is a known class effect of S1P receptor
Version date: October 12, 2018
Reference ID: 5259863

NDA/BLA Multi-Disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 216956
Velsipity (etrasimod)
modulators, Investigators were blinded to the complete blood count lab results after
randomization. An unblinded Medical Monitor assessed WBC differential lab results and
alerted the Investigator if (1) ANC < 1000 cells/µL and/or (2) ALC <200 cells/µL.
All laboratory assessments required by the protocol were performed by a central laboratory
unless otherwise stated.

Velsipity (etrasimod)
Pertinent Narratives for SAEs
The following SAEs occurred only in the etrasimod group and were considered to be possibly
related to etrasimod by the review team: bacterial pneumonia, arthralgia, increased intracranial
pressure, and hepatic enzyme increased.
•

Bacterial pneumonia: A 55-year-old White male had an SAE of "pneumonia bacterial” on
Study Day 163. On Study Day 158, the subject reported fatigue and body aches, followed by
a nonserious AE of pyrexia. The subject continued to have fever and developed headache,
nausea, bloating, and abdominal pain through Study Day 163. On Study Day 163, the subject
was hospitalized for bacterial pneumonia where a chest X-ray showed mild infiltration on
the left, lower fields. The subject was treated with antibiotics and on Study Day 168, the
SAE was considered recovered/resolved. Relevant medical history included UC (since 2011)
and daily cigarette smoking (since 1985). The only concomitant medication at the onset of
the SAE was sulfasalazine. Study treatment was unchanged. The Investigator assessed the
event to be not related to study treatment. Given that S1P receptor modulators are known
to affect lymphocyte trafficking, it is possible that etrasimod may increase the risk of
infection, and the SAE of bacterial pneumonia may possibly be related to etrasimod. The
risk of infections will be included in the Warnings and Precautions section of the label.

•

Arthralgia: A 48-year-old White female had an SAE of “arthralgia” on Study Day 162. On
Study Day 137, the subject was placed on bedrest and started on methylprednisolone for a
nonserious AE of “UC-associated arthralgia” of the wrist, elbow, and knee. On Study Day
141, the subject complained of pain in the knee, hip, and wrist joints and was hospitalized
for an SAE of “arthralgia.” During the hospitalization, the subject did not receive additional
treatment. Laboratory analyses showed mild anemia and elevated C-reactive protein (CRP).
An ultrasound did not demonstrate venous thrombosis in the lower limbs. No action was
taken with the study treatment. The subject’s last dose of study treatment prior to the SAE
of arthralgia was on Study Day 141. The SAE of arthralgia was resolved on Study Day 145.
The review team agrees with investigator’s assessment that the SAE of arthralgia was likely
not related to study treatment and is likely an extraintestinal manifestation of UC. However,
arthralgia was reported in a greater proportion of subjects who received etrasimod
compared to placebo in APD334-301 (4% etrasimod vs. 2% placebo). While arthralgia is not
a known S1P receptor class effect, it has been reported in subjects with UC who received
another S1P receptor, ozanimod. Additionally, arthralgia has been reported as an adverse
reaction of other IBD therapeutics. Therefore, arthralgia will be included in the Adverse
Reactions section of the label.

•

Increased intracranial pressure: A 63-year-old male had an SAE of “increased intracranial
pressure” first reported on Study Day 90. Relevant medical history included ulcerative colitis
(since 2019), anal fissure (since 2019), and anemia (since 2019). Concomitant therapy
included mesalazine. The subject reported gradual loss of eyesight on Study Day 90 that
started on Study Day 58. On Study Day 96, an optical coherence tomography (OCT) was
performed, which did not show macular edema. An MRI was performed on Study Day 100,

Version date: October 12, 2018
Reference ID: 5259863

139

NDA/BLA Multi-Disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 216956
Velsipity (etrasimod)
which was reported as showing no significant abnormalities. The subject was seen by a
neurologist on Study Day 105. On Study Day 110, a spinal tap and fluid analysis was
performed, and the neurologist diagnosed the subject with increased ICP of unknown
origins. The study treatment was interrupted from Study Day 107 to Study Day 114. On
Study Day 114, the subject reported that his eyesight had improved but was not fully
recovered. On the same day, study treatment resumed. On Study Day 160, an OCT was
performed and revealed atrophy of the optic nerve in the left eye. The subject’s visual
acuity in the left eye had not improved. On Study Day 236, an OCT was performed and
showed similar results as on Study Day 160. The SAE of increased intracranial pressure was
considered recovered/resolved on Study Day 243, with sequelae of ocular atrophy and
partial vision loss.
According to the Applicant, the subject’s neurologist attributed the increased ICP as more
likely an episode of what could be a chronic process of rise in ICP; however, S1P receptor
modulators have been associated with ocular disorders (e.g., macular edema) and disorders
of the central nervous system (e.g., PRES, PML). It is also possible the increased ICP was
secondary to mesalazine use, in which increased ICP has been reported as an AE. Therefore,
a relationship between etrasimod and the SAE of increased intracranial pressure cannot be
ruled out.
•

Migraine: A 52-year-old White female had an SAE of migraine on Study Day 80 requiring
hospitalization. The subject’s relevant medical history included ulcerative colitis (since
1997), migraine (since 1981), and arthralgia (2019-2020). Concomitant medication ongoing
at the onset of the SAE of migraine included rizatriptan, zolmitriptan, paracetamol, and
estradiol. It was reported that the subject did not have a history of recurrent migraine
episodes with the same symptoms or cluster headaches, and she had not experienced any
usual migraine triggers. However, 2 weeks prior to the event, the subject reported recurrent
headaches that per the Investigator, were not from the usual triggers that were consistent
with her usual migraine headaches; they were much more severe.
The subject’s last dose of study treatment (etrasimod 2 mg) prior to the SAE was on Study
Day 79, and study treatment was interrupted. A magnetic resonance imaging scan was
normal. The SAE was considered resolved on Study Day 81.
On Study Day 89, the subject experienced nonserious AEs of mild vomiting and mild
migraine. On Study Day 93, after the SAE resolved, study treatment resumed. Upon
interruption, the event abated, but then reappeared after study treatment was
reintroduced. On Study Day 116, the subject stopped estradiol treatment that had been
initiated on Study Day 71 as hormone replacement. Per the investigator, a gynecologist had
advised to stop estradiol treatment due to its side effect profile, which includes migraines.
The subject continued to experience mild and moderate AEs of migraine through Study Day
174. The Investigator assessed the event to be unlikely related to study treatment;
however, the review team determined that a relationship between etrasimod and the SAE
of migraine cannot be ruled out. Although the subject had a history of migraines and

Version date: October 12, 2018
Reference ID: 5259863

140

NDA/BLA Multi-Disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 216956
Velsipity (etrasimod)
migraine is a potential side effect of the concomitant estradiol, migraine was reported in a
greater proportion of subjects who received etrasimod (3/289, 1%) compared to placebo
(none) during APD334-301. Therefore, headache, including related terms headache,
migraine, tension headache, and sinus headache, will be included as an adverse reaction in
the Adverse Reactions section of the label.
•

Hepatic enzyme increased: A 34-year-old female reported SAEs of “abdominal pain” and
“elevated hepatic enzymes” first reported on Study Day 196, requiring hospitalization for
abdominal pain. The subject’s relevant medical history included ulcerative colitis (since
2020). Concomitant medications ongoing at the onset of the SAEs included
fluticasone/formoterol and levonorgestrel. Baseline alanine aminotransferase (ALT),
aspartate aminotransferase (AST), bilirubin, alkaline phosphatase, and gamma-glutamyl
transferase (GGT) were normal (Study Day 1). On Study Day 195, the subject reported
“intense epigastric abdominal pain that was radiating to her back” and exacerbated by
eating and drinking. The subject was hospitalized on Study Day 196 for “severe abdominal
pain.” The same day, the subject was found to have elevated liver tests during the
hospitalization. The subject’s ALT was 3.5 x ULN, AST was 7.9 x ULN, GGT was 1.9 x ULN, and
bilirubin was 1.8 x ULN. The subject’s alkaline phosphatase was normal. An ultrasound did
not show gallstones or gallbladder inflammation; however, a magnetic resonance
cholangiopancreatography was performed which showed stenosis of the posterior right
hepatic duct with distal dilation. According to the Investigator, the findings could represent
early primary sclerosing cholangitis. The subject received symptomatic treatment for
abdominal pain during the hospitalization. On Study Day 202, the subject’s abdominal pain
had resolved, and she was discharged from the hospital. On the same day, the subject’s AST
and bilirubin had returned to normal though her ALT and GGT remained elevated 2.8 x ULN
and 2.2 x ULN, respectively. No action was taken with study treatment due to the events.
On Study Day 224, the subject’s hepatic enzymes were normal, and the SAE of hepatic
enzyme increased was considered recovered/resolved. The investigator assessed the events
to be unlikely related to study treatment; however, liver injury has been reported in other
S1P receptor modulators and is an AESI for etrasimod. The risk of liver injury will be
communicated in the Warnings and Precautions section of the label.

In summary, the causes of SAEs were varied and the overall frequency of SAEs and SAEs leading
to discontinuation were similar between the etrasimod and placebo groups. However, several
SAEs were considered likely related to etrasimod due to the mechanism of action of the drug
and will be described in labeling.
8.2.4.3.

Discontinuations Due to Adverse Events

The overall incidence of AEs leading to discontinuation was similar in the etrasimod and
placebo groups with 12/289 (4%) of subjects treated with etrasimod experiencing at least one
AE that led to discontinuation, compared to 7/144 (5%) of subjects who received placebo.
Ulcerative colitis and colon perforation were the only SAEs that led to permanent treatment
discontinuation, occurring in 1 subject in the etrasimod group and 1 subject in the placebo

There were no AEs that led to discontinuation that were reported by more than one subject
who received etrasimod, except for ulcerative colitis. However, ulcerative colitis was reported
in a similar proportion of subjects in the etrasimod group as in the placebo group and is most
likely related to the underlying disease and lack of response to treatment, rather than an
adverse event. Narratives for AEs leading to discontinuation of etrasimod that were also AESIs
are described below.
AEs Leading to Discontinuation That Were Also AESIs
•

ALT elevated: An 18-year-old White male with a 10-year history of primary sclerosing
cholangitis (PSC) was discontinued from study treatment due to elevated ALT and AST.
Concomitant medications included mesalazine and ursodeoxycholic acid. At the baseline
visit, mild elevations of ALT and AST (1.7 x ULN and 1.4 x ULN, respectively) were noted. On
Study Day 15, both ALT and AST increased to 3.1 x ULN but on Study Day 21, both had
returned to baseline levels after the study drug was held from Study Days 18-23.

Version date: October 12, 2018
Reference ID: 5259863

142

NDA/BLA Multi-Disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 216956
Velsipity (etrasimod)
On Study Day 45, ALT was 8.7 x ULN, and AST was 10.6 x ULN. No treatment was given for
the events and study drug was discontinued on Study Day 46. Both ALT and AST decreased
significantly on Study Day 50 (2.1 and 1.0 x ULN, respectively). Alkaline phosphatase was
mildly elevated throughout the study from 1.1-2.1 x ULN and there was a mild to moderate
elevation of GGT (1.8-4.0 x ULN). Bilirubin was consistently normal. On Study Day 65, the
events of ALT increased and AST increased were considered recovered/resolved at which
time ALT was 1.8 x ULN and AST was 1.2 x ULN (i.e., similar to baseline). The review team
determined that the events may have been related to study treatment given the liver
enzymes returned to baseline after study drug discontinuation; however, the liver enzyme
elevations could have been due to the subject’s past medical history of PSC.
Recommendations for evaluation of transaminases and bilirubin prior to initiation of
treatment and during treatment are listed in the Warnings and Precautions section of the
label.
•

Alkaline phosphatase elevated: A 44-year-old White female had an alkaline phosphatase
1.0 - 1.2 x ULN beginning on Study Day 171. Mild increases were noted in ALT beginning on
Study Day 115 (1.5 - 2.7 x ULN) and in AST beginning on Study Day 227 (1.3 - 1.4 x ULN). No
transaminase increase reached 3 x ULN. GGT was elevated at screening (5.5 x ULN) and
throughout the study (maximum of 21.8 x ULN on Study Day 280). The subject reported no
symptoms. Relevant medical history included ulcerative colitis and obesity. Study drug was
discontinued due to the event of alkaline phosphatase increased, with the last dose taken
on Study Day 293. At the time of the subject’s last available report, the events were
considered not recovered/not resolved. The investigator assessed the events of GGT
increased, ALT increased, and blood alkaline phosphatase increased to be probably related
to study treatment. Given that the subject’s baseline GGT was elevated, the liver test
elevations may not be related to etrasimod; however, S1PR modulators have been
associated with a risk of hepatic injury. Therefore, a relationship between the AE of
“alkaline phosphatase elevated” and etrasimod cannot be ruled out. The risk of liver injury
will be communicated in the Warnings and Precautions section of the label.

•

Bradycardia: A 51-year-old White male had an AE of bradycardia and dizziness reported on
Study Day 2. On Study Day 1, prior to dosing of study treatment, the subject’s heart rate
(HR) was 72 beats per minute (bpm). On Study Day 2, the subject reported the AEs of
bradycardia and dizziness, and his heart rate was 44 bpm. Five hours later, the subject’s
heart rate was 42 bpm. The subject’s blood pressure remained stable throughout and
electrocardiograms (ECGs) were normal. No treatment was administered for these events.
The subject discontinued study treatment due to the AE of bradycardia and received the
last dose of study treatment on Study Day 2. On Study Day 3, the subject’s heart rate was 46
and 47 bpm, and the severity of the dizziness had improved. On Study Day 4, the subject’s
heart rate was 58 bpm, and the bradycardia and dizziness were considered
recovered/resolved. Bradycardia after the first dose of S1P receptor modulators is a known
class effect; therefore, the AE of bradycardia and dizziness are likely related to etrasimod.
The risk of bradyarrhythmia will be described in the Warning and Precaution section of the
label.

Version date: October 12, 2018
Reference ID: 5259863

143

NDA/BLA Multi-Disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 216956
Velsipity (etrasimod)
•

AV block second degree: A 48-year-old Black or African American female had AEs of
bradycardia and first-degree AV block on Study Days 1 and 2. The subject’s baseline ECG
was normal. At 4 hours post-dose on Study Day 1, the ECG results showed a HR of 49 bpm,
and a moderate AE of bradycardia was recorded. The ECG also showed a PR interval > 200
msec (258 msec). The investigator and central ECG reader reported a second-degree AV
block, Mobitz type I. No symptomatic AEs were reported, and no treatment was
administered for either event. The subject discontinued study treatment due to the AE of
“AV block second degree.” The subject’s last dose of study treatment was taken on Study
Day 1. On Study Day 2, repeat ECG showed a normal PR interval (172 msec) and HR (72
bpm), the events of AV block second degree and bradycardia were considered
recovered/resolved. The subject had no history of cardiac events and AV conduction delays
are a known risk with S1P receptor modulators; therefore, the AE of “AV block second
degree” is likely related to etrasimod. The risk of AV conduction delays will be described in
the Warning and Precaution section of the label.

•

Macular edema: A 43-year-old White female had an AE of macular edema on Study Day 75.
The subject reported blurred vision in the left eye on Study Day 74. On Study Day 75,
ophthalmic examination showed a decrease in visual acuity and macular edema. Treatment
was discontinued on Study Day 75 due to the nonserious AE of macular edema. The
subject’s last dose of study treatment was taken on Study Day 74. Medical history included
cerebral infarction and hypertension. Concomitant medications included mesalazine,
methylprednisolone, lercanidipine, and perindopril. Macular edema and decreased visual
acuity were ongoing at the time of early termination visit (Study Day 81). The AE of macular
edema was considered recovered/resolved on Study Day 139, and a follow-up
ophthalmologic exam on Study Day 173 was normal. Macular edema has been identified as
a risk in other S1P receptor modulators and was reported more frequently in subjects who
received etrasimod compared to placebo (18% vs. 8%). The risk of macular edema will be
communicated in the Warnings and Precautions section of the label and a baseline
evaluation of the fundus near the start of treatment and monitoring during treatment will
be recommended. Refer to Section 8.2.6.5 for additional analyses regarding the risk of eye
disorders.
8.2.4.4.

Adverse Events of Special Interest

At the time of protocol development, progressive multifocal leukoencephalopathy (PML) was
identified as an AESI. Additional AESIs were identified by the Applicant as the understanding of
the safety profile of etrasimod evolved. The Applicant conducted a retrospective, blinded
medical review to identify AESIs based on the mechanism of action of etrasimod, prior
experience with other S1PR modulators, and disease-specific potential risks. The following
categories and preferred terms were used to query the clinical database, and a blinded medical
review of these AEs was conducted to identify which events were AESIs. Refer to Section 8.2.6
for the review team’s analysis of adverse events that are of special interest based on known
class effects but did not meet the Applicant’s criteria for AESI.

Cardiovascular
o Bradycardia – if one or more of the following:
 HR was <40 bpm (with or without symptoms)
 Event was associated with clinical symptoms (e.g., lightheadedness)
 Event led to subject withdrawal
o Atrioventricular (AV) conduction delay – if one or more of the following:
 AV block was second degree (Mobitz Type I or Type II) or higher
 PR interval was greater than 230 ms
 Event was associated with clinical symptoms (e.g., dizziness) considered
potentially related based upon medical review
o Hypertension – if one or more of the following:
 Sustained systolic BP elevation was greater than 160 mmHg
 Sustained diastolic BP elevation was greater than 100 mmHg
 Medication was added for BP control
Macular edema – defined as an increase in central foveal thickness (CFT) ≥40 µm,
and/or with associated symptoms or clinically significant abnormal findings
Hepatic injury
o Liver transaminases increased – defined as any liver transaminase elevation
>5 x ULN or a sustained elevation >3 x ULN
o Bilirubin elevation – defined as bilirubin >2 x ULN without Gilbert’s syndrome
Infections

Pulmonary

Posterior reversible encephalopathy syndrome (PRES)
Malignancy

subjects with at least one AESI in the etrasimod group compared to placebo with 26/289 (9%)
of subjects treated with etrasimod experiencing at least one AESI, compared to 10/144 (7%) of
subjects who received placebo.

Cardiovascular adverse events, including hypertension, bradycardia, and AV conduction delay,
macular edema, and herpes infections were reported more frequently in the etrasimod group
compared to placebo. The overall incidence of liver injury AESIs were balanced between the
two groups. No AESIs of PML, bilirubin elevation, PRES, or malignancy were reported. AESIs that
were reported more frequently in subjects who received etrasimod compared to placebo are
described in detail below.
•

Cardiovascular events: Cardiovascular events were reported more frequently in
subjects who received etrasimod compared to placebo. One AE of bradycardia and 1 AE
of AV block second degree led to study discontinuation; refer to Section 8.2.4.3.
o AV Block First Degree: One subject experienced AV block first degree, 4 hours
post-dose on Study Day 1. The subject had no history of cardiac disease. Predose
on Study Day 1, ECG results showed normal T-wave morphology, sinus rhythm,
and atrial premature complexes, which were considered abnormal and clinically
significant by the investigator and the central reader. At 4 hours post-dose on
Study Day 1, ECG results showed PR interval >200 msec (238 msec, increased
from baseline 152 msec), normal T-wave morphology, sinus rhythm, and atrial
premature complexes considered abnormal and clinically significant by the
investigator and the central reader, and an AESI of AV block first degree was

Version date: October 12, 2018
Reference ID: 5259863

146

NDA/BLA Multi-Disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 216956
Velsipity (etrasimod)
reported. No treatment was administered for the event and no action was taken
with study treatment in response to the event. On Study Day 2, ECG results were
normal, and the event was considered recovered/resolved. The Investigator
assessed the event to be probably related to study treatment.
o Hypertension: Hypertension was reported as an adverse reaction in 3% of
subjects treated with etrasimod. No subjects treated with placebo met the
Applicant’s criteria for hypertension. The majority of the events were reported
by the Applicant as mild or moderate in severity. There was a small but
persistent increase in systolic blood pressure (SBP) compared to baseline in
subjects treated with etrasimod. However, the mean change from baseline to
Week 52 was 2.2 mmHg in the etrasimod group compared to -1.3 mmHg in the
placebo group, the clinical significance of which is unclear.
One subject reported an AE of “hypertensive crisis” on Day 218. The subject’s
vital signs before and after this episode were all normal. This event was
confirmed by the Investigator as not being a "hypertensive crisis" event based on
the objective blood pressure measurements and accepted diagnostic definitions.
No action was taken with study treatment and the event resolved on the same
day without intervention from the Investigator.
Due to the risk of cardiovascular events observed in the etrasimod development program and
the known risks described in other S1P receptor modulators, a Warnings and Precautions
section will be included in the label regarding the risk of bradyarrhythmia and AV conduction
delays, as well as increased blood pressure.
•

Macular edema: Macular edema was considered an AESI because it has been identified
as a risk in other S1P receptor modulators. The Applicant reported one case of a
nonserious AE of macular edema in a 43-year-old female treated with etrasimod during
APD334-301, which led to study discontinuation. The AE of macular edema is described
in detail in Section 8.2.4.3.

•

Infections (severe, opportunistic, or herpes infections)
o Herpes simplex and herpes zoster: Herpes viral infections were reported by more
subjects in the etrasimod group compared to placebo (2% vs. 1%). Herpes
simplex, including preferred terms herpes simplex and oral herpes, was reported
by 4/289 (1%) subjects in the etrasimod group versus 1/144 (1%) in the placebo
group. Herpes zoster was reported by 2/289 (1%) subjects in the etrasimod
group versus none in the placebo group.
Of the subjects who experienced herpes zoster, one was a 50-year-old White
female who on Study Day 395 had a nonserious AE of herpes zoster, followed by
a nonserious AE of post-herpetic neuralgia on Study Day 407. The subject

NDA/BLA Multi-Disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 216956
Velsipity (etrasimod)
Liver injury is described in labeling as a Warnings and Precautions for other S1P receptor
modulators, including Zeposia (ozanimod), which is approved for the treatment of
moderately to severely UC. Although the mechanism by which etrasimod might cause
liver injury is not known, a similar Warnings and Precautions will be included in the
etrasimod label. Refer to Section 8.2.6.4 for additional analyses that support the
potential of hepatotoxicity with etrasimod.
•

Dizziness: A greater proportion of subjects who received etrasimod had an AE of
dizziness (5%) compared to placebo (2%). All the AEs of dizziness were mild or moderate
in severity. No AEs were considered SAEs, and none led to study discontinuation.
Approximately half (7/15) of the AEs of dizziness were reported within the first 2 days of
treatment with etrasimod and may be related to the transient bradycardia observed
with etrasimod initiation. Dizziness has also been observed in trials of other S1P
receptor modulators. Taken together, the review team recommends including dizziness
in the Adverse Reactions section of the label.

•

Arthralgia: A greater proportion of subjects who received etrasimod had an AE of
arthralgia (4%) compared to placebo (2%). All the arthralgia AEs were mild or moderate
in severity. One AE of arthralgia was considered an SAE and is described in detail in
Section 8.2.4.2. None of the arthralgia AEs led to study discontinuation. Arthralgia can
be an extra-intestinal manifestation of inflammatory bowel disease, including UC;
however, arthralgia has been reported as an adverse reaction in other S1P receptor
modulators, including Zeposia (ozanimod), which is approved for the treatment of
moderately to severely active UC. Arthralgia will be included in the Adverse Reactions
section of the label.

•

Hypertension: A greater proportion of subjects who received etrasimod had an AE of
hypertension (3%) compared to placebo (1%). Most hypertension AEs were reviewed in
Section 8.2.4.4 as hypertension with a sustained systolic BP elevation greater than 160
mmHg was considered an AESI. Two additional AEs of hypertension (1 preferred term
“blood pressure increased” in the etrasimod group and 1 preferred term “hypertension”
in the placebo group) were identified as TEAEs but not considered AESIs by the
Applicant. Both AEs were either mild or moderate and did not result in study
discontinuation. Therefore, the two additional AEs of hypertension do not change the
analysis of the risk of hypertension due to etrasimod as described in the previous
section. A Warnings and Precautions section will be included in the label regarding the
risk of increased blood pressure, and hypertension will be included in the Adverse
Reactions section of the label.

•

Urinary Tract Infection: A greater proportion of subjects who received etrasimod had an
AE of urinary tract infection (UTI) compared to placebo (3% and 2%, respectively). No
events of UTI were SAEs or led to study discontinuation or interruption. UTI was
reported as a frequent adverse reaction in subjects with multiple sclerosis who received

Velsipity (etrasimod)
S1P receptor modulators Zeposia (ozanimod)4 and Ponvory (ponesimod) 5. Given that
etrasimod may increase the risk of infection, UTI will be included in the Adverse
