•

Nausea: A greater proportion of subjects who received etrasimod had an AE of nausea
(3%) compared to placebo (1%). Most nausea AEs were mild in severity, with only one
considered moderate. None of the AEs were SAEs, led to study discontinuation, or a
pause in study treatment. An imbalance was also observed in APD334-302 and APD334003 as described in Section 8.2.5.5. Nausea has also been reported as an adverse
reaction in other S1PR modulators, including Zeposia (ozanimod), which is approved for
the treatment of moderately to severely active UC. Taken together, nausea will be
included in the Adverse Reactions section of the label.

•

Hypercholesterolemia: A greater proportion of subjects who received etrasimod had an
AE of hypercholesterolemia (3%) compared to placebo (0%). Most AEs were mild, only
one AE was considered moderate. All subjects with hypercholesterolemia AEs had
cholesterol levels that remained < 2 x ULN. Hypercholesterolemia has been reported as
an adverse reaction in another S1P receptor modulator, Ponvory (ponesimod). Although
the clinical significance of the degree of hypercholesterolemia is uncertain, the review
team considered it important to inform patients and prescribers of this potential risk.
Therefore, hypercholesterolemia will be included in the Adverse Reactions section of
the label.

•

Herpes viral infection: The AEs of herpes viral infection were considered AESI and are
described in detail in Section 8.2.4.4.

•

Blood creatine phosphokinase increased: A greater proportion of subjects who
received etrasimod had an AE of increased blood creatine phosphokinase (CPK) (2%)
compared to placebo (1%). All the AEs were mild or moderate, none of the AEs were
SAEs, and none of the AEs led to study discontinuation. Elevation of CPK resolved in all
subjects despite continued treatment with etrasimod. There were no other AEs related
to rhabdomyolysis (e.g., rhabdomyolysis, myoglobinuria, chromaturia) reported in these
subjects. CPK elevation has not been reported as an adverse reaction in other S1P
receptor modulators. Therefore, the increased CPK is not likely to be related to
etrasimod. The review team does not recommend its inclusion in labeling.

•

Muscle spasms and vomiting: A greater proportion of subjects who received etrasimod
had an AE of muscle spasms (2%) and vomiting (2%) compared to placebo (none for
both). All of the AEs were mild or moderate, none of the AEs were SAEs, and none of the
AEs led to study discontinuation. One AE of vomiting led to study drug interruption for 1
day. Only 1 subject who reported an AE of vomiting also reported an AE of nausea on

8.2.4.6.

Laboratory Findings, Vital Signs, and Electrocardiograms

Laboratory Findings
Routine hematology and chemistry clinical laboratory testing were performed during Screening,
at Baseline, and throughout the study from Weeks 2 to 52, and at 2- and 4-week follow-up visits
(Weeks 54 and 56), early termination visit, and/or end of treatment visit according to the
Schedule of Activities.
No clinically meaningful differences between etrasimod and placebo were observed in
hematology and chemistry parameters except for lymphopenia. However, lymphopenia was
anticipated based on the mechanism of action of the drug. Additional safety analyses for
lymphopenia are described in detail in Section8.2.6.2.
Vital Signs
Due to the anticipated effects of etrasimod on bradyarrhythmia and atrioventricular conduction
delays, first-dose cardiac monitoring was implemented during APD334-301. Subjects were
monitored in-clinic for at least 4 hours with the following monitoring:
•

Baseline vital signs and 12-lead ECG at pre-randomization

•

Subjects remained under observation at the site for at least 4 hours after the first dose of
study treatment.

•

At Hours 1, 2, and 3 post-dose, the HR and systolic/diastolic BP were assessed. If the subject
had a HR <50 bpm or if cardiovascular symptoms developed, then the subject would remain
closely monitored and a 12-lead ECG would be performed.

•

At Hour 4, a discharge assessment was performed including heart rate, systolic/diastolic BP,
and a 12-lead ECG. Subjects could be discharged from the clinic if they met the discharge
criteria (Table 56).

•

If subjects did not meet the discharge criteria at Hour 4, extended cardiac monitoring was
performed including:
o Hourly vital signs and 12-lead ECG until the subject meets the discharge criteria.
o Any subject who required extended monitoring on Day 1 must return on Day 2 for
the second dose and were re-monitored as on Day 1.

Additionally, subjects underwent the same first-dose cardiac monitoring procedures as was

Bradycardia was reported by a greater proportion of subjects who were treated with etrasimod
compared to placebo. The impact of etrasimod on bradyarrhythmia is discussed in detail in
Section 8.2.6.2. Similarly, hypertension was an AESI and observed in a greater proportion of
subjects treated with etrasimod compared to placebo. Refer to Section 8.2.4.4 for additional
details.
No other clinically meaningful differences between etrasimod and placebo were observed in
other routine vital signs during APD334-301.
Electrocardiograms (ECGs)
During APD334-301, 12-lead ECGs were obtained during the first-dose cardiac monitoring as

during unscheduled visits. Bradyarrhythmia and AV conduction delays are described in Section

QT
During APD334-301, no clinically significant trends for QT prolongation were observed. The QT
prolongation potential of etrasimod was assessed in a thorough QT (TQT) study. The results
from the TQT study indicated that etrasimod was not associated with significant QTc prolonging
effect. Refer to the QT-IRT Review by Eliford Kitabi dated March 3, 2023 (DARRTS ID: 5135842).

Two subjects that received etrasimod experienced SAEs that led to study discontinuation, both
due to ulcerative colitis. SAEs of ulcerative colitis were considered unlikely to be related to
etrasimod as these represent worsening of the underlying disease. Pertinent narratives for SAEs
are described below.
Pertinent Narratives for SAEs
•

Coronary artery disease: During APD334-302, a 62-year-old female was hospitalized for
an SAE of coronary artery disease on Study Day 15. Relevant medical history included
dyslipidemia (since 1975), obesity (since 1980), sleeve gastrectomy (2020), ulcerative
colitis (since 2017), and hypertension (since 2021). Concomitant medications ongoing at
the onset of the event of coronary artery disease included paracetamol and tramadol.
The subject’s last dose of study treatment prior to the onset of the SAE was on Study
Day 14. On Study Day 15, the subject reported symptoms of dyspnea on exertion for last
few months. The subject underwent coronary angioplasty on Study Day 15. Study
treatment dosing was not changed due to the event. The event was considered
recovered/resolved on Study Day 16 and the subject was discharged from the hospital.
The review team considered the SAE as unlikely to be related to etrasimod given the

Version date: October 12, 2018
Reference ID: 5259863

155

NDA/BLA Multi-Disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 216956
Velsipity (etrasimod)
subject’s past medical history and lack of other coronary artery disease events reported
in the etrasimod development program.
•

Hepatobiliary procedural complication: During APD334-302, a 29-year-old White male
underwent an endoscopic retrograde cholangiopancreatography (ERCP) with stent
placement on Study Day 9 for the nonserious adverse event of primary sclerosing
cholangitis, diagnosed on Study Day -3 by magnetic resonance
cholangiopancreatography. Approximately 1.5 hours post ERCP procedure, the subject
reported abdominal pain (5/10 on the pain scale) and was admitted to the hospital for
pain control. The subject was diagnosed with post-ERCP pancreatitis, although no
laboratory values of pancreatic enzymes were provided by the Applicant. No action was
taken with study treatment due to this event. On Study Day 10, the SAE was considered
recovered/resolved and the subject was discharged. The subject’s last dose of study
treatment prior to the SAE was on Study Day 8. Relevant medical history included iron
deficiency (date not available), ulcerative colitis (since 2008), and osteopenia (since
2017). Concomitant medications ongoing at the onset of the event of hepatobiliary
procedural complication included mesalazine. The review team considered the SAE as
unlikely to be related to etrasimod, as post-ERCP pancreatitis is a well-described
potential complication of ERCP. Further, the ERCP was performed due to PSC, which was
diagnosed prior to initiating study treatment.

•

Migraine: During APD334-302, a 28-year-old White female with a history of migraine
and intermittent headache reported an SAE of migraine leading to hospitalization and
study treatment interruption. The event was first reported on Study Day 9 when the
subject presented to the emergency department with frontal headache and associated
monocular visual blurring, vomiting, a tingling sensation in her arm, and dysphasia.
Computed tomography (CT) of the head and neck and magnetic resonance imaging
(MRI) of the brain were normal. The last dose of study drug prior to the SAE onset was
on Study Day 8. Concomitant medications at the onset of the event included
drospirenone/ethinylestradiol betadex clathrate, mesalazine, and paracetamol.
On Study Day 10, the SAE of severe migraine was considered recovered/resolved and
study treatment was resumed on Study Day 11. The Investigator assessed the event as
unlikely to be related to study treatment; however, the review team determined that a
relationship between etrasimod and the SAE of migraine cannot be ruled out. Although
the subject had a past medical history of migraine, an imbalance in headache AEs
(including migraine) was observed for APD334-301 (refer to Section 8.2.4.5) and
APD334-302 and APD334-003 (refer to Section 8.2.5.5). Therefore, headache, including
related terms headache, migraine, tension headache, and sinus headache, will be
included as an adverse reaction in the Adverse Reactions section of the label.

The most common AE that led to study discontinuation was ulcerative colitis. Two of the AEs of
ulcerative colitis were SAEs. However, AEs of ulcerative colitis are most likely related to the
underlying disease and lack of response to treatment, rather than an adverse event. Four
subjects discontinued from the study due to AEs that were considered AESIs, one AE of liver
function test abnormal, one AV block first degree, and three AEs of bradycardia. These AEs are
described in detail below.
•

Liver function test abnormal: During APD334-302, a 36-year-old Asian female
experienced both AST and ALT elevations to 3.1 x ULN and 5.8 x ULN, respectively, on
Study Day 15. On Study Day 19, the subject discontinued study treatment due to the AE
of liver function test abnormal. The AST remained elevated to ≥ 4 x ULN and ALT to > 6 x
ULN through Study Day 28, after which both returned to normal on Study Day 36 and
remained there through the final visit on Study Day 50. No elevation in bilirubin was
observed. Azathioprine had been discontinued 14 days before randomization, and
concomitant medications included propranolol for migraine and oral contraceptive. No

treatment was administered for the event. Given S1P receptor modulators have been

in the etrasimod development program, the review team considered this AE as likely

o Bradycardia: 3 subjects treated with etrasimod discontinued the study due to
AESIs of bradycardia.


The first subject was a 73-year-old White female with a longstanding

Study Day 1 were 61 bpm and 66 bpm, respectively. Baseline ECG on
Study Day -28 was interpreted as normal. On Study Day 1, the subject had
a moderate AE of “sinus bradycardia” (HR of 49 bpm) and discontinued
study treatment on the same day. No treatment was given for the event.
There were no associated symptomatic adverse events reported and no
changes to the subject’s blood pressure. The subject’s only dose of study
treatment was taken on Study Day 1. The subject was monitored on
Study Day 2 (after discontinuing treatment) where the HR was 48-53
bpm. On Study Day 3, the event was considered recovered/resolved with
HR of 56 bpm. The event was assessed to be related to study treatment.



The second subject was a 43-year-old White female who experienced a
mild AE of “sinus bradycardia” 4 hours after receiving etrasimod (HR of
45 bpm) on Study Day 1. A repeat ECG showed a HR of 51 bpm on Study
Day 1, and the event was considered recovered/resolved. The subject
had repeat vital signs and ECG performed at the Early Termination visit
on Study Day 6, which showed a HR of 60 bpm.



The third subject was a 23-year-old Black male who experienced a mild
AE of bradycardia after receiving etrasimod with a pre-dose HR of 60 bpm
and a 4-hour post-dose HR of 45 bpm on Study Day 1. Two additional
hours of monitoring on Day 1 had similar heart rates of 49 bpm and 50

recovered/resolved. No action was taken with study drug at that time. On
Study Day 2, the subject had a mild AE of bradycardia with a pre-dose HR
of 51 bpm and a 1-hour post-dose HR of 45 bpm. The subject also had
nonserious mild AEs of dizziness and palpitations on Study Day 2. The AEs

ECG finding of nonspecific T wave abnormality that remained constant

concomitant medications. The study drug was permanently discontinued
because of the adverse event of ECG T wave abnormal. On Study Day 382, the
event of ECG T wave abnormal was considered recovered/resolved. Given that
the ECG T wave abnormality was identified prior to initiating treatment with
etrasimod, it is unlikely that that the AE was related to the study drug.

event of neurological symptom to be related to study drug. A follow up report was
solicited by the Applicant and received approximately 3 years and 7 months after study
completion. At that time, the subject reported ongoing “transient symptoms” (slight
staggering when walking, loss of sensitivity in fingers and toes). After study completion,
she was never evaluated by a neurologist and had no imaging studies. Hypertension has
been controlled since medications were adjusted around the time of study completion.
The review team considered that the neurological symptoms may be related to the
subject’s past medical history and other concomitant medications; however, details are
lacking regarding the subject’s symptoms due to unavailability of additional information.
Therefore, a relationship between etrasimod and the AE of neurological symptom
cannot be determined.
8.2.5.4.

Adverse Events of Special Interest

Similar to APD334-301, PML was identified as an AESI for APD334-302 and APD334-003 at the
time of protocol development. APD334-302 and APD334-003 had a similar retrospective,
blinded medical review to identify additional AESIs as described for APD334-301; refer to
Section 8.2.4.4 for additional details. The review team performed additional analyses of adverse
events that are of special interest based on known class effects but that did not meet the
Applicant’s criteria for AESI; refer to Section 8.2.6.
The AESIs as identified by the Applicant for APD334-302 and APD334-302 are shown in Table
61. Overall, there were more subjects with at least one AESI in the etrasimod group compared
to placebo with 15/288 (5%) of subjects treated with etrasimod experiencing at least one AESI,
compared to 5/170 (3%) of subjects treated with placebo.

•

Liver injury: The overall incidence of liver injury AESIs was low at 1% in the etrasimod
group; however, there were no subjects with AESIs of liver injury in the placebo group.

severity. Additionally, there were no cases that raised concern for drug-induced liver
injury, including no Hy’s Law cases. One AESI of liver transaminase elevation (PT “liver

subject was a 68-year-old Asian male who had withdrawn from the study on Study Day
20 due to “withdrawal by subject or parent/guardian.” On Study Day 36, the subject
reported a worsening of bowel movement frequency, and a CMV stool antigen test was
performed and was positive. An AESI of CMV infection was reported on the same day. At
the time of the CMV AESI, the subject had been receiving rectal budesonide since Study
Day 20 (day of study discontinuation) and had just started vedolizumab on Study Day 36
(the day of the AESI). Given CMV is a common infection observed in patients with UC
and AESI was reported after the subject had discontinued etrasimod, the relationship
between etrasimod and the AESI is uncertain.
8.2.5.5.

Treatment Emergent Adverse Events and Adverse Reactions

An overview of treatment emergent adverse events (TEAEs) for the pooled data for APD334302 and APD334-003 is shown in Table 58. Overall, the proportion of subjects with at least one
TEAE was similar in subjects who received etrasimod (140/288, 49%) compared to placebo
(81/170, 47%). The majority of TEAEs were mild or moderate in severity. The number of
subjects who reported a severe TEAE was similar between etrasimod (10/288, 4%) and placebo
(8/170, 4%).
TEAEs that occurred in at least 2% of subjects during APD334-302 and APD334-003 and at a
higher frequency than placebo are shown in Table 62. Headache and elevated liver tests were
the most common TEAEs during APD334-302 and APD334-003, which was similar to APD334301.

Version date: October 12, 2018
Reference ID: 5259863

162

NDA/BLA Multi-Disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 216956
Velsipity (etrasimod)
SAE. One subject was discontinued from study treatment due to a TEAE of elevated liver
tests (preferred term “liver function test abnormal”); refer to Section 8.2.5.3. An
imbalance was also observed in APD334-301 (refer to Section 8.2.4.5). Liver injury is a
Warnings and Precautions for other S1P receptor modulators, including Zeposia, which
is approved for the treatment of moderately to severely active UC. Although the
mechanism by which etrasimod might cause liver injury is not known, a similar Warnings
and Precautions will be included in the etrasimod label. Refer to Section 8.2.6.4 for
additional hepatotoxicity analyses.
•

Colitis ulcerative: Although a greater proportion of subjects who received etrasimod
had a TEAE of “colitis ulcerative” (i.e., ulcerative colitis) (4%) compared to placebo (3%),
the difference was small. Three TEAEs of ulcerative colitis were SAEs. Two SAEs and 6
non-serious TEAEs of ulcerative colitis led to study discontinuation. The review team
determined that TEAEs of ulcerative colitis are likely to be related to the underlying
disease/lack of response to therapy and unlikely to represent a drug effect and does not
recommend inclusion in the Adverse Reactions section of the label.

•

Nausea: A greater proportion of subjects who received etrasimod had a TEAE of nausea
(4%) compared to placebo (2%). The majority of the TEAEs of nausea were mild or
moderate, one TEAE was severe. None of the TEAEs of nausea were considered SAEs,
led to study discontinuation, or a pause in treatment. An imbalance was also observed
in APD334-301 as described in Section 8.2.4.5. Nausea has also been reported as an
adverse reaction in other S1P receptor modulators, including Zeposia, which is approved
for the treatment of moderately to severely active UC. Taken together, nausea will be
included in the Adverse Reaction sections of the label.

•

Pyrexia: Although a greater proportion of subjects who received etrasimod had a TEAE
of pyrexia (3%) compared to placebo (2%), the difference was small. The majority of
TEAEs of pyrexia were associated with other TEAEs that may explain the pyrexia (e.g.,
recent COVID-19 vaccination, diarrhea, upper respiratory tract infection). The review
team determined that pyrexia was unlikely to be related to etrasimod and does not
recommend inclusion in the Adverse Reactions section of the label.

•

Urinary tract infection: A greater proportion of subjects who received etrasimod had a
TEAE of urinary tract infection (3%) compared to placebo (0%). All of the urinary tract
infection TEAEs were mild or moderate. None of the TEAEs were considered SAEs or led
to study discontinuation. The mechanism of action of etrasimod (i.e., inhibition of
lymphocyte trafficking) has been associated with an increased risk of infection. Aligned
with other S1P receptor modulators, the label for etrasimod will include a Warnings and
Precautions regarding infection. Additionally, urinary tract infections will be included in
the Adverse Reactions section of the label.

•

Bradycardia: A greater proportion of subjects who received etrasimod had a TEAE of
bradycardia (3%) compared to placebo (0%). Four TEAEs of bradycardia were considered

Reference ID: 5259863

164

NDA/BLA Multi-Disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 216956
Velsipity (etrasimod)
AESIs and are described in Section 8.2.5.4. An additional four TEAEs of bradycardia were
not reported as AESIs because they did not meet the Applicant’s criteria. Three subjects
discontinued the study due to TEAEs of bradycardia, as described in Section 8.2.5.3. The
mechanism of action of etrasimod has been associated with bradyarrhythmia and a
Warnings and Precautions regarding bradyarrhythmia will be included in labeling.
Additionally, bradycardia will be described in the Adverse Reactions section of the label.
•

Blood creatine phosphokinase increased: A greater proportion of subjects who
received etrasimod had an AE of blood creatine phosphokinase (CPK) increased (2%)
compared to placebo (1%). All of the AEs were mild or moderate, none of the AEs were
SAEs, and none of the AEs led to study discontinuation. All the subjects had normal CPK
following the AE of increased CPK. There were no other AEs related to rhabdomyolysis
(e.g., rhabdomyolysis, myoglobinuria, chromaturia) reported in these subjects.
Therefore, the increased CPK is not likely to be related to etrasimod. The review team
did not recommend inclusion in labeling.

•

Vomiting, abdominal distension, and diarrhea: Vomiting, abdominal distension, and
diarrhea were each reported by more subjects who received etrasimod compared to
placebo. However, the majority of TEAEs of vomiting, abdominal distension, and
diarrhea were reported in association with additional TEAEs (e.g., pyrexia, abdominal
pain, ulcerative colitis, gastroenteritis) that may explain the reported TEAEs. The review
team determined that vomiting, abdominal distension, and diarrhea were unlikely to be
related to etrasimod and does not recommend inclusion in the Adverse Reactions
section of the label.
8.2.5.6.

Laboratory Findings, Vital Signs, and Electrocardiograms

Laboratory Findings
Routine hematology and chemistry clinical laboratory testing were performed during Screening,
at Baseline, and throughout the study from Weeks 2 to 12, and at 2-week follow-up visits
(Week 14), early termination visit, and/or end of treatment visit according to the Schedule of
Activities.
No clinically meaningful differences between etrasimod and placebo were observed in
hematology and chemistry parameters except for lymphopenia. However, lymphopenia was
anticipated based on the mechanism of action of the drug. Additional safety analyses for
lymphopenia are described in detail in Section 8.2.6.2.
Vital Signs
First-dose cardiac monitoring was performed during APD334-302, similar to APD334-301. Refer
to Section 8.2.4.6, Vital Signs for further details. During APD334-003, subjects had 24-hour
Holter monitoring. Bradycardia was reported by a greater proportion of subjects who were
treated with etrasimod compared to placebo, as described previously in Section 8.2.5.4 and
Version date: October 12, 2018
Reference ID: 5259863

165

NDA/BLA Multi-Disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 216956
Velsipity (etrasimod)
Section 8.2.5.5. The impact of etrasimod on bradyarrhythmia is also discussed in detail in
Section 8.2.6.3.
No other clinically meaningful differences between etrasimod and placebo were observed in
other routine vital signs during APD334-302 and APD334-003.
Electrocardiograms (ECGs)
During APD334-302, 12-lead ECGs were obtained during the first-dose cardiac monitoring as
described previously. Additionally, 12-lead ECGs were obtained at Week 12 and during
unscheduled visits. During APD334-003, Holter monitoring was performed during the first 24hours of treatment and 12-lead ECGs were obtained at screening, Weeks 0, 1, 2, 4, 8, and 12
and during unscheduled visits. Bradyarrhythmia and AV conduction delays are described in
Section 8.2.5.4 and Section 8.2.6.2. Overall, the number of abnormal and clinically significant
ECGs (e.g., bradycardia, first-degree AV block, second-degree AV block, complete right bundle
branch block, left bundle branch block, sinus tachycardia, atrial fibrillation) was balanced
between etrasimod (8%) and placebo (11%) for APD334-302 and no clinically meaningful
changes in ECG parameters were reported for APD334-003.
QT
During APD334-302 and APD334-003, no clinically significant trends for QT prolongation were
observed. The QT prolongation potential of etrasimod was assessed in a thorough QT (TQT)
study. The results from the TQT study indicated that etrasimod was not associated with
significant QTc prolonging effect. Refer to the QT-IRT Review (DARRTS ID: 5135842) by Eliford
Kitabi dated March 3, 2023.
Analysis of Submission-Specific Safety Issues
8.2.6.1.

Infections

S1P receptor modulators have been associated with an increased risk of infection due to the
mechanism of action of the drug, which causes a reduction in peripheral blood lymphocytes.
The Applicant evaluated the risk of severe infections, opportunistic infections, and herpes
simplex and herpes zoster infections; refer to Section 8.2.4.4 and Section 8.2.5.4 for details of
these analyses. To further characterize the potential risk of infection, the review team
conducted additional safety analyses.

group than placebo; however, most infections were mild or moderate in severity, were not
serious, and did not lead to study discontinuation. However, based on the mechanism of action
of the drug and the observed imbalance in infections, a Warnings and Precautions regarding
infection will be included in labeling. Additionally, urinary tract infection and herpes viral
infection will be described in the Adverse Reactions section of the label to inform patients and
prescribers of the specific risk of these infections.
8.2.6.2.

Lymphopenia

Lymphopenia is a known S1P receptor modulator class effect due to the mechanism of action,
which reduces lymphocyte egress from lymph nodes. Since etrasimod was expected to cause
lymphopenia and may cause neutropenia, investigators were blinded to the complete blood
count lab results in order to preserve the blind. An unblinded Medical Monitor assessed WBC
differential lab results and alerted the Investigator if (1) ANC <1000 cells/µL and/or (2) ALC
<200 cells/µL. As a result, the incidence of TEAEs of lymphopenia and neutropenia may not be
an accurate reflection of lymphopenia and neutropenia during studies APD334-301, APD334302, and APD334-003 because the TEAE threshold for the investigators was high.
An analysis of the incidence of lymphopenia and neutropenia based on laboratory data for
APD334-301 and pooled data for APD334-302 and APD334-003 is shown in Table 64.

As expected, lymphopenia was more common in the subjects who received etrasimod
compared to placebo. An ALC of <200 cells/µL was observed in 16/289 (5.5%) and 2/288 (0.6%)
subjects who received etrasimod during APD334-301 and APD334-302 and APD334-003,
respectively versus none in placebo. Among subjects with an ALC of <200 cells/µL, rhinitis,
COVID-19, and tinea each occurred once in 3 subjects, and upper respiratory tract infection
occurred twice in a fourth subject; all were reported as mild in severity and no infection in a
subject with ALC <200 cells/µL was serious or led to study discontinuation. As described in
Section 16.3.2, the median time for peripheral blood lymphocytes to return to the normal range
was 2.6 weeks, with approximately 90% of subjects in the normal range within 4.7 weeks.
Neutropenia was generally similar between subjects who received etrasimod compared to
placebo, only 1 subject (0.3%) who received etrasimod had an ANC of <500 cells/µL versus none
in placebo and this subject experienced no TEAEs of infection.
Although the overall incidence of infections was greater in the etrasimod group compared to
placebo during APD334-301 and pooled data from APD334-302 and APD334-003, the difference
between the two groups was small (25% etrasimod vs. 22% placebo in APD334-301, and 14%
vs. 12% in pooled APD334-302 and APD334-003). Refer to Section 8.2.6.1 for further analysis of
infection risk with etrasimod.
Based on the available data and consistent with the mechanism of action of the drug,
lymphopenia, including severe lymphopenia (<200 cells/ µL), was observed in subjects who
received etrasimod. However, the overall rate of infection between etrasimod and placebo was
only slightly higher in etrasimod compared to placebo. Additionally, the majority of infections,
even for subjects with severe lymphopenia, were mild, not serious, and did not lead to study
discontinuation. Therefore, to address the risk of lymphopenia due to etrasimod, the Warnings
and Precautions section of the label will describe the reduction in peripheral blood lymphocyte
count and the risk of infection in subjects in APD334-301 and pooled studies APD334-302 and
Version date: October 12, 2018
Reference ID: 5259863

168

NDA/BLA Multi-Disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 216956
Velsipity (etrasimod)
APD334-003. The label will include recommendations to obtain a baseline complete blood
count, including lymphocyte count, and to consider interruption in treatment with etrasimod if
a patient develops a serious infection. This information in the label is similar to other S1P
receptor modulators.
8.2.6.3.

Bradyarrhythmia and Atrioventricular Conduction Delays

Cardiovascular events including bradycardia, AV conduction delays, and hypertension are
known class effects in S1P receptor modulators thought to be related to their effect on S1P
receptors found on atrial myocytes and smooth muscle cells (i.e., S1P1, S1P2, and S1P3).
Transient lowering of the heart rate and atrioventricular conduction delay were observed with
the first dose of etrasimod and when therapy was reinitiated and occurred more frequently in
subjects treated with etrasimod versus placebo. The largest heart rate reduction was observed
3 hours after the first etrasimod dose -7.2 (8.98) bpm compared to 0.4 (7.93) bpm in the
placebo group. These effects were self-limited and resolved within 8 hours without the need for
intervention. Refer to Section 6.3.1 for additional details regarding etrasimod’s heart rate
reduction effects.
The Applicant considered cardiovascular events as adverse events of special interest (AESIs) if
the preferred term met specific criteria, which was reviewed by a blinded medical reviewer to
determine whether the AE was considered an AESI. Refer to the description in Section 8.2.4.4
for details on the criteria used by the Applicant to define AESIs. 6
The review team analyzed the available safety data to characterize the potential risk of
bradyarrhythmia and AV conduction delay. These events were identified as those with
preferred terms bradycardia, sinus bradycardia, heart rate decreased, atrioventricular block
first degree, and atrioventricular block second degree. There was no requirement for a specific
HR threshold or associated clinical symptoms, which differs from the Applicant’s AESI analysis.
The incidence of cardiovascular events identified by the review team during APD334-301 and
APD334-302 and APD334-003 are shown in Table 65.

The review team considered that the risk of hypertension was sufficiently characterized by the AESI and TEAE
analyses performed for APD334-301 and pooled data from APD334-302 and APD334-003. Additional analyses in
this section were not performed. Refer to the respective sections for additional details regarding hypertension.

The review team’s broader criteria identified an increased number of subjects with bradycardia
in the etrasimod groups of APD334-301 and pooled APD334-302 and APD334-003 compared to
the number of subjects reported by the Applicant as AESIs. However, the overall trend between
the review team’s analysis and the Applicant’s AESI analysis was similar.
•

Bradycardia: During APD334-301, 4 subjects (1%) treated with etrasimod reported AEs
of bradycardia, 3 of which occurred on Study Day 1. One subject (<1%) treated with
etrasimod reported bradycardia on Study Day 2. In the placebo group, no AEs of
bradycardia were reported. During APD334-302 and APD334-003, 8 subjects (2.8%)
treated with etrasimod reported AEs of bradycardia, which all occurred on Study Day 1.
One subject (0.3%) treated with etrasimod reported bradycardia on Study Day 2 after
having reported bradycardia on Study Day 1. In the placebo group, no AEs of
bradycardia were reported.

As described previously, no bradycardia TEAEs were SAEs. A total of four bradycardia TEAEs led
to study discontinuation (1 during APD334-301 and 3 during APD334-302/APD334-003), which
are described in Sections 8.2.4.3 and 8.2.5.3.
•

AV Conduction Delay: During APD334-301, 2 subjects (1%) in the etrasimod group had
AV conduction delay (1 subject with first-degree AV block and 1 subject with seconddegree AV block, Mobitz type I) compared to none in placebo. Both events occurred on
Day 1. During APD334-302 and APD334-003, 2 subjects (1%) in the etrasimod group had
AV conduction delay (1 subject with first-degree AV block and 1 subject with seconddegree AV block, Mobitz type I) compared to none in placebo. Both events occurred on
Day 1.

As described previously, no AV conduction delays were SAEs. Two subjects (1 from APD334-301
and 1 from APD334-302/APD334-003) were discontinued due to TEAEs of AV conduction delay
(both first-degree AV blocks), which are described in Sections 8.2.4.3 and 8.2.5.3.

NDA/BLA Multi-Disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 216956
Velsipity (etrasimod)
In summary, treatment with etrasimod resulted in a transient decrease in heart rate and an
increased risk of AV conduction abnormalities (first-degree and second-degree [Mobitz Type I]
AV block). Bradycardia after the first dose is a known class effect of S1P receptor modulators.
Bradycardia and AV conduction delays were transient, mostly asymptomatic, and most
episodes resolved within 8 hours. Therefore, the Warnings and Precautions section of the label
will describe the risk of bradyarrhythmia and AV conduction delays, aligned with other currently
approved S1P receptor modulators.
8.2.6.4.

Hepatotoxicity

Liver injury has been reported in S1P receptor modulators and a Warnings and Precautions is
described in the labels of all approved S1P receptor modulators. The mechanism of liver injury
has not been well described; however, liver injury may be caused by a toxic or immunogenic
intermediate because of extensive metabolism in the liver. During the etrasimod development
program, the Applicant defined liver injury as an AESI for TEAEs that met certain criteria.
Overall, elevations in liver transaminases (AST and/or ALT) occurred more frequently in subjects
who received etrasimod compared to placebo. These criteria and AESI analyses are described in
detail in Section 8.2.4.4 and Section 8.2.5.4. The review team conducted additional analyses to
evaluate for Hy’s law cases and the risk of elevated liver tests not limited to the Applicant’s
definition of “hepatic injury.”
As shown in Figure 14, there were no Hy’s Law cases reported in the integrated summary of
safety, which included data from the “All UC Pool,” which included APD334-301, APD334-302,
and APD334-003; the open-label extension studies APD334-303 and APD334-005; and openlabel data from ES101002, APD334-301, APD334-302, APD334-003.

In APD334-301, elevations in ALT >3.0 x ULN, alkaline phosphatase >2.0 x ULN, and GGT
occurred at a higher frequency in the etrasimod group compared to placebo. Similarly, in
pooled data from APD334-302 and APD334-003, elevations in ALT >3.0 x ULN and GGT occurred
at a higher frequency in the etrasimod group compared to placebo. One (<1%) subject who
received etrasimod reported an SAE of hepatic enzymes increased during APD334-301 (refer to
Section 8.2.4.2). In APD334-301, the proportion of subjects that discontinued treatment due to
liver enzyme elevations (i.e., ALT and alkaline phosphatase) was balanced between the
etrasimod and placebo groups (refer to Section 8.2.4.3). In APD334-302 and APD334-003, there
were no SAEs related to increased hepatic enzymes and 1 (<1%) subject in the etrasimod group
discontinued treatment due to elevated AST and ALT compared to none in the placebo group
(refer to Section 8.2.5.3).
In summary, elevated liver tests was observed more frequently in the etrasimod group
compared to placebo but there were no Hy’s Law cases, and the majority were not serious or
led to study discontinuation. Additionally, most AEs that were either serious or led to study
discontinuation were considered unlikely to be related due to other underlying conditions (e.g.,
primary sclerosing cholangitis). However, elevations in liver enzymes and liver injury have been
reported in S1P receptor modulators and will be included in etrasimod’s Warnings and
Precautions section, which will describe the rate of ALT >3.0 x ULN, recommend testing liver
enzymes and bilirubin levels before initiation of treatment and if concern for liver injury is
present during treatment, and to discontinue etrasimod if liver injury is confirmed. Additionally,
elevated liver tests, which includes elevated ALT, AST, GGT, alkaline phosphatase, bilirubin,
transaminases, and liver function test, will be included in the Adverse Reactions section of the
label.
8.2.6.5.

Ophthalmologic Findings

To characterize the potential risk of macular edema and other ophthalmologic findings, the
review team analyzed the results of ophthalmologic examinations, including ophthalmoscopy,
optical coherence tomography (OCT), visual acuity measurements, and slit lamp examinations.
The analysis of the results of ophthalmologic evaluations were limited to APD334-301 because
it was the only completed, 52-week, randomized comparison of etrasimod versus placebo.
Version date: October 12, 2018
Reference ID: 5259863

173

NDA/BLA Multi-Disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 216956
Velsipity (etrasimod)
Additionally, the assessment of ophthalmologic findings was limited due to missing
ophthalmologic evaluations. Refer to the Ophthalmology Consult Review by Dr. Wiley
Chambers for full details of the review (DARRTS ID: 5106241).
Macular Edema
Macular edema has been reported in other S1P receptor modulators and observed during the
etrasimod development program. The mechanism by which S1P receptor modulators may
cause macular edema is not well described but may be due to effects on endothelial cells. The
Applicant defined macular edema as “an increase in CFT ≥ 40 µm and/or with associated
symptoms or clinically significant abnormal findings.” Based on this definition, 1 case of macular
edema in a subject who received etrasimod during APD334-301 was reported, which led to
study discontinuation. This AE of macular edema is described in detail in Section 8.2.4.4.
In subjects that had OCT macular thickness reported at baseline, 16/95 (17%) of subjects in the
etrasimod group compared to 2/26 (8%) in the placebo group had increases of ≥ 40 µm, which
was the predefined OCT cut-off by the Applicant for macular edema. The review team did not
consider a requirement for "associated symptoms” to be relevant because most cases of
macular edema are asymptomatic. Furthermore, a decrease in visual acuity due to macular
edema may not be noticed if it occurs in one eye.
On October 10, 2023, the Applicant submitted additional analyses of OCT findings, in which
they claimed they verified the CFT data by reviewing the case report forms (CRFs) and OCT
reports that were available for APD334-301. However, the Applicant did not submit the CRFs or
OCT reports and several reports were missing; therefore, the review team could not verify the
additional analyses. The analyses raised concerns about the integrity of the OCT data because
nearly half (i.e., 41.7%) were identified by the Applicant to be inaccurate readings of CFT. Due
to the data quality issues, the review team was unable to agree with the Applicant to report the
rate of macular edema in the proposed label.
Given an imbalance was observed in the originally submitted OCT analyses and S1P receptor
modulators are associated with macular edema, a Warnings and Precautions regarding macular
edema will describe the association of etrasimod with an increased risk of macular edema and
recommend that patients be evaluated near the start of therapy and periodically during
treatment for macular edema. Additionally, consideration to discontinue etrasimod if macular
edema develops will be included.
Decrease in Visual Acuity
The review team defined a decrease in visual acuity as a clinically significant decrease in visual
acuity (i.e., doubling of the visual angle). For subjects with a baseline and follow-up
examination, a decrease in visual acuity was reported in 4/156 (3%) of subjects who received
etrasimod compared to no subjects who received placebo. Based on these findings, the
decrease in visual acuity will be described in the Adverse Reactions section of the label.

Reference ID: 5259863

Velsipity (etrasimod)
8.2.6.6.

Respiratory Effects

S1P receptor modulators have been associated with changes to pulmonary function including
forced expiratory volume over 1 second (FEV1) and forced vial capacity (FVC). The Applicant
evaluated the risk of pulmonary function changes as AESIs as described in Sections 8.2.4.4 and
8.2.5.4. However, the review team conducted a separate analysis of the pulmonary function
data to better characterize the potential risk of pulmonary function changes. The analysis
focused on data from APD334-301 and APD334-302 because pulmonary function testing was
not performed during APD334-003. Refer to the Pulmonology Consult Review by Dr. Rekha
Jhamnani for full details of the review (DARRTS ID: 5186355)
During APD334-301 and APD334-302, there were no respiratory SAEs, and no respiratory TEAEs
that led to treatment interruption or discontinuation in either study. In Study APD334-301 the
mean difference in change from baseline in FEV1 is shown in Table 67. A greater decrease in
FEV1 was observed in the etrasimod group compared to placebo at Week 12, which was
nominally significant. A difference was not present at Week 52; however, there was significant
dropout in both the etrasimod and placebo groups between Week 12 and Week 52 that limited
the interpretation of the Week 52 results. No difference was observed between etrasimod and
placebo for FVC (not shown). Asthma and chronic obstructive pulmonary disease (COPD)
subjects were included in the study, but the small sample size limited conclusions on pulmonary
function effects in this population.

In Study APD334-302, there were no pulmonary function tests that were significantly different

were included in the study, but the small sample size limited conclusions on pulmonary

Based on these findings the Warnings and Precautions section and the Clinical Pharmacology
section of the label will reflect the observed changes in FEV1.
Safety Results for Integrated Summary of Safety
The Applicant submitted an integrated summary of safety (ISS), which was an analysis that
pooled data from controlled and uncontrolled studies of subjects with UC. The dataset included
APD334-301, APD334-302, and APD334-003 as well as the open-label extension (OLE) studies
APD334-303, APD334-005, and ES101002. Refer to Section 7.1 for details regarding the studies
included in the ISS. Given that much of the data was uncontrolled, safety information was
considered supportive, and the review team focused on the assessment for rare events, given
the extended duration of the open-label studies. Although etrasimod 1 mg was evaluated in
APD334-003, the review team focused on safety assessments for etrasimod 2 mg because there
were a limited number of subjects who received etrasimod 1 mg (i.e., 42 subjects for 12 weeks)
and there were no additional safety findings for the etrasimod 1 mg group.
Table 68 shows the duration of exposure to etrasimod 2 mg in the overall safety database used
for the primary assessment of safety and ISS.

Deaths

One death was reported in the ISS from APD334-303 due to an SAE of “neuroendocrine tumor
of unknown primary site.” The subject was a 33-year-old White male who was enrolled in
APD334-301 where he received placebo. He was subsequently enrolled in APD334-303 where
he received open-label etrasimod 2 mg for a total of 24 weeks. On Study Day 170, etrasimod 2
mg was stopped due to lack of efficacy. The subject was subsequently started on IV
methylprednisolone (Study Day 171) followed by vedolizumab and rectal mesalazine (Study Day
185) for the treatment of his ulcerative colitis. The subject’s last study visit was conducted on
Study Day 189. However, on Study Day 194 the subject had an unscheduled visit for a
laboratory abnormality (GGT, not reported) which prompted an abdominal ultrasound (US). At
Version date: October 12, 2018
Reference ID: 5259863

176

NDA/BLA Multi-Disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 216956
Velsipity (etrasimod)
the time, the subject reported abdominal pain with cramping as well as bloating, nausea,
vomiting, and loose stool. The abdominal US showed a large number of abdominal lymph nodes
and liver lesions. Further imaging identified multiple lesions in the liver and bones (vertebrae,
pelvis, sternum, and femur). Upon further testing, the subject was diagnosed with poorly
differentiated neuroendocrine carcinoma of unknown origin. The subject initiated treatment on
Study Day 207 for the neuroendocrine carcinoma; however, the subject died on Study Day 212.
The Investigator and Sponsor assessed the event of neuroendocrine tumor as unlikely to be
related to study treatment. Given the short duration of exposure to etrasimod, the review team
assessed the event as unlikely to be related. S1P receptor modulators have been associated
with cases of malignancies. Therefore, the label will include a Warnings and Precautions
regarding the risk of malignancies.
8.2.7.2.

Serious Adverse Events

In the ISS, there were 50 subjects who received etrasimod 2 mg and reported at least 1 SAE that
was not previously reported as an SAE during their parent study. There were 41 unique reports
of SAEs that were not previously reported during APD334-301, APD334-302, and APD334-003.
Most SAEs were reported by 1 subject. The only SAEs that were reported by more than 1
subject included: ulcerative colitis, COVID-19, anemia, gastroenteritis, iron deficiency anemia,
migraine, and transient ischemic attack. One SAE led to death, which is described in the
previous section. SAEs that led to study discontinuation were reported by 12 subjects and were
not previously reported in the parent studies. These included atrial fibrillation (1), ulcerative
colitis (9), herpes simplex meningitis (1), and transient ischemic attack (1). SAEs (other than
ulcerative colitis) that led to study discontinuation are described below.
•

Herpes simplex meningitis: An SAE of herpes simplex (HSV) meningitis was reported by
a 66-year-old White male who had received placebo treatment in parent study APD334301 and then received etrasimod 2 mg up to Study Day 12 of APD334-303. On Study Day
7, the subject reported symptoms of headache, nausea, blurry vision, and
hypoaesthesia. On Study Day 9, the subject presented to the emergency room (ER) with
similar complaints. In the ER, chest X-ray and head/brain CT were normal; however, a
lumbar puncture and examination of cerebrospinal fluid (CSF) showed lymphocytes and
CSF HSV 1/2 IgG mildly elevated (which may indicate past or recent HSV infection). Of
note, HSV 1/2 PCR was not detected. Brain MRI and cervical spine CT did not show
evidence to suggest HSV encephalitis or meningitis. In consultation with an infectious
disease specialist, the subject received acyclovir. The Investigator discontinued
etrasimod due to the SAE of HSV meningitis on Study Day 18 and the last dose of study
treatment was taken on Study Day 12. The Investigator and Applicant assessed the SAE
as unlikely to be related to study treatment and due to the open-label design of the
study the results are difficult to interpret. However, etrasimod may increase the
susceptibility to infections, and herpes viral infections have been reported with S1P
receptor modulators. Therefore, the label will include a Warnings and Precautions
regarding the risk of infections, including herpes viral infections.

Version date: October 12, 2018
Reference ID: 5259863

177

NDA/BLA Multi-Disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 216956
Velsipity (etrasimod)
•

Atrial fibrillation: During APD334-005, a 69-year-old White male who received placebo
in APD334-003 presented on Study Day 197 with complaints of chest pain and shortness
of breath. The subject had a past medical history of obesity and hyperglycemia. ECG
results were considered abnormal with atrial fibrillation with controlled ventricular
response, and the subject was hospitalized. Cardioversion was performed successfully,
and the subject was discharged from the hospital on Study Day 198. Etrasimod was
permanently withdrawn due to the AE of atrial fibrillation. The Investigator and
Applicant reported the AE as not related to etrasimod. Atrial fibrillation is not a known
risk of S1P receptor modulators, and the effects of S1P receptor modulators on the
cardiovascular system have been related to bradyarrhythmia, AV conduction delays, and
hypertension. The label will include a Warnings and Precautions regarding the risk of
bradycardia and AV conduction delay.

•

Transient ischemic attack (TIA): A 54-year-old male with a history of hypertension was
hospitalized for Grade 2 TIA on Study Day 18 of APD334-303. The subject received
etrasimod 2 mg in parent study APD334-301 prior to enrollment in APD334-303. On
Study Day 15, the subject went to the hospital with symptoms suspicious for a TIA.
Concomitant medications included sulfasalazine, nebivolol, and barnidipine. On Study
Day 22, brain magnetic resonance imaging and magnetic resonance angiography did not
show any acute cerebrovascular lesions of recent significance and the event was
considered recovered/resolved. The subject withdrew from the study and study
treatment (etrasimod) was discontinued due to an SAE of moderate TIA. TIA is not a
known risk of S1P receptor modulators, and the results are difficult to interpret due to
the open-label study design. However, hypertension was observed in the etrasimod
development program and, therefore, a Warnings and Precautions regarding
hypertension will be included in the label.

Overall, the unique SAEs reported in the ISS did not identify a new safety signal. Most subjects
had a past medical history that may have contributed to the SAE. The majority of SAEs were
reported by one subject. Additionally, due to the open-label nature of the extension studies
(from which, unique subjects were identified), the safety information is challenging to interpret
without a comparator arm.
8.2.7.3.

Adverse Events that Led to Study Discontinuation

In the ISS, there were 21 subjects treated with etrasimod 2 mg who reported TEAEs that led to
study discontinuation. TEAEs that led to study discontinuation and occurred in more than 2
subjects were ulcerative colitis, lymphopenia, and headache. TEAEs that led to study
discontinuation that were also SAEs (other than ulcerative colitis) are described in the previous
section. There were no TEAEs that led to study discontinuation during the extension studies
APD334-303, APD334-005, and ES101002 that were considered AESIs. Overall, the TEAEs that
led to study discontinuation in the ISS were similar to APD334-301, APD334-302, and APD334003.

Velsipity (etrasimod)
8.2.7.4.

Treatment Emergent Adverse Events of Special Interest

The ISS was reviewed for AESIs that have been previously described in Section 8.2.4.4. No TEAEs
of PML, respiratory disorders, or PRES were reported in the ISS. Pertinent TEAEs corresponding
to the AESI categories previously described, are noted below.
•

Bradyarrhythmia, AV Conduction Delay, and Hypertension: TEAEs of AV conduction
delay (AV block first degree and AV block second degree) were reported in 3 subjects in
the ISS that were not reported during the parent studies. All 3 subjects were switched
from placebo to etrasimod 2 mg during either APD334-303, APD334-005, or ES101002.
Additionally, all 3 subjects reported the AESI on the first day of treatment with
etrasimod 2 mg, which is consistent with TEAEs observed in APD334-301, APD334-302,
and APD334-003. No new subjects reported AESIs of bradycardia in the ISS. Two
additional subjects reported a TEAE of hypertension in the ISS; however, neither TEAEs
were SAEs nor led to study discontinuation.

•

Ophthalmologic Findings: One additional subject reported a TEAE of “cystoid macular
oedema” in the ISS that was not previously reported in APD334-301, APD334-302, or
APD334-003. The subject was a 58-year-old White woman who received placebo in
parent study APD334-301, and etrasimod 2 mg up to study day 248 in APD334-303. On
Study Day 85, the subject had a moderate AESI of “cystoid macular oedema” according
to findings from retinal photographs, which were reported as abnormal and clinically
significant for the left eye. On Study Day 120, the event was considered
recovered/resolved. A dilated funduscopic examination on this day was reported as
abnormal clinically significant for epiretinal fibrosis and mild hyperopia; remaining
structures in that examination were deemed normal at that time. On Study Day 169,
OCT and retinal photographic findings were reported as abnormal clinically significant
for paramacular retinal edema of the left eye. On Study Day 248, the subject had a
moderate AESI of cystoid macular edema. There were no ophthalmic examination
findings reported from this day. Study drug was discontinued due to the event with the
last dose on Study Day 248. At the time of the subject’s last available report, the
outcome of the event of cystoid macular edema was unknown.

•

Hepatotoxicity: There were no TEAEs that met Hy’s law criteria during the ISS. There
were 27 additional subjects who reported TEAEs related to elevated liver tests
(preferred terms: ALT increased, AST increased, GGT increased, alkaline phosphatase
increased, elevated liver enzymes, transaminases increased, and bilirubin increased).
The majority of TEAEs were mild or moderate in severity and no subjects were
discontinued from the study due to the TEAEs of elevated liver enzymes.

•

Infections: In the ISS, there were 8 severe infections in subjects that received etrasimod
2 mg, which included COVID-19 (3), pneumonia (2), appendicitis (1), gastroenteritis (1),
and pyelonephritis (1). Two subjects who received etrasimod 2 mg reported TEAEs that
led to study discontinuation that had not been reported previously, herpes simplex

Version date: October 12, 2018
Reference ID: 5259863

179

NDA/BLA Multi-Disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 216956
Velsipity (etrasimod)
meningitis (described in Section 8.2.7.2) and latent tuberculosis (TB). The subject with
latent tuberculosis had been screened with a “tuberculosis test” and chest X-ray during
screening for APD334-301; however, an Interferon Gamma Release Assay was
performed on Study Day 385, prior to initiating etrasimod 2 mg for APD334-303, which
was positive. The subject was asymptomatic and was treated for latent TB but was
discontinued from the study due to the AE of latent TB. Overall, the types of infectionrelated TEAEs reported in the ISS were similar to those described in APD334-301,
APD334-302, and APD334-003.
•

Malignancy: Two subjects reported one TEAE of malignancy in the ISS, which were not
previously reported. One subject that received etrasimod 2 mg in parent study APD334301 and continued on etrasimod 2 mg in APD334-303, reported a TEAE of “malignant
melanoma” from the verbatim reported term of “Melanocytic mole manifestation.”
However, this TEAE was incorrectly coded and was corrected to the term melanocytic
nevus (i.e., not a malignancy). The second TEAE of malignancy was a neuroendocrine
tumor resulting in death, which is described in Section 8.2.7.1.
8.2.7.5.

Treatment Emergent Adverse Events

The TEAEs reported in the ISS were generally similar to those reported in APD334-301, APD334302, and APD334-003. The majority of TEAEs were mild or moderate in severity. The most
common TEAEs in the ISS (reported by at least 10 subjects who received etrasimod 2 mg) were
ulcerative colitis, COVID-19, lymphopenia, headache, upper respiratory tract infection, and GGT
increased.
8.2.7.6.

Summary of Integrated Summary of Safety

Overall, safety data from the ISS did not identify any new safety signals. The reported AEs were
generally similar to those in the placebo-controlled studies APD334-301, APD334-302, and
APD334-003; however, interpretation of the results of the ISS is limited by the lack of a
comparator arm and the open-label nature of APD334-303, APD334-005, and ES101002.

Overall, there were no notable differences between the treatment groups within the
demographic subgroups. Although no discernable differences were observed, the
interpretability of subgroup analyses were limited due to the small number of non-White
subjects, subjects aged 65 years of age or older, and Hispanic or Latino subjects.
Specific Safety Studies/Clinical Trials
No specific safety studies or clinical trials were performed.
120-Day Safety Update Report
A 120-day safety update report (SUR) was provided by the Applicant that incorporated safety
information and additional integrated cumulative clinical study safety data from February 1,
2022, to August 30, 2022. The safety update includes cumulative safety data from ongoing
studies in subjects with UC (APD334-303 and ES101002), as well as from other indications
APD334-202 (a phase 2, 14-Week induction study in subjects with Crohn’s disease [CD]), and
APD334-205 (a phase 2, 28-week OLE study in subjects with alopecia areata). The review team
focused on the data available in subjects with UC to evaluate for rare events or events that may
have a longer latency period.
As shown in Table 73, during the SUR, an additional 221 subjects had at least 52 weeks of
exposure and 90 subjects had at least 104 weeks of exposure to etrasimod 2 mg as compared
to the ISS.

Deaths
There were no additional deaths reported during the SUR.
Serious Adverse Events
SAEs were reported in an additional 27 subjects with UC in the SUR. The most frequent SAEs
were ulcerative colitis (9) and COVID-19/COVID-19 pneumonia (2). No other SAEs were
reported in more than a single subject during the SUR.
Discontinuation Due to Adverse Events
Twenty-five additional subjects discontinued study treatment due to an AE during the SUR. The
most frequently reported TEAEs occurring in ≥ 2 subjects in the etrasimod 2 mg group that led
to permanent study treatment discontinuation were colitis ulcerative (13), lymphocyte count
decreased (3), and weight decreased (1).
Treatment Emergent Adverse Events of Special Interest
The SUR included AESIs that have been previously described in Section 8.2.4.4. No TEAEs of
PML, respiratory disorders, or PRES were reported in the SUR. Pertinent TEAEs corresponding
to the AESI categories previously described, are noted below.
Bradyarrhythmia, AV Conduction Delay, and Hypertension
•

Bradycardia: Three additional subjects not previously reported in the ISS, experienced
TEAEs of sinus bradycardia on Day 1. All sinus bradycardia TEAEs were asymptomatic and
none of these subjects experienced an HR < 50 bpm.

•

AV Conduction Delay: No additional subjects with UC reported TEAEs related to AV
conduction delay.

•

Acute myocardial infarction: One subject that received etrasimod 2 mg experienced an SAE
of acute myocardial infarction Grade 3 on Day 27 of ES101002. Prior to receiving open-label
etrasimod 2 mg in ES101002, the subject received blinded treatment in the double-blind
period of the study. Medical history included alcohol and tobacco use. Study treatment was
discontinued due to the AE of acute myocardial infarction.

•

Hypertension: TEAEs of hypertension were reported in 8 additional subjects during the SUR.
None of the TEAEs were SAEs or led to study discontinuation.

Reference ID: 5259863

NDA/BLA Multi-Disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 216956
Velsipity (etrasimod)
Infections and Opportunistic Infections
The most common Infection TEAEs reported by subjects treated with etrasimod 2 mg during
the SUR were COVID-19, upper respiratory tract infection, nasopharyngitis, urinary tract
infection, bronchitis, and gastroenteritis. There were no additional TEAEs of PML, cryptococcus,
nasopharyngitis, urinary tract infection, bronchitis, or gastroenteritis that occurred in UC
subjects during the SUR.
•

Opportunistic Infections: One subject with UC that received etrasimod 2 mg reported an
TEAE of opportunistic infection of esophageal candidiasis on Study Day 42. The event was
preceded by an SAE of rectal hemorrhage and study treatment withdrawal on Study Day 35
prior to SDEI onset.

•

Severe Infections: Six additional subjects in the etrasimod 2 mg group reported an TEAE of
severe infection. The types of infections were varied. Previously unreported serious
infection PTs included bacterial superinfection, anal abscess, colonic abscess, diverticulitis,
and vestibular neuronitis.

•

Herpes Viral Infections: One additional subject each in the etrasimod 2 mg group
experienced TEAEs oral herpes and herpes zoster. Both TEAEs were not SAEs and did not
lead to study discontinuation.

•

Lymphopenia: During the SUR, 108 UC subjects treated with etrasimod 2 mg reported
TEAEs of lymphopenia (PTs: lymphopenia, lymphocyte count, lymphocyte percentage
decreased, and CD4 lymphocytes decreased). Three subjects were discontinued from
treatment due to the TEAEs of decreased lymphocyte count.

Hepatic Injury
Twelve additional subjects experienced TEAEs related to hepatic injury. TEAEs reported by > 1
subject were: hepatic function abnormal (6) and cholangitis sclerosing (2). One subject in the
etrasimod 2 mg group reported 1 SAE of cholelithiasis that led to temporary interruption of
study treatment. There were no AEs that led to study treatment discontinuation and no TEAEs
that met Hy’s law criteria.
Treatment Emergent Adverse Events
The most frequently reported TEAEs during the SUR were colitis ulcerative, COVID-19,
lymphopenia, anemia, lymphocyte count decreased, and headache. These TEAEs are similar to
those reported in the placebo-controlled studies APD334-301, APD334-302, and APD334-003 as
well as the ISS.
Summary of the 120-Day Safety Report
The SUR did not identify any new safety signals; however, the interpretation of data from the
SUR is limited due to the lack of a comparator arm. Overall, similar TEAEs were reported,

consistent with data from the primary safety analysis, performed with placebo-controlled data
from APD334-301, APD334-302, and APD334-003.
Human Reproduction and Pregnancy
Pregnancy
Pregnant women were excluded from clinical trials with etrasimod during the clinical
development program. At the time of the SUR a total of 11 pregnancy exposure cases were
reported with outcomes including: normal livebirth (2), preterm birth (1), ongoing (6), elective
abortion (1), and spontaneous abortion (1). Overall, these limited available human data are
insufficient to evaluate for a drug-associated risk of major birth defects, miscarriage, or other
adverse maternal or fetal outcomes.
Etrasimod is indicated for a condition that would be expected to be seen in females of
reproductive potential (including during pregnancy), and current data are insufficient to inform
women regarding etrasimod use during pregnancy. Therefore, labeling will include that based
on findings from animal studies, etrasimod may cause fetal harm when administered to a
pregnant woman. In addition, postmarketing requirements will be issued for the Applicant to
conduct a pregnancy exposure registry and a complimentary study of a different design (such as
a claims database study). Language regarding the pregnancy exposure registry should be added
to Subsection 8.1 and Section 17 of labeling once the study protocol has been finalized and
registry contact information established.
Lactation
Lactating women were excluded from clinical trials with etrasimod. At the time of the 120-day
SUR, no lactation exposure cases were reported. Etrasimod is indicated for a condition that
would be expected to be seen in females of reproductive potential (including during lactation),
and there are no current data available to inform women regarding etrasimod use during
lactation. Therefore, a PMR will be issued for the Applicant to conduct a clinical lactation study
with etrasimod to inform labeling.
Females and Males of Reproductive Potential
There are no available human data on the effects of etrasimod on male or female fertility.
Based on animal data, etrasimod may cause fetal harm when administered to pregnant women.
Therefore, labeling will include that females of reproductive potential should be counseled on
the potential for a serious risk to the fetus and the need for effective contraception during
treatment with etrasimod and for one week following the last dose.
Pediatrics and Assessment of Effects on Growth
Insufficient pediatric data were submitted as part of this BLA to support an indication in
pediatric subjects or to inform an assessment of the impact of etrasimod on growth. At the

Overdose, Drug Abuse Potential, Withdrawal, and Rebound
There are no specific concerns about drug abuse potential or withdrawal with etrasimod use in
UC.
Safety in the Postmarket Setting
Safety Concerns Identified Through Postmarket Experience
No postmarketing experience is available because etrasimod is not currently approved in any
country.
Expectations on Safety in the Postmarket Setting
Refer to Section 13 for details regarding postmarketing requirements and commitments agreed
upon with the Applicant.

Conclusions and Recommendations
The Applicant submitted the results of two adequate and well-controlled phase 3 trials
(APD334-301 and APD334-302) to establish the substantial evidence of effectiveness. The
review team agreed with the Applicant’s conclusion that a clinically meaningful and statistically
significant benefit was demonstrated on the primary endpoints and multiple secondary
endpoints for APD334-301 and APD334-302.
The Applicant submitted data from the 52-week phase 3 trial APD334-301, the 12-week phase 3
trial APD334-302, and 12-week phase 2 trial APD334-003 to support the safety of etrasimod 2
mg in subjects with moderately to severely active UC. The safety profile of etrasimod 2 mg once
daily for up to 52 weeks was acceptable and supports approval of the proposed dosing regimen.
There are several known potential risks related to the S1P receptor modulator class which were
observed in the etrasimod development program (i.e., infections, bradyarrhythmia and
atrioventricular conduction delays, liver injury, macular edema, increased blood pressure, and
respiratory effects), which were consistent with other S1P receptor modulators. The labeling is
considered adequate to communicate these risks and provide appropriate risk mitigation.

A Medication Guide (MG) was proposed by the Applicant and deemed necessary by the review

adverse reactions. MGs are required for all other drugs in the S1P receptor modulator class.

13 Postmarketing Requirements and Commitment
Under the Pediatric Research Equity Act (PREA) (21 U.S.C. 355c), all applications for new active
ingredients (which includes new salts and new fixed-combinations), new indications, new
dosage forms, new dosing regimens, or new routes of administration are required to contain an
assessment of the safety and effectiveness of the product for the claimed indication in pediatric
patients unless this requirement is waived, deferred, or inapplicable. NDA 216956 triggers PREA
because etrasimod is a new active ingredient. The following PREA PMRs will be issued.
4499-1

Complete the ongoing phase 2 study to evaluate the safety, efficacy,
pharmacokinetics, and pharmacodynamics of Velsipity (etrasimod) in pediatric
patients 12 to less than 18 years of age with moderately to severely active
ulcerative colitis.

May 2026

years of age with moderately to severely active ulcerative colitis.

14 Division Director (Clinical) Comments
I concur with the recommendation of the review team to approve NDA for Velsipity (etrasimod)
for the treatment of moderately to severely active ulcerative colitis (UC) in adults. Etrasimod, a
new molecular entity (NME), is a sphingosine 1-phosphate (S1P) receptor modulator that binds
with high affinity to S1P receptors 1, 4, and 5. Other S1P modulators have been approved for
UC, multiple sclerosis, psoriasis, and graft-versus-host disease. The recommended dosage is 2
mg once daily.
As discussed in detail in this multi-disciplinary review, the benefits of etrasimod outweigh the
risks for the indication sought. The NDA submission included the results of two adequate and
well-controlled phase 3 trials (APD334-301 and APD334-302), which formed the basis of the
substantial evidence of effectiveness. The co-primary endpoints of clinical remission at Week 12
and Week 52 in APD334-301 and clinical remission at Week 12 in APD334-301, all assessed by

Version date: October 12, 2018
Reference ID: 5259863

198

NDA/BLA Multi-Disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 216956
Velsipity (etrasimod)
modified Mayo score, achieved statistical significance. Secondary endpoints also supported the
primary efficacy analyses in both trials.
The safety profile was generally consistent with the known adverse event profile for S1P
receptor modulators. Some of the adverse events of special interest, such as infections
including herpes zoster, liver injury, and malignances, are shared across multiple drugs used to
treat UC; however, other observed or potential risks, such as bradyarrhythmia and
atrioventricular conduction delays, macular edema, fetal risk, and respiratory effects, are
unique to this drug class. The most common adverse reactions reported in clinical trials of
patients with UC include headache, elevated liver tests, and dizziness. Product Labeling
(including Contraindications and Warnings and Precautions) and Medication Guide are
adequate to communicate these known and potential risks to healthcare providers and
patients, respectively. A REMS will not be required.
Postmarketing required studies, under Section 505(o)(3) of the Federal Food, Drug, and
Cosmetic Act, will assess the pregnancy outcomes following etrasimod exposure during
pregnancy and the effect of etrasimod on lactation. In addition, the Applicant agreed to
conduct postmarketing required studies, under the Pediatric Research Equity Act, to assess the
safety, efficacy, pharmacokinetics, and pharmacodynamics of etrasimod in pediatric patients 12
to 17 years of age with moderately to severely active UC; and the safety, efficacy,
pharmacokinetics, and pharmacodynamics of etrasimod in pediatric patients 2 to 17 years of
age with moderately to severely active UC.

15 Office Director (or Designated Signatory Authority) Comments
I concur with the review team’s assessment of the application, labeling recommendations, and
the recommendation to approve etrasimod, a sphingosine 1-phosphate (S1P) receptor
modulator, for the treatment of moderately to severely active ulcerative colitis (UC) in adults,
as detailed in this document.
The regulatory action is Approval of NDA 216956 for Velsipity (etrasimod) for the treatment of
moderately to severely active ulcerative colitis (UC) in adults.
A REMS is not warranted at this time. Postmarketing required (PMR) studies will include those
required under PREA, as well as pregnancy and lactation studies, as detailed in Section 13.

Reference ID: 5259863

The final PopPK dataset included a total of 16,205 quantifiable etrasimod plasma
concentrations from 1079 subjects.
The basic PopPK structure model consisted of a two-compartment model with sequential zeroand first-order absorption and first-order elimination as shown in Figure 15.
Figure 15. Schematic Representation of the Structural Population Pharmacokinetic Model of

The base model also included fixed weight effects on clearance and volume parameters:

Additionally, for covariate analyses, age, sex, patient status, ethnicity (Japanese, Chinese, or
other ethnicities), race (Black or African American versus other races), baseline bilirubin, eGFR,
tobacco use, and formulations were tested for their potential influence on the apparent
clearance, volume of distribution, bioavailability, first-order absorption rate constant (ka), and
zero-order absorption duration (D1) of etrasimod.
Briefly, a covariate modeling approach emphasizing parameter estimation rather than stepwise
hypothesis testing was implemented as the primary covariate analysis methodology. A full
model was constructed to avoid correlation or collinearity in predictors (covariates with
correlation coefficients > |0.6| were not simultaneously included as potential predictors).
Population parameters, including fixed effects parameters (covariate coefficients and structural
model parameters), and random effects parameters were estimated. An exploratory
assessment of any remaining trends was conducted by graphical inspection of all covariate
effects.
The final PopPK model that best described etrasimod PK was a two-compartment model with
sequential zero- and first-order absorption. The PK parameter estimates from the final PopPK
model are summarized in Table 78, Table 79, Table 80.
Table 78. Summary of Structural Parameter Estimates for the Final PopPK Model

PopPK estimates that healthy subjects have slightly smaller (within 10%) mean etrasimod Cmax,ss
and AUCss compared to reference UC patient of the same body weight, following 2 mg QD
dosing. Hence, disease status is not expected to have a clinically significant effect on etrasimod
PK exposure in subjects with UC.
Effect of Body Weight on PK
Body weight was identified as a statistically significant covariate on both clearance and volume
for etrasimod. Etrasimod exposures are higher in subjects with lower body weight and lower in
subjects with higher body weight. Based on popPK analysis, the predicted Cmax,ss, AUCss, and
Ctrough,ss values for etrasimod in a typical 50 kg subject are 125, 123, and 120%, respectively, of
those from a reference 70 kg subject. The predicted Cmax,ss, AUCss, and Ctrough,ss values for
etrasimod in a typical 110 kg subject are 74.1, 75.7, and 77.9%, respectively, of those from a
reference 70 kg subject. Hence, the effect of body weight on PK is not considered to be
clinically significant to warrant dose adjustment across the typical body weight range in adults.
Smoking Status
Population PK analysis estimated that steady state PK estimates (Cmax,ss, AUCss, and Ctrough,ss) of
etrasimod in a typical tobacco user are 99.1, 98.8, and 98.5%, respectively, of those from a
reference male non-user of tobacco of the same body weight.
In addition, PopPK estimates that there are no meaningful differences in the PK of etrasimod
based on sex, age, race, or ethnicity in adults.
Exposure-Response Analysis
Population PK-PD analyses were conducted to characterize the exposure-response (E-R)
relationships between etrasimod and lymphocyte count, clinical efficacy endpoints (clinical
remission, endoscopic improvement, symptomatic remission, and mucosal healing at Week 12),
and heart rate.
The Applicant conducted population E-R analyses using data from 15 phase 1 studies in adult
healthy volunteers and adults with hepatic or renal impairment, two phase 2 studies in adult
patients with UC, and two phase 3 studies in adult patients with UC. Post hoc estimates of PK
parameters for each subject from the final PopPK model were included in the PK-PD analysis.
Lymphocyte Count
Exploratory graphical evaluations of the observed lymphocyte count versus time and
relationships between exposure and response were initially performed prior to analysis (Figure
17). An indirect response model with a circadian rhythm for lymphocyte egress over a 24-hour
period was used to describe the etrasimod and lymphocyte relationship.

Heart Rate
An E-R analysis for heart rate was conducted using heart rate data obtained from 12-lead safety
ECG and Holter monitor measurements in two phase 1 studies APD334-008 (QT study) and
APD334-110 (cardiodynamic effect study). Post hoc estimates of PK parameters from the final
PopPK model were included in the analysis.
Exploratory graphical evaluations of the observed heart rate versus time and relationships
between exposure and response (e.g., heart rate nadirs) were performed prior to analysis.
Initial modeling was conducted based on a direct effect of etrasimod concentration on heart
rate with circadian rhythm and etrasimod-dependent tolerance. Subsequently, other simplified
structures for the tolerance mechanism were evaluated in addition to the combination of other
circadian rhythms and placebo effects. In the final model (Figure 21), etrasimod concentrations
were assumed to decrease heart rate via a direct inhibitory Emax model with a maximum effect
that decreased with increasing tolerance.

Overall, visual inspection indicates that the PK-PD model for heart rate is generally predictive
for the central tendency of heart rate since the median of the observed lymphocyte are
generally within the 90% CI of the simulated data (Figure 22).

Simulations were performed to compare the predicted heart rate in subjects with UC following
