and 2, 0.5 mg on Day 3, 0.75 mg on Day 4, 1.25 mg on Day 5, and 2 mg QD on Days 6-21). The
model estimated that the extent of maximal HR reduction was generally similar between
regimens with and without dose titration although the time to maximal HR reduction was
delayed with the regimen with dose titration (reached after 5 days of treatment) compared to
that following 2 mg QD without titration (reached on Day 1) (Figure 3).
Drug Interaction Studies
Is Etrasimod a Substrate of CYP Enzymes?
Yes. In vitro studies (Study XT164054 and Study APD334.DMPK.010) indicated that CYP2C8,
CYP2C9, and CYP3A4 are the major CYPs involved in the metabolism of etrasimod with fraction
metabolized of 0.38, 0.37 and 0.22, respectively, with CYP2C19 and CYP2J2 as minor
contributors of 0.01.
Identification of In Vitro Metabolites

In vitro study (MPR-11-005) using human liver microsomes identified five metabolites, M1, M2,
M3, M4, and M5. Metabolite M1 was an acyl-glucuronide of etrasimod whereas M2 and M3
were 2 mono-hydroxyl metabolites. M2 was the major metabolite produced by rCYP2C8 and
rCYP2C19 whereas M3 was primarily produced by CYP2C9*1 (MPR-16-002). M2 and M3 were
further metabolized to glucuronide conjugates, M4 and M5, respectively.
In in vitro study (MPR-16-003), 18 metabolites of etrasimod were identified in human
hepatocytes following incubation with [14C]etrasimod (20 μM). Among them, the hydroxyl (M3)
and ketone (M6) metabolites were the most abundant at 4.3% and 9.0% of total radioactivity;
Version date: October 12, 2018
Reference ID: 5259863

215

NDA/BLA Multi-Disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 216956
Velsipity (etrasimod)
while all other metabolites formed were less than 4% of total radioactivity, including the
glucuronide metabolite M1 and other metabolites that included combination of oxidation and
glucuronidation.
Identification of Human CYP Enzymes Involved in the Metabolism of Etrasimod

Based on in vitro study (MPR-16-002) using recombinant human CYP enzymes, CYP2C9*1,
CYP2C19, and CYP2C8 were the primary enzymes involved in the oxidative metabolism of
etrasimod. M2 was the major metabolite produced by rCYP2C8 and rCYP2C19 whereas M3 was
primarily produced by CYP2C9*1; and M6 was a minor metabolite of all three rCYPs.
Effect of Other Drugs on Etrasimod PK

Three clinical DDI studies (APD334-009, APD334-111 and APD334-116) were conducted and see
Section 6.3.2 above for details.
Concomitant Inducers
In a clinical DDI study (APD334-009), rifampin (a strong inducer of CYP3A4 and CYP2C19, and a
moderate inducer of CYP2C8 and CYP2C9) reduced AUC of etrasimod by 49%.
Concomitant Inhibitors
In a clinical DDI study (APD334-111), fluconazole (a moderate inhibitor of CYP2C9 and CYP3A4,
and a strong inhibitor of CYP2C19) increased AUC of etrasimod by up to 84%.
In a clinical drug interaction study (APD334-116, co-administration of itraconazole (a strong
inhibitor of CYP3A4) 200 mg QD for 13 days with a single dose of etrasimod 1 mg had no or
minimum impact on the mean Cmax (6% increase) but resulted in an increase of 32% in mean
AUC0-inf of etrasimod.
Is Etrasimod an Inhibitor and/or an Inducer of CYP Enzymes?
The ability of etrasimod to inhibit or induce the activity of metabolic enzymes was investigated
in vitro in HLM (inhibition), rUGTs (inhibition), and human hepatocytes (induction only).
CYP Enzyme/UGT Inhibition
•

Etrasimod did not inhibit human CYPs or UGTs (see comments below for details).

•

Two in vitro studies (Study MPR-11-004; Study XT165088) were conducted to evaluate the
potential of etrasimod to inhibit CYP1A2, CYP2B6, CYP2C8, CYP2C9, CYP2C19, CYP2D6, or
CYP3A4/5. Activity was assessed at concentrations of etrasimod up to 50 μM using selective
probe substrates. There was little or no direct or time dependent inhibition by etrasimod
(10 µM) with any of the CYP enzymes evaluated, except for CYP2C8. Etrasimod reversibly
inhibited CYP2C8 with an IC50 of 4.4 μM (amodiaquine as the in vitro substrate). However,

Version date: October 12, 2018
Reference ID: 5259863

216

NDA/BLA Multi-Disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 216956
Velsipity (etrasimod)
the calculated AUCR (ratio of area under the concentration-time curve) was < 1.25 (using
repaglinide as the sensitive clinical CYP2C8 substrate).
•

Etrasimod also reversibly inhibited CYP2C8 with an IC50 value of 34.7 μM in the study with
paclitaxel as a probe substrate (Study MPR-11-004). However, the calculated AUCR value of
1.14 for CYP2C8 inhibition was less than the threshold value of 1.25 that would necessitate
conduct of a clinical DDI study. Thus, etrasimod is not likely to be a clinically relevant
CYP2C8 inhibitor.

•

In Study XT165088, etrasimod did not inhibit UGT1A3, UGT1A4, UGT1A9, UGT2B7, or
UGT2B17. However, etrasimod directly inhibited UGT1A1 and UGT1A6-mediated activities
up to 28% and 48%, respectively, at 10 μM which greatly exceeds the typical observed mean
steady-state Cmax for etrasimod (2 mg once daily) of 0.247 μM [113 ng/mL]). Hence,
etrasimod is unlikely to potentially produce clinically meaningful inhibition of these UGTs.
No clinical study has been conducted to assess the impact of etrasimod on UGTs.

CYP Enzyme Induction
•

Etrasimod is not an inducer of human CYP1A2, CYP2B6, and CYP3A4 based on the foldchange method as mRNA evaluations (see comments below for details).

•

In vitro study using human hepatocytes (No. XT163079) showed that etrasimod (up to 10
μM) caused no significant induction of CYP1A2 mRNA levels; however, in one of three
preparations up to 3.73-fold and 8.94-fold increase was observed for CYP2B6 and CYP3A4
mRNA, respectively, but the increases were less than 11% and 10% as effective as the
positive controls (phenobarbital for CYP2B6 and rifampin for CYP3A4).

•

In addition, the Applicant also assessed the potential for induction by estimating drug
concentration in the liver by using two methods per the Guidance:
o Method 1: Ih = fu,p × [Cmax + (Fa × Fg × ka × Dose)/Qh/RB], where Ih is the hepatic drug
concentration, fu,p is the unbound fraction in plasma, Cmax is the steady state total
maximal concentration in plasma, Fa is the fraction absorbed, Fg is the intestinal
availability, ka is the absorption rate constant, Qh is the hepatic blood flow rate, and RB is
the blood-to-plasma concentration ratio
o Method 2: Imax,u × 30, where Imax,u is the maximal unbound plasma concentration of the
interacting drug at steady state

•

Based on the two methods above, following etrasimod 2 mg once daily, steady-state
etrasimod liver concentration was estimated to be 6.8 nM (Ih) or 156 nM (Imax,u × 30) while
in vitro variable induction potential was shown at 1 μM in human hepatocytes: mRNA
increase of 3.44 -fold in one donor’s samples while < 2-fold in samples from two other
donors.

•

Taken together, the potential for etrasimod to induce CYP enzymes at clinically relevant
concentrations is likely low.

Version date: October 12, 2018
Reference ID: 5259863

217

NDA/BLA Multi-Disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 216956
Velsipity (etrasimod)
Is the Drug a Substrate and/or an Inhibitor of Transport Processes?
•

Study XT168106 was designed to evaluate etrasimod (as etrasimod L-arginine) as an
inhibitor and a substrate of human transporters (i.e., P-gp, BCRP, BSEP, OATP1B1, OATP1B3,
OAT1, OAT3, OCT1, OCT2, MATE1, and MATE2-K).

•

Etrasimod (up to 10 μM) inhibited less than 50% of activity towards BSEP, OATP1B3, OAT1,
OAT3, OCT1, OCT2, MATE1, and MATE2-K but inhibited P-gp, BCRP, and OATP1B1 with IC50
values of ~100, 35.7, and 10 μM, respectively (concentrations of 45,750, 16,333, and 4575
ng/mL respectively). The results are summarized in Table 82 and Table 83.

•

The Applicant also conducted a definitive bidirectional Caco-2 study to confirm the
membrane passive permeability of etrasimod and potential as a substrate for P-gp and
BCRP (APD334.DMPK.007). The efflux ratio for etrasimod (0.5 and 5 μM) was ≤ 2, indicating
it is not a substrate for either P-gp or BCRP.

•

Therefore, etrasimod is not a substrate or an inhibitor of human ABC transporters (P-gp,
BCRP, and BSEP), SLC transporters (OATP1B1, OATP1B3, OAT1/3, OCT1, OCT2, and
MATE1/2-K). Overall, etrasimod is not expected to have transporter mediated clinical DDIs.

Metabolite Profiling in Mass Balance Study
Following single-dose oral administration of [14C]etrasimod in humans (human mass balance
study, No. APD334-107), etrasimod was primarily eliminated through metabolism; unchanged
etrasimod was not detected in urine. Metabolism of etrasimod occurred via oxidation,
dehydrogenation, sulfation, and glucuronidation with 11 minor circulating metabolites
identified in plasma. Oxidative metabolites, M3 and M6, were the 2 most abundant minor
circulating metabolites, with each accounting for < 10% of total radioactivity. M2 was not
detected in human plasma. Etrasimod was the predominant and only major circulating
compound detected in plasma, representing 49% of plasma total radioactivity.
Pharmacogenomics Analysis
No pharmacogenomics analysis was conducted. In vitro effect of recombinant CYP2C9 was
studied with CYP2C9*1 enzyme variant.

Study Design
This was an open-label, single-dose, parallel-group study to evaluate the PK as well as the safety
and tolerability of etrasimod 2 mg administration in males and females with mild, moderate, or
severe hepatic impairment (Child-Pugh A/B/C) as compared to matched-control males and

based on their Child-Pugh score:
• Group 1 – Normal hepatic function
•

Group 2 – Mild hepatic impairment (Child-Pugh Class A)

•

Group 3 – Moderate hepatic impairment (Child-Pugh Class B)

•

Group 4 – Severe hepatic impairment (Child-Pugh Class C)

Subjects received a single oral dose of 2 mg etrasimod following an overnight fast.

Study Design
This was an open-label, single-dose study to evaluate the PK, safety, and tolerability of etrasimod
in subjects with renal impairment.
This study was first conducted using a reduced design. Subjects with severe renal impairment
and/or end-stage renal disease (ESRD) (estimated glomerular filtration rate [eGFR] ≤ 29 mL/min;
either requiring hemodialysis or not; defined as Group C) and matched subjects with normal renal
function were initially enrolled. The eGFR was determined by the Modification of Diet in Renal
Disease equation. For subjects on dialysis, etrasimod dosing on Day 1 occurred immediately after
the approximately 4-hour dialysis procedure completed.
Only if warranted, the study would proceed to the full design (additional evaluation of subjects
with mild and moderate renal impairment, and their matched healthy controls) as described in
Table 92 below.
Table 92. Renal Impairment Study APD334-112 Full Design

Of note, the results of the initial investigation did not warrant further investigation, so Group A
(mild renal impairment), Group B (moderate renal impairment) and their matched subjects with
normal renal function were not studied in this study.

Mass Balance Study APD334-107
Title: A Phase 1, Open-label Study to Evaluate the Absorption, Metabolism, and Excretion of
[14C]Etrasimod (APD334) Following a Single Oral Dose in Healthy Male Subjects
Clinical Site: Covance Clinical Research Unit, Madison, Wisconsin
Study Date: 20 July 2018 to 12 September 2018
Study Design
This was an open-label, non-randomized, single-dose study of radiolabeled etrasimod in eight
healthy male subjects. Subjects received a single oral dose of 2 mg [14C] etrasimod containing
approximately 100 µCi radioactivity following an overnight fast.
Blood, urine, and fecal PK samples were collected for determination of total radioactivity
concentrations (whole blood, plasma, urine, and feces), for determination of etrasimod
concentrations (plasma only), and for metabolite profiling and characterization (plasma, urine,
and feces). Beginning on Day 8, subjects were to be discharged if 1 of the following discharge
criteria for total radioactivity was met:
• ≥ 90% of the administered radioactive dose was recovered in the excreta, or
•

≤ 1% of the radioactive dose was recovered in urine and feces for 3 consecutive 24-hour
collection intervals where samples were provided.

If neither of these discharge criteria were met by the morning of Day 8, subjects were to remain
in the CRU and blood, urine, and feces collections continued at 24-hour intervals until 1 discharge
criterion was met or until Day 15.

The mean etrasimod plasma concentrations, and total radioactivity in plasma and whole blood
in subjects following single oral administration of 2 mg [14C] etrasimod are presented in Figure
27.

Metabolite Profiling and Identification Results
Following oral administration of [14C]etrasimod to healthy male subjects, samples of plasma,
urine, and feces were analyzed and a total of 11, 21, and 9 metabolites were observed in plasma,
urine, and feces, respectively; of these observed metabolites, 14 were identified.
An overall metabolic pathway for etrasimod in humans is presented in Section 6.2.1 above.
Version date: October 12, 2018
Reference ID: 5259863

230

NDA/BLA Multi-Disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 216956
Velsipity (etrasimod)
Metabolite profiling was assessed for the 0-to-312-hour cross-subject plasma pool, which
represented approximately 92% of the mean AUC0-inf for total radioactivity (Table 97). Etrasimod
was identified as the only major radioactive component (49.28%) in the cross-subject extracted
pooled plasma. The 2 most abundant metabolites were AR503641 (M3) and AR504344 (M6), with
each representing < 10% of total radioactivity, as shown in Table 97.
Table 97. Summary of Radiolabeled Components in Cross-Subject Extracted Plasma Pool (0 to
312 hours)

Source: Clinical Study Report APD334-107, Table 14

Bioanalytical Method Report
The Applicant developed six LC-MS/MS (Table 98) and adequately validated. The validation
reports were submitted for all methods. The method CA22262-01 was mainly used for
etrasimod in most clinical trials and method CA27234-01 was used for metabolites M3 and M6.

Applicant’s Original Efficacy Analyses
Table 103 and Table 104 display the Applicant’s analysis results for the primary and multiplicitycontrolled secondary endpoints in APD334-301 using the Applicant’s prespecified protocol
analyses (i.e., assessed within subjects with baseline mMS of 5 or more in the FAS and using the

results are consistent with the results of the Agency’s preferred analyses as shown in Section

Executive Summary (Clinical Relevance)
As part of the NDA package, the Applicant submitted three in vitro pharmacology
studies and a pre- and postnatal development (PPND) study in rats with etrasimod, as
well as genetic toxicology studies with two process-related impurities. These studies
have not previously been reviewed under any IND for etrasimod and are reviewed here.
Etrasimod is a sphingosine-1-phosphate receptor subtype 1,4, and 5 (S1P1,4,5) agonist.
Etrasimod inhibited generation of cAMP, rapidly induced S1P1 internalization, and
induced β-arrestin recruitment to hS1P1 and hS1P5 with similar potency and efficacy to
other S1P receptor modulators (i.e., fingolimod, ozanimod, and siponimod). However,
etrasimod was generally a less potent agonist of GTPγS binding to S1P receptors,
including S1P1, compared to other S1P receptor modulators. Etrasimod also induced a
smaller increase in the IKACh channel current in vitro using isolated human atrial
cardiomyocytes compared to ozanimod or fingolimod under the same assay conditions.
In the PPND study, the NOAEL for maternal toxicity was considered the low dose of 0.4
mg/kg/day (approximately 2 times exposure at the recommended clinical dose of 2 mg,
based on AUC) due to observations of mortality during delivery, increased postimplantation loss, increased number of females with stillborn pups, increased number of
stillborn pups per litter, decreased viability index, and decreased lactation index at
higher doses. A developmental NOAEL could not be established based on the findings
of increased perinatal mortalities (≥ 2 mg/kg/day), adverse clinical signs and decreased
body weight/body weight gain in offspring during the preweaning period (all doses), as
well as a reduced number of implantations, increased rate of pre-implantation loss, and
corresponding decrease in the number of live embryos per litter observed (4
mg/kg/day); these effects occurred at exposures less than that expected at the clinical
dose.

Pharmacology/ Toxicology Summary
Pharmacology
Key Findings:
Etrasimod is sphingosine-1-phosphate receptor subtype 1,4, and 5 (S1P1,4,5) agonist.
Etrasimod concentration-dependently inhibited generation of cAMP in vitro with similar
efficacy and potency compared to the endogenous ligand, S1P, as well as other S1P
receptor modulators (i.e., fingolimod phosphate, ozanimod, siponimod). Similar to other
S1P receptor modulators but in contrast to S1P, etrasimod rapidly and concentrationdependently induced S1P1 internalization in S1P1-overexpressing CHO cells, with the
maximum effect occurring by 2 hours and persisting through at least 6 hours posttreatment.
Etrasimod potently induced GTPγS binding to human S1P1 (hS1P1) and hS1P5.
Etrasimod and was also a partial agonist for GTPγS binding at hS1P3 but not hS1P2 or
hS1P4. Etrasimod was generally a less potent agonist of GTPγS binding to S1P
receptors, including S1P1, compared to other S1P receptor modulators.

5
Reference ID: 5166472

NDA # 216956

Sarah J. Morgan, PhD

preweaning terminal necropsy; however, drug-related findings in prematurely terminated
or stillborn pups included full body edema at 4 mg/kg/day and no milk in the stomach at
2 and 4 mg/kg/day. No etrasimod-related findings, including behavioral assessments
and sexual maturation, were observed in pups during the postweaning period, and the
previously observed differences in body weights normalized during this time in all
treated groups compared to controls.
There were no etrasimod-related effects on pre-coital intervals, mating index, or fertility
index in the F1 generation. A reduced number of implantations and increased rate of
pre-implantation loss were observed at 4 mg/kg/day compared to controls and
correlated with a decreased number of live embryos per litter at 4 mg/kg/day compared
to controls.
The NOAEL for maternal toxicity was considered the low dose of 0.4 mg/kg/day
(approximately 2 times exposure at the recommended clinical dose of 2 mg, based on
AUC), due to observations of mortality during delivery, increased post-implantation loss,
increased number of females with stillborn pups, increased number of stillborn pups per
litter, decreased viability index, and decreased lactation index at higher doses. Based
on increased perinatal mortalities at ≥ 2 mg/kg/day, adverse clinical signs and
decreased body weight/body weight gain in offspring prior to weaning at all doses, as
well as the reduced number of implantations, increased rate of pre-implantation loss,
and corresponding decrease in the number of live embryos per litter in F1 animals
observed at 4 mg/kg/day during the postweaning period, a developmental NOAEL could
not be determined.

4 Pharmacology
4.1 Primary Pharmacology
Activity of Etrasimod and Other S1P Modulators in S1P1 Cyclic AMP and Internalization
Assays in Recombinant CHO Cells
APD334.TS.012
The ability of etrasimod and other sphingosine 1-phosphate (S1P) receptor modulators,
fingolimod phosphate, ozanimod, and siponimod, to induce S1P1 receptor
internalization and inhibit cyclic adenosine monophosphate (cAMP) generation was
evaluated using two Chinese hamster ovary (CHO) cell lines engineered to express
human S1P1 (one high-expressing line, CHO-21BWc1, and one moderate-expressing
line, CHO-21BWc2) and primary human umbilical vein endothelial cells (HUVEC), which
endogenously express S1P1.
Etrasimod concentration-dependently inhibited cAMP generation in both CHO-21BWc1
and CHO-21BWc2 cells, with IC50 values of 453.9 and 464.5 pM, respectively, and
103% efficacy relative to that of the endogenous ligand S1P in both cell lines. These
findings were similar to those observed with fingolimod phosphate (IC50 = 7.2 and 7.9
pM, 101% and 103% efficacy, respectively), ozanimod (IC50 = 29.5 and 38.9 pM, 104%
and 100% efficacy), siponimod (IC50 = 28.2 and 27.5 pM, 101% and 103% efficacy),
and S1P itself (IC50 = 28.2 and 27.5 pM).

In HUVEC, etrasimod concentration-dependently inhibited cAMP generation with an

those observed with fingolimod phosphate (IC50 = 1.6 nM, 94% efficacy), ozanimod
(IC50 = 2.0 nM, 102% efficacy), siponimod (IC50 = 1.6 nM, 101% efficacy), and S1P itself
(IC50 = 19.1 nM).

Following incubation of hS1P1-expressing CHO cells with 100 nM of the various S1P
receptor modulators, all compounds induced rapid internalization in both CHO-21BWc1
and CHO-21BWc2 cells that reached a maximum level within 2 hours and persisted
through the end of the assay (6 hours). In contrast, S1P induced minimal internalization,
and surface expression of S1P1 returned to baseline levels within approximately 3
hours.

APD334: etrasimod; FTY720p: fingolimod phosphate.

As S1P at 100 nM induced minimal and more transient internalization in the previous
assay, internalization of S1P1 with S1P receptor modulators at 10x the concentration (1
µM) was quantified in the recombinant CHO cell lines with an incubation time of 2
hours., Fingolimod phosphate was used as the control to determine the relative efficacy
of the other compounds.

Etrasimod concentration-dependently induced S1P1 internalization in both CHO21BWc1 and CHO-21BWc2 cells, with IC50 values of 8.9 and 8.3 nM, respectively. The
efficacy of etrasimod for internalization relative to fingolimod phosphate was 90% and
99% in the two cell lines, respectively. These findings were similar to those observed
with ozanimod (IC50 = 4.2 and 3.5 nM, 103% and 111% efficacy, respectively),
siponimod (IC50 = 4.2 and 1.8 nM, 107% and 103% efficacy) and fingolimod phosphate
(IC50 = 3.7 and 1.4 nM). Under the tested conditions, S1P dose-dependently induced
internalization with IC50 values of 5211.9 and 608.1 nM and efficacy of 103% and 94%,
respectively, relative to fingolimod phosphate.

Pharmacological Profiling of Etrasimod, Its M3 and M6 Metabolites, and Other S1P
Receptor Modulators in β-Arrestin and GTPγS Assays for S1P1-5 Receptors
APD334.TS.020
The ability of etrasimod, its M3 and M6 metabolites, and other S1P receptor modulators
ozanimod, ozanimod ketone metabolite, fingolimod phosphate, and siponimod, to
induce the binding of guanosine 5'-O-(gamma-thio)-triphosphate (GTPγS) and recruit βarrestin to human S1P1-5 were evaluated using in vitro assays. GTPγS binding was
evaluated in S1P receptor-expressing CHO-K1 cells using radiolabeled GTP. β-arrestin
recruitment was evaluated using the relevant DiscoverX assay (a modified
chemiluminescent assay using HEK cells). Efficacy values for experiments with
hS1P1,2,3,5 were normalized to the endogenous ligand, S1P, while experiments with
hS1P4 were normalized to amiselimod phosphate.
GTPγS binding
Etrasimod potently induced GTPγS binding to hS1P1 and hS1P5 (EC50s = 57 nM and
63.5 nM, respectively, and efficacy 99% and 72% of S1P, respectively). Etrasimod was
also a partial agonist at hS1P3 (EC50 = 6050 nM, efficacy 42%); however, the clinical
relevance of this effect is expected to be limited, as the EC50 of 6050 nM is
approximately 25 times higher than the mean steady state Cmax in healthy volunteers
following 21 days of dosing with etrasimod at 2 mg (113 ng/mL or 247 nM; Study
APD334-002). Etrasimod did not induce GTPγS binding to hS1P2 or hS1P4.
Etrasimod metabolite M3 had similar efficacy with approximately 10-fold lower potency
for GTPγS binding at hS1P1 and hS1P5 as well as similar efficacy and potency at hS1P3

14
Reference ID: 5166472

NDA # 216956

Sarah J. Morgan, PhD

compared to the parent drug. Metabolite M6 had similar efficacy with approximately 3fold lower potency at hS1P1 as well as similar efficacy and potency at hS1P3 and hS1P5
compared to the parent drug.
At hS1P1, etrasimod had similar efficacy but 15- to 48-fold lower potency compared to
ozanimod, fingolimod phosphate, and siponimod. At hS1P5, etrasimod had similar
efficacy but 577-fold lower potency compared to fingolimod phosphate but lower
efficacy and 52- to 1270-fold lower potency compared to ozanimod and siponimod.
Overall, etrasimod is a less potent agonist of GTPγS binding to S1P receptors, including
S1P1, compared to other S1P receptor modulators. This finding may have clinical
relevance, as GTPγS binding to S1P1 has been proposed to contribute to the transient
heart rate reduction observed after the first dose of other S1P receptor modulators.5,6,7

β-arrestin recruitment
Etrasimod potently induced β-arrestin recruitment to hS1P1 and hS1P5 (EC50s = 44 nM
and 63.4 nM, respectively, and efficacy 120% and 86% of S1P, respectively). Etrasimod
was a partial agonist of β-arrestin recruitment at hS1P4 (EC50 = 141 nM, efficacy 54% of
amilselimod phosphate); the physiological or clinical relevance of this finding is
unknown. Etrasimod did not induce β-arrestin recruitment to hS1P2 or hS1P3.

16

Sarah J. Morgan, PhD

Etrasimod metabolites M3 and M6 had similar efficacy at hS1P1 and hS1P5 but
approximately 3-fold (M6) or 10-fold (M3) lower potency compared to the parent drug.
M6 was also a partial agonist at hS1P4, with similar efficacy and approximately 10-fold
lower potency compared to the parent drug.
Compared to ozanimod and siponimod, etrasimod had similar efficacy and potency
(EC50) in the β-arrestin recruitment assay at hS1P1. Etrasimod also had similar efficacy
but approximately 4-fold lower potency at this receptor compared to fingolimod
phosphate. Notably, the various S1P receptor modulators differed in efficacy and
potency at hS1P4 and hS1P5 (see tables below). Etrasimod consistently recruited βarrestin more potently in this assay compared to other S1P receptor modulators, though
with a lower efficacy.

4.2 Secondary Pharmacology
Analysis of Effects of AR401959, Fingolimod and Ozanimod on GIRK (IKAch currents)
in voltage clamp studies in human atrial myocytes
APD334.TS.011
The ability of etrasimod (named AR401959 in this report), ozanimod, and fingolimod to
affect IKACh channel current was evaluated using patch clamping of isolated human atrial
cardiomyocytes (n = 6 donors). Compounds were tested over the range of 0.1-1000 nM.
Changes in the IKACh channel current were normalized the increased observed with 10
µM carbachol.
Overall, etrasimod increased IKACh channel current in human atrial cardiomyocytes less
than ozanimod and fingolimod. Etrasimod concentration-dependently induced increases
in the IKACh channel current, with a maximum increase of 39% at 1000 nM, (4 times
higher than the mean steady state Cmax in healthy volunteers following 21 days of
dosing with etrasimod at 2 mg: 113 ng/mL or 247 nM; Study APD334-002). Ozanimod
and fingolimod also concentration-dependently induced increases in the IKACh channel
current, with maximum increases of 70.4% and 53.3%, respectively, at 1000 nM.

9 Reproductive and Developmental Toxicology
9.3 Prenatal and Postnatal Development (PPND)+
9.3.1 A Dose Range-Finding Prenatal/Postnatal Development Study of APD334

Summary of Findings
In this preliminary dose range-finding study, pregnant Sprague Dawley rats were
administered vehicle or etrasimod (named APD334 in this report) at 0.05, 0.2, 1, or 2
mg/kg/day from gestation day (GD) 6 through lactation day (LD) 20. There were no
maternal deaths, clinical signs of toxicity, or drug-related effects on body weight/body
weight gain, feed consumption, or macroscopic examinations during gestation or
lactation. No effects of etrasimod administration were observed on parturition. An
increased number of stillborn pups and decreased viability index were observed at ≥ 1
mg/kg/day compared to controls. Lower pup body weights were observed at ≥ 0.2
mg/kg/day compared to controls on PNDs 20 and 21, but these effects were not dosedependent. Based on these findings, a high dose of at least 2 mg/kg/day was
considered appropriate for use in the pivotal PPND study.

Table 16. Design of Pre- and Postnatal Development Study with Etrasimod
(APD334) in Sprague Dawley Rats

Table copied directly from study report (Study APD334-TOX-011).

Summary of Findings
Pregnant Sprague Dawley rats were administered vehicle or etrasimod (named APD334
in this report) at 0.4, 2, or 4 mg/kg/day from GD 6 through LD 20. There were no
adverse effects on maternal clinical signs, or body weights at any dose of etrasimod,
although decreased feed consumption was observed in all treated groups during
gestation.
The number of pregnant females was similar in all groups. One female at 4 mg/kg/day
was prematurely terminated on GD 22 due to severe, adverse clinical signs during
delivery. A significant increase in post-implantation loss was observed at 4 mg/kg/day.
There was also a significant increase in the number of females with stillborn pups at 2
and 4 mg/kg/day and increased number of stillborn pups per litter at 2 mg/kg/day
compared to controls. The viability index was decreased at 2 and 4 mg/kg/day while the
lactation index was decreased at 4 mg/kg/day compared to controls.
An increased number of F1 offspring were found dead or found missing and presumed
to be cannibalized, predominantly between PND 0-4, in the 2 and 4 mg/kg/day groups
compared to controls. Etrasimod-related clinical signs in F1 offspring from birth through
PND 4 included cold to touch, suspected dehydration (based on skin turgor), discolored
skin, skin pallor, and swelling at 4 mg/kg/day and no milk band in the stomach at 2 and
4 mg/kg/day. After litters were culled on PND 4, clinical signs related to etrasimod
administration at 4 mg/kg/day included suspected dehydration (based on skin turgor)
and thin appearance. Decreased maternal feed consumption during lactation
corresponded to decreased pup body weights and body weight gain during the
preweaning period at 2 and 4 mg/kg/day. Pup body weights at the end of the
preweaning period (PND 21) were 10%, 12%, and 20% lower than controls at 0.4, 2,
and 4 mg/kg/day, respectively, while total body weight gains were 11%, 14%, and 23%
lower than controls, respectively. No etrasimod-related macroscopic findings were
26
Reference ID: 5166472

NDA # 216956

Sarah J. Morgan, PhD

observed in F1 offspring at the preweaning terminal necropsy; however, drug-related
findings in prematurely terminated or stillborn pups included full body edema at 4
mg/kg/day and no milk in the stomach at 2 and 4 mg/kg/day. No etrasimod-related
findings were observed in the F1 generation during the postweaning period, including
behavioral assessments and sexual maturation, and the previously observed
differences in body weights normalized during this time in all treated groups compared
to controls.
There were no etrasimod-related effects on pre-coital intervals, mating index, or fertility
index in the F1 generation. A reduced number of implantations and increased rate of
pre-implantation loss were observed at 4 mg/kg/day compared to controls. These
findings correlated with a decrease in the number of live embryos per litter at 4
mg/kg/day compared to controls.
Based on the premature termination of one female during delivery (4 mg/kg/day),
increased post-implantation loss (4 mg/kg/day), increased number of females with
stillborn pups (2 and 4 mg/kg/day), increased number of stillborn pups per litter (2
mg/kg/day), decreased viability index (2 and 4 mg/kg/day), and decreased lactation
index (4 mg/kg/day), the NOAEL for maternal toxicity was considered the low dose of
0.4 mg/kg/day (approximately 2 times exposure at the recommended clinical dose of 2
mg, based on AUC). Based on the increased perinatal mortalities at ≥ 2 mg/kg/day,
adverse clinical signs, and findings of decreased body weight/body weight gain at all
doses during the preweaning period as well as a reduced number of implantations,
increased rate of pre-implantation loss, and corresponding decrease in the number of
live embryos per litter observed at 4 mg/kg/day, a developmental NOAEL could not be
determined.

Observations and Results
F0 Dams
Mortality
One control female and three females at 4 mg/kg/day were prematurely terminated
during the study.
The control female and two females at 4 mg/kg/day were prematurely terminated on
lactation day (LD) 1 due to a lack of surviving pups. No clinical signs of toxicity or
adverse changes in body weight or feed consumption were observed in these animals
prior to termination. Upon necropsy, one of the 4 mg/kg/day females was observed to
have pup tissue in the stomach.
A third female at 4 mg/kg/day was prematurely terminated on GD 22 due to severe,
adverse clinical signs during delivery. No clinical signs of toxicity were observed during
the gestation period. However, this animal lost 25 g (7% of body weight) from GD 18-22.
There were no macroscopic abnormalities observed during necropsy. This rat was
pregnant and had a litter consisting of 14 dead fetuses.

27
Reference ID: 5166472

NDA # 216956

Sarah J. Morgan, PhD

All other F0 animals survived to scheduled necropsy.
Clinical Signs
No clinical signs related to etrasimod administration were observed.
Body Weight
Although isolated differences in body weight and/or body weight gain were observed
between treated and untreated groups (or in individual animals), no clear effects of
etrasimod on these parameters were observed during gestation or lactation.
Feed Consumption
Decreased feed consumption was observed in all treated groups during gestation and
lactation.
During gestation, significantly decreased feed consumption exceeding 10% below
controls was observed at 2 and 4 mg/kg/day from GD 6-9. Significantly decreased feed
consumption (less than 10% different from controls) was also observed at 4 mg/kg/day
from GD 12-15. These differences resulted in an overall reduction in feed consumption
over the gestation period (GD 6-18) of −6.6% at 2 mg/kg/day and −8.5% at 4 mg/kg/day
compared to controls. Decreased feed consumption did not correlate with decreased
body weight or decreased body weight gain during any interval in any group.
During the lactation period, significantly decreased feed consumption was consistently
observed at 4 mg/kg/day, such that overall feed consumption during this period was
27% lower than the control group. Feed consumption was also decreased by
approximately 10% at 2 mg/kg/day during the periods of PND 1-4 and PND 17-21
compared to controls. Decreased feed consumption did not correlate with decreased
body weight or decreased body weight gain during any interval of the lactation period in
any treated group.

Maternal Performance
The number of pregnant females was similar in all groups (22, 21, 22, and 22 in the
control, low, mid, and high dose groups, respectively). One female at 4 mg/kg/day was
prematurely terminated on GD 22 due to severe, adverse clinical signs during delivery.

29
Reference ID: 5166472

NDA # 216956

Sarah J. Morgan, PhD

A significant increase in post-implantation loss was observed at 4 mg/kg/day compared
to controls (26.15% versus 12.29%).
Although gestation length significantly increased at 2 and 4 mg/kg/day compared to
controls (21.9 and 21.8 days, respectively, versus 21.4 days), these values were all
within the historical control range (mean: 21.9 days, range: 21.3-22.7 days). There was
also a significant increase in the number of females at 2 and 4 mg/kg/day with stillborn
pups (seven and four, respectively, compared to zero in the control group). This
corresponded with a significant increase in the number of stillborn pups per litter at 2
mg/kg/day (3.11) but not at 4 mg/kg/day (2.24) compared to controls (0); the number of
stillborn pups at both 2 and 4 mg/kg/day exceeded the historical control range (0.4-1.7).
The viability index (percentage of liveborn pups that survived to PND 4) was decreased
at 2 mg/kg/day (87.36%) and significantly decreased at 4 mg/kg/day (42.84%)
compared to controls (92.45%). Lactation index (percentage of liveborn pups that
survived to PND 21) was decreased at 4 mg/kg/day compared to controls (88.95%
versus 99.40%).
Although not significant, the percentage of live male pups was lower at 4 mg/kg/day
compared to controls (44.71% versus 51.30%).
No effects of etrasimod were observed on maternal-litter interactions (e.g., pup
grooming, nesting, nursing).
No etrasimod-related macroscopic abnormalities were observed in F0 females following
necropsy.

Toxicokinetics
Following the first dose of etrasimod on GD 6, plasma concentrations for all post-dose
samples (i.e., 1, 4, and 24 hours post-dose) were below the lower limit of quantification
(< 5.00 ng/mL) for all control animals as well as for one animal at 0.4 mg/kg/day and
one animal at 4 mg/kg/day. All other treated animals had measurable concentrations of
drug. There were no deviations or documented events noted in the dosing records;
thus, no explanation was provided for the observed lack of drug exposure in these
animals. Toxicokinetic analyses were reported both with and without the apparently
anomalous data from these animals, but only the analysis excluding the anomalous
values is presented below. No unexplained abnormalities were observed in the
toxicokinetic data collected on GD 17 or PND 20.
Following a single dose (GD 6), Cmax and AUC of etrasimod increased approximately
proportionally with increasing dose. Tmax of etrasimod occurred between 1-4 hours and
tended to occur later with increasing dose.
On GD 17, Cmax and AUC of etrasimod increased approximately proportionally with
increasing dose. Tmax of etrasimod occurred at 4 hours for all dose levels. No significant
accumulation of etrasimod was observed between GD 6 and 20.
On PND 20, Cmax and AUC of etrasimod increased approximately proportionally with
increasing dose. Tmax of etrasimod occurred between 1-2 hours with no clear effect of
dose. A slight loss of exposure to etrasimod was observed at 0.4 and 2 mg/kg/day (PND
20 AUC values 64% and 69% those observed on GD 6, respectively) but not at 4
mg/kg/day.

F1 Generation
Preweaning
An increased number of mortalities was observed in pups from the 2 and 4 mg/kg/day
groups compared to controls, either found dead or missing/cannibalized. Specifically, 12
and 27 pups were found dead at 2 and 4 mg/kg/day, respectively, compared to 3 pups
in the control group (5.3% and 15% of liveborn pups, respectively, versus 1.4% of
liveborn control pups). Most pups were found dead between PND 0-4. Additionally, 18
and 60 pups were found missing and presumed cannibalized at 2 and 4 mg/kg/day,
respectively, compared to 6 pups in the control group (7.9% and 33% of liveborn pups,
respectively, versus 2.8% of liveborn control pups). Most presumptive cannibalizations
occurred between PND 0-4. The mortality rates at 2 and 4 mg/kg/day greatly exceed the
historical control rates (maximum 1.3% of liveborn pups through PND 21 in studies with
culling).

Decreased pup body weight was observed in all treated groups compared to controls.
Based on sex-combined data, significantly decreased body weight (> 10% difference
from controls) was observed starting at PND 7 and persisted throughout the study. Final
body weight at 4 mg/kg/day was 20% lower compared to controls, and total body weight
gain over the study was 23% lower than controls. At 2 mg/kg/day, final body weight was
12% lower than controls, and body weight gain from PND 1-21 was 14% lower than
controls. Finally, at 0.4 mg/kg/day, final body weight was 10% lower than controls and
total body weight gain was 11% lower than controls. The observed effects on body
weight were similar between male and female pups.

Drug-related macroscopic findings were observed in pups examined prior to PND 21

Postweaning
No etrasimod-related mortality or clinical signs of toxicity were observed in F1 offspring
during the postweaning period.
In males, though the observed decreased body weight during the preweaning period
resolved during the postweaning period in all treated groups, reduced body weight gain
(> 10% difference) was observed at 4 mg/kg/day compared to controls between PND
22-57; these findings correlated with reduced feed consumption during this period
(−10% to −15% compared to controls). However, body weight gains were similar after
this point. Final body weights were not significantly different between treated and control
animals (−0.3%, −4.6%, and −7.6% in the low, mid, and high dose groups, respectively,
compared to controls).

No effects of etrasimod were observed on vaginal patency in females. In male offspring
at 2 and 4 mg/kg/day, preputial separation occurred significantly later than controls
(46.0 and 49.2 days, respectively, compared to 44.4 days), and the number of days at 4
mg/kg/day exceeded the historical control range of 40.6-46.7 days. These findings were
considered a consequence of the reduced pup body weight in these groups and not a
direct effect of etrasimod administration.

No effects of etrasimod were observed on behavioral assessments (i.e., motor activity,

Reproductive Performance
There were no etrasimod-related effects on pre-coital intervals, mating index, or fertility
index. Pregnancy was confirmed in 16, 17, 19, and 14 F1 females in the control, 0.4, 2,
and 4 mg/kg/day groups, respectively. All values for mating, fertility, and pregnancy
index were within the historical controls range.
In pregnant F1 females, no differences in body weight, body weight gain, or feed
consumption were observed over the period GD 0-13.
The number of implantations was highly variable in all groups, including controls, and
ranged from 3-21 per animal. The mean number of implantations was reduced at 4
mg/kg/day compared to controls (11.1 versus 14.8). Pre-implantation loss was also
increased at 4 mg/kg/day compared to controls (23.76% versus 3.56%). The number of
implantations and pre-implantation loss at 4 mg/kg/day were outside the respective
historical control ranges. The decrease in implantations correlated with a decrease in
the number of live embryos per litter at 4 mg/kg/day compared to controls (10.5 versus
14.3). No effects of etrasimod were observed at 0.4 or 2 mg/kg/day.

Toxicokinetics
A full toxicokinetic analysis was not performed in F1 offspring.
On PND 4, the plasma concentration of etrasimod increased approximately
proportionally with dose in male offspring at all doses and in females at 0.4 and 4
mg/kg/day but not 2 mg/kg/day. The pup to maternal concentration ratio in male pups
decreased with increasing dose, while no clear effect of dose was observed on the pup
to maternal concentration ratio in female pups. Plasma concentrations were consistently
higher in dams compared to pups.

Sarah J. Morgan, PhD

Executive Summary (Clinical Relevance)
To support clinical development of etrasimod in subjects with ulcerative colitis, the
Applicant submitted a nonclinical study with etrasimod using the CD4+CD45RBhigh T cell
transfer mouse model of ulcerative colitis. In this model, etrasimod ameliorated the
effects of colitis induction including body weight loss, increased colon weight to length
ratio, increased mucosal thickness, increased histopathology score, increased
expression of immune cell markers, and increased expression of pro-inflammatory
cytokines. The observed effects of etrasimod in this model were not dose-dependent,
but generally occurred at ≥ 3 mg/kg/day (approximately 2.4-fold the clinical dose of 2
mg/day, based on HED).

Pharmacology/ Toxicology Summary
Pharmacology
Key Findings:
The pharmacodynamic effects of etrasimod were investigated in the CD4+CD45RBhigh T
cell transfer mouse model of ulcerative colitis. Etrasimod ameliorated the effects of
colitis induction including body weight loss, increased colon weight to length ratio,
increased mucosal thickness, increased histopathology score, increased expression of
immune cell markers, and increased expression of pro-inflammatory cytokines. The
observed effects of etrasimod in this model were not dose-dependent, but generally
occurred at ≥ 3 mg/kg/day.

Reference ID: 5158142

NDA # 216956

Sarah J. Morgan, PhD

4 Pharmacology
4.1 Primary Pharmacology
Effect of Treatment with 1 & 3 mg/kg APD334 on Experimental Colitis in Mice
APD334.TS.001
The pharmacodynamic effects of etrasimod (named APD334 in this report) were
investigated in a mouse model of ulcerative colitis. In this model, CD4+CD45RBhigh T
cells are isolated from wild type mice and transferred to SCID mice (“naïve”) wherein
they induce colon inflammation and pathology reminiscent of human ulcerative colitis
(e.g., loose, mucinous stool, body weight loss). These mice were administered
etrasimod at 1, 3, or 10 mg/kg/day from the day of T cell transfer (day 1) through day
32.
While healthy mice (labeled “unsorted/water” in the figures below) gained weight during
the course of the study, vehicle-treated colitis mice (“water”) lost body weight during the
course of the study, particularly after day 20. Etrasimod significantly inhibited colitisassociated body weight loss at 3 and 10 mg/kg/day compared to vehicle, though the
effects were independent of dose. Fingolimod (named FTY720 in this report) also
significantly inhibited colitis-associated body weight compared to vehicle.
Figure 1. Effects of Etrasimod on Body Weight Change in Colitis Mice

* p < 0.05, *** p < 0.001 compared to vehicle.
APD334: etrasimod; FTY720: fingolimod.
Figure copied directly from study report (Study APD334-TS-001).

7
Reference ID: 5158142

NDA # 216956

Sarah J. Morgan, PhD

The induction of colitis resulted in increased colon weight to length ratio compared to
healthy animals (“naïve” or “unsorted/H2O”). Etrasimod significantly reduced the colitisassociated increase in colon weight to length ratio at all tested doses compared to
vehicle (“sorted/water”). Fingolimod also significantly reduced the colitis-associated
increase in colon weight to length ratio compared to vehicle.
Figure 2. Effects of Etrasimod on Colon Weight to Length Ratio in Colitis Mice

* p < 0.05, ** p < 0.01, *** p < 0.001, **** p < 0.0001 compared to vehicle.
APD334: etrasimod; FTY720: fingolimod.
Figure copied directly from study report (Study APD334-TS-001).

The induction of colitis also resulted in increased mucosal thickness compared to
healthy animals. Etrasimod significantly reduced the colitis-associated increase in
mucosal thickness at 3 and 10 mg/kg/day compared to vehicle. Fingolimod also
significantly reduced the colitis-associated increase in mucosal thickness compared to
vehicle.

8
Reference ID: 5158142

NDA # 216956

Sarah J. Morgan, PhD

Figure 3. Effects of Etrasimod on Mucosal Thickness in Colitis Mice

* p < 0.05, *** p < 0.001, **** p < 0.0001 compared to vehicle.
APD334: etrasimod; FTY720: fingolimod
Figure copied directly from study report (Study APD334-TS-001).

Histopathology was performed on the distal colon. The tissue was scored based on the
following criteria:
 Crypt score based on abnormal crypt architecture including distortion, branching,
atrophy and crypt loss (range 0-3)
 goblet cell loss (range 0-2)
 mucosal erosion and ulceration (range 0-1)
 immune cell infiltration (range 0-2)
Total histopathological score was calculated by combining the scores for each of the
four parameters for a maximum score of 8. However, the Applicant did not clearly report
which criteria contributed to the observed drug-related effects on this parameter.
The histopathology score was significantly increased in colitis animals compared to
healthy controls. Etrasimod treatment significantly reduced the histopathology score at 3
mg/kg/day only compared to vehicle control. Fingolimod also significantly reduced the
histopathology score compared to vehicle control

Sarah J. Morgan, PhD

Mice

** p < 0.01, **** p < 0.0001 compared to vehicle.
APD334: etrasimod; FTY720: fingolimod
Figure copied directly from study report (Study APD334-TS-001).

Compared to healthy mice, colitis mice demonstrated increased mRNA expression of
immune cell markers and cytokines in colonic tissue. Specifically, colitis mice had higher
levels of CD4 (indicative of T cells, natural killer cells, and/or dendritic cells), CD3γ (T
cells), and CD11b (monocytes, natural killer cells, and/or dendritic cells), while levels of
Ly6G (neutrophils and/or granulocytes) were unchanged. Etrasimod treatment resulted
in decreased levels of CD4 (≥ 3 mg/kg/day), CD3γ (≥ 3 mg/kg/day), and CD11b (all
doses) compared to vehicle controls, while no differences were observed on Ly6G.

Similarly, mRNA expression of T cell and/or monocyte-derived pro-inflammatory
cytokines, including tumor necrosis factor alpha (TNF-α), interleukin (IL)-1β, IL-6, IL17A, and interferon gamma (IFN-γ), was higher in colitis mice compared to healthy
animals (“naïve” or “unsorted/H2O”), while no differences were observed in the
expression of IL-10 or IL-13 compared to vehicle control mice (“unsorted/H2O”).
Etrasimod treatment significantly decreased mRNA expression of TNF-α (all doses), IL1β (all doses), IL-6 (3 mg/kg/day only), IL-17A (all doses), and IFN-γ (1 mg/kg/day only)
compared to vehicle controls.

Sarah J. Morgan, PhD

Executive Summary (Clinical Relevance)
Etrasimod was positive for carcinogenicity in mice but negative for carcinogenicity in
rats.
In a two-year carcinogenicity study in mice, a statistically significant positive doseresponse in the incidences of hemangiosarcoma malignant tumor (multicentric
neoplasm) and combined hemangioma/hemangiosarcoma were observed at 6 and 20
mg/kg/day males and females compared to controls. These doses correspond to 46and 134-fold, respectively, the MRHD of 2 mg/day etrasimod (based on AUC
comparison).
In a two-year carcinogenicity study in rats, no carcinogenic findings were observed in
males or females administered up to 20 mg/kg/day for 86 to 89 weeks, after which
mortalities precluded continued conduct of the study. This dose corresponds to 88- and
198-fold the MRHD of 2 mg/day etrasimod in males and females, respectively (based
on AUC comparison).
The Executive Carcinogenicity Assessment Committee concurred that the two-year
mouse carcinogenicity study was adequately conducted, and there was a statistically
significant, drug-related increase in the incidences of hemangiosarcoma malignant
tumor (multicentric neoplasm) and combined hemangioma/hemangiosarcoma at the mid
and high doses (6 and 20 mg/kg/day) in male and female mice. The Committee also
concurred that the two-year rat carcinogenicity study was adequately conducted, and
there were no drug-related neoplasms in either males or females.1

that survival in the control males had reached 20 at Week 91, and all surviving

Mouse Study:
 On October 2, 2020 (SDN 46), the Applicant requested advice from DPT-N on
their proposed modification plans for the 2-year carcinogenicity study in mice,
based on decreased survival at Week 79. ECAC recommended that no actions
be taken at that time but provided recommended stopping and termination
criteria should survival decrease further.8
 On December 30, 2020 (SDN 53), the Applicant notified DPT-N that survival in
the high dose male group had reached 20 at Week 91, and dose administration
was suspended at that time. This group reached 15 survivors at Week 99,
triggering termination of surviving males. Survival in the mid dose male group
reached 20 at Week 99, and dose administration was suspended at that time.
However, mid dose males did not reach 15 survivors prior to scheduled necropsy
at the end of the study period. Water control, vehicle control, and low-dose male
groups were administered control or etrasimod daily, as appropriate, until the end
of the scheduled dosing period (Week 104).
 On November 17, 2020 (SDN 51), the Applicant notified DPT-N that survival in
the high dose female group had reached 20 at week 84, and dose administration
was suspended at that time. On December 30, 2020 (SDN 53), the Applicant
notified the Division that survival in the high dose female group had reached 15
at Week 91, triggering termination of surviving females in that group. Survival in
mid and low dose females reached 20 at Weeks 92 and 98, respectively,
resulting in suspension of dose administration. The mid dose female group
reached 15 survivors at Week 96, triggering termination of surviving females in
this group. Survival in the female water control group reached 20 at Week 100,
triggering termination of all surviving females at this time.
On October 14, 2022 (SDN 1), the Applicant submitted NDA 216956 to the Division of
Gastroenterology (DG) for etrasimod as a treatment of moderately to severely active
ulcerative colitis. The NDA submission included the final study reports and
corresponding SEND datasets for the 2-year carcinogenicity studies in mice and rats.
The adequacy and findings of these studies are the subject of this review.

Lot # 5015-12-16

Y
The high dose of 20 mg/kg/day was based
on findings of mortality at 200 mg/kg/day
and adverse histopathology findings (lung
and thyroid) at ≥ 60 mg/kg/day in the 91day preliminary study (Study 900-169).
The mid and low doses were selected to
provide an appropriate dose-response.

Reviewer Carcinogenicity Conclusion (negative/ positive): Positive
ECAC Carcinogenicity Conclusion (negative/ positive): Positive

Key Study Findings
 Etrasimod had a dose-dependent effect on the mortality rate with significantly
increased mortalities in high dose males and females compared to vehicle
controls. Mortality rates were 72%, 64%, 66%, 72%, and 77% for water control,
vehicle control, low, mid, and high dose males, respectively. Mortality rates were
73%, 69%, 69%, 77%, and 77% in female mice, respectively.
 Etrasimod-related non-neoplastic lesions were observed in the lungs in all
treated groups and in the thymus in the mid and high dose groups. Additionally,
angiomatous hyperplasia was observed in various tissues (bone marrow,
epididymides, heart, liver, subcutis, femorotibial joint) in high dose males and in
the uterus of mid and high dose females with increased incidence compared to
control animals.
 Etrasimod-related neoplastic lesions included a dose-dependent increase in the
incidence of combined hemangioma and hemangiosarcomas (multicentric
neoplasm) in both male and female mice compared to control animals.

NDA # 216956

Sarah J. Morgan, PhD

Tumor Findings:
Independent analyses of the tumor findings were conducted by FDA statistical reviewer,
Feng Zhou, M.S.9,10 Statistically significant positive dose-responses in the incidences of
hemangiosarcoma malignant tumor (multicentric neoplasm) and combined
hemangioma/hemangiosarcoma were observed in both male and female mice
(Ps<0.005) when compared with the vehicle control group. The incidences of
hemangiosarcoma malignant tumor and combined hemangioma/hemangiosarcoma in
the mid and high dose groups were statistically significantly higher compared to the
vehicle control group (Ps<0.01). Tumor incidences were similar between the vehicle
control and water control groups.

Observations and Results
Mortality
Animals were observed at least twice daily for morbidity and mortality. Increased early
terminations related to hemangiomas/hemangiosarcomas were reported at ≥ 6
mg/kg/day and were attributed to etrasimod administration. Other causes of
morbidity/moribundity were observed with similar incidence in all groups, including
controls, and therefore were considered unrelated to etrasimod administration.
Due to mortality, dosing was suspended and/or groups were terminated prior to
completion of the planned 104-week study. the timeline of dosing cessation and/or
termination for this study is provided in Table 3.

Independent analyses of survival were conducted by FDA statistical reviewer, Feng
Zhou, M.S.9,10 The survival distributions of mice in all treatment groups were estimated
using the Kaplan-Meier product limit method. For control, low, medium, and high dose
groups, the dose-response relationship was tested using the likelihood ratio test and the
homogeneity of survival distributions was tested using the log-rank test.
The statistical reviewer’s analysis showed the numbers (percent) of deaths that
occurred prior to scheduled termination were 54 (72%), 48 (64%), 43 (66%), 47 (72%),
or 50 (77%) in male mice and 55 (73%), 52 (69%), 45 (69%), 50 (77%), or 50 (77%) in
female mice in the WC, VC, LD, MD, and HD groups, respectively. Survival analyses
showed a statistically significant dose-response relationship in mortality in both males
and females when compared with the VC group (Ps <0.05). Mortality rates in HD groups
were statistical significantly higher than VC group for both male and female mice
(Ps<0.05). Death rates for the WC group were similar as that of the VC group for both
male and female mice.

Clinical Signs
Detailed clinical examinations were conducted weekly throughout the study. No
etrasimod-related clinical signs were observed, including no trends in the incidence or
frequency of palpable masses/nodules.
Body Weights
Decreased absolute body weight was observed in all treated groups compared to the
WC throughout the study, though differences rarely exceeded 10%. Decreased body
weight was also observed in all treated groups compared to the VC throughout the
study, with differences exceeding 10% on some occasions from Week 76 onward.
Similarly, dose-independent decreased body weight gain was observed in all treated
males compared to the WC (−10% to −25%) and VC groups (−16% to −30%) and in LD
an HD females compared to the WC (−31% and −28%, respectively) and VC groups
(−30% and −27%).

Feed Consumption
Decreased feed consumption was observed in all treated groups compared to WC and
VC groups at various points during the study with no dose-dependence. Decreased
feed consumption generally correlated with decreased body weights in treated
compared to control animals.
Gross Pathology
Etrasimod-related macroscopic findings were observed in the liver and subcutis of a few
HD males. In most cases, cysts in the liver and edema in the subcutis of these animals
correlated with histopathological findings of hemangiosarcoma.
No treatment-related macroscopic findings were reported in LD or MD males or in
treated females from any group.

Histopathology
Peer Review Conducted: Yes (Theodore Slone, Jr., DVM, DACVP)
Historical Control Provided for Tumor Incidence: No
Neoplastic
Neoplastic findings attributed by the Applicant to treatment with etrasimod were limited
to an increased incidence of hemangiosarcomas and hemangiomas in males and
females in the MD and HD groups. Commonly affected tissues included the liver,
subcutis, heart, bone marrow, and spleen. A few hemangiosarcomas/hemangiomas
were also observed in the uterus, intestine, skeletal muscle, lung, thymus, adrenal
glands, kidneys, eyes, mesenteric lymph node, mesentery/peritoneum, spinal cord, and
epididymides. These neoplasms were observed earlier in the study in the MD and HD
groups compared to control animals. Additionally, the number of animals with two or
more tissues with hemangiosarcomas/hemangiomas was higher in MD and HD males
as well as HD females compared to controls.

Non Neoplastic
Non-neoplastic findings related to etrasimod (based on increased incidence and/or
severity compared to controls) included angiomatous hyperplasia in various tissues as
well as findings in the lungs and thymus.
Angiomatous hyperplasia, consisting of focal to multifocal localized areas of increased
capillaries and small vessels lined by flattened endothelium with no distortion of the
adjacent normal tissue, was observed in various tissues (bone marrow, epididymides,
heart, liver, subcutis, femorotibial joint) in HD males with increased overall incidence
compared to control animals. Angiomatous hyperplasia was observed in various tissues
in females (control or treated) as well, but only considered drug-related in the uterus, (

19
Reference ID: 5152963

NDA # 216956

Sarah J. Morgan, PhD

thick-walled blood vessel within the myometrium that was more prominent near the
broad ligament); this finding was observed with increased incidence and severity in MD
and HD females compared to controls.
Etrasimod-related changes in the lungs, including minimal to marked alveolar
histiocytosis, minimal to moderate alveolar eosinophilic accumulation, minimal to mild
multifocal fibrosis, minimal to mild osseous metaplasia, and minimal fibrin deposition,
were observed in all treated groups compared to controls; two or more of these findings
were often observed in affected animals.
Finally, minimally to moderately increased lymphocytes were observed in the medulla of
the thymus with increased incidence and/or severity in MD and HD males and females
compared to controls.

Toxicokinetics
Etrasimod was not detected in any control group samples (limit of quantitation = 500
ng/mL).
Etrasimod was quantifiable in plasma samples from all treated animals through 24
hours post-dose on day 364 of dosing. Cmax and AUC for etrasimod increased
approximately proportionally with increasing dose over the tested range. Tmax occurred
between 2-12 hours with no clear effect of dose. No sex differences in toxicokinetic
parameters were observed.

Based on the findings in a previous 6month study in rats (Study TX14009), the
high dose of 20 mg/kg/day was expected
to exceed 25 times the clinical exposure
(AUC) for both etrasimod and the
metabolite M3. The low and mid doses
were selected to allow for adequate AUC
spacing between doses.

Reviewer Carcinogenicity Conclusion (negative/ positive): Negative
ECAC Carcinogenicity Conclusion (negative/ positive): Negative
Key Study Findings
 Etrasimod did not have a dose-dependent effect on the mortality rate or
significantly increased mortalities in treated animals compared to vehicle
controls. The statistical reviewer’s analysis showed the numbers (percent) of
deaths that occurred prior to scheduled termination of the group were 73%, 67%,
57%, 62%, and 72% in male rats and 73%, 65%, 55%, 58%, and 58% in female
mice in the water control, vehicle control, low dose, mid dose, and high dose
groups, respectively.
 Etrasimod-related non-neoplastic lesions were observed in the lymphoid tissues
and brain of males and females at all tested doses as well as in the liver of all
treated females and males in the MD and HD groups.
 No statistically significant increases in tumor incidences occurred in this study.

Tumor Findings:
Independent analyses of the tumor findings were conducted by FDA statistical reviewer,
Feng Zhou, M.S.9,10 The tumor analysis showed no statistically significant positive doseresponse relationship between the treated groups and vehicle control group for tumors
of any type.

Observations and Results
Mortality
Animals were observed at least twice daily for morbidity and mortality. No morbidity or
mortality was attributed to administration of etrasimod (named APD334 in this report).
Survival in the male HD group (20 mg/kg/day) reached 20 at Week 86, and dose
administration was suspended at that time. Additionally, survival in the male and female
WC groups reached 20 at Week 90 and Week 89, respectively. As such, all surviving
25

Sarah J. Morgan, PhD

males were necropsied during Weeks 90 and 91 and all surviving females were
necropsied during Weeks 89 and 90.
Independent analyses of survival were conducted by FDA statistical reviewer, Feng
Zhou, M.S.9,10 The survival distributions of rats in all treatment groups were estimated
using the Kaplan-Meier product limit method. For control, LD, MD, and HD groups, the
dose-response relationship was tested using the likelihood ratio test and the
homogeneity of survival distributions was tested using the log-rank test.
The statistical reviewer’s analysis showed the numbers (percent) of deaths that
occurred prior to scheduled termination of the group were 73%, 67%, 57%, 62%, and
72% in male rats and 73%, 65%, 55%, 58%, and 58% in female mice in the WC, VC,
LD, MD, and HD groups, respectively. No statistically significant dose-response
relationship in mortality was observed in treated males and females compared to the VC
group. Death rates for the WC group were similar to that of the VC group for both male
and female mice.

Feed Consumption
Decreased feed consumption was observed in all treated groups compared to the WC

Gross Pathology

Histopathology
Peer Review Conducted: Yes (Joelle Ibanes, DVM, DACVP)
Historical Control Provided for Tumor Incidence: No
Neoplastic
According to the Applicant, there were no neoplastic findings related to etrasimod
administration. A statistically significant incidence of carcinoma of the Zymbal gland was
reported in female mice treated with etrasimod compared to controls (p = 0.0244 by the
Poly-3 trend test). However, the absolute incidences of this tumor were low in all groups
(0/74, 1/74, 0/64, 0/65, and 2/63 in the WC, VC, LD, MD, and HD groups, respectively),
no statistically significant differences were observed using other tests (Fisher exact test,
Cochran-Armitage trend test, and Peto test), and a similar trend was not observed in
male animals. The incidence in females at the high dose (2/63 or 3.2%) was slightly
above the top of the historical control range for females (1.7%). Based on these factors,
this finding was considered incidental and not related to treatment with etrasimod.
Independent analyses of survival were conducted by FDA statistical reviewer, Feng
Zhou, M.S., using the SAS data sets received on October 14, 2022, via NDA
216956/0001.9,10 The tumor data were analyzed for dose-response relationships and
pairwise comparisons of the WC and VC groups separately with each of the treated
groups using the Poly-k method (see Statistical Review for further details).
Based on the criteria of adjustment for multiple testing, there were no statistically
significant positive dose-response relationships between the treated groups and VC
group for male and female rats. The tumor incidences were similar between the vehicle
control group and water control group.

Non Neoplastic
Non-neoplastic findings related to etrasimod administration (based on increased
incidence and/or severity compared to controls) were observed in the lymphoid system
(lymph nodes, spleen, thymus) and brain of males and females at all tested doses as
well as in the liver of all treated females and males in the MD and HD groups.
Etrasimod-related effects in the lymphoid organs of males and females at all doses
included minimally to moderately increased lymphocytes in the thymus medulla,
minimally to markedly decreased lymphocytes in the marginal zone of the spleen,

30
Reference ID: 5152963

NDA # 216956

Sarah J. Morgan, PhD

minimally to markedly increased extramedullary hematopoiesis in the spleen, as well as
variably increased incidence and/or severity of sinus dilation, sinus histiocytosis, and/or
sinus erythrocytosis/erythrophagocytosis in the iliac, mandibular, mediastinal,
mesenteric, and/or renal lymph nodes. Findings in the thymus were generally not dosedependent in incidence or severity, although increased severity (moderate) was
observed in HD males. Decreased marginal zone lymphocytes and increased
extramedullary hematopoiesis in the spleen were observed without dose-dependent
incidence or severity. Lymph node findings also did not show clear or consistent dosedependence.
An increased incidence of minimal to mild focal thalamic mineralization, frequently
bilateral, was observed in the brain in all etrasimod-treated animals with no dosedependence. The mineralization was characterized by focal accumulations of circular,
densely basophilic concretions within the thalamus.
In the liver, increased incidence and/or severity of basophilic foci of cellular alteration
were observed in females at all doses compared to controls. Increased incidence and/or
severity of clear or eosinophilic foci of cellular alteration were observed in MD and HD
males and females. Increased incidence of minimal or mild bile duct hyperplasia was
observed in MD and HD males and females compared to controls. Findings in the liver
were generally not dose-dependent in incidence or severity.

Toxicokinetics
Etrasimod was not detected in any control group samples (limit of quantitation = 5.00
ng/mL).
Etrasimod was quantifiable in plasma samples from all treated animals through 24
hours post-dose on day 364 of dosing. Cmax and AUC for etrasimod increased
approximately proportionally with increasing dose over the tested range. Tmax occurred
between 2-12 hours with no clear effect of dose. AUC and Cmax values for etrasimod
were approximately 2-fold higher in females compared to males at all tested doses.

Summary

This review evaluates statistically the data of the 2-year oral carcinogenicity study of Etrasimod
(referred to as APD334 hereafter) in rats and the 2-year oral carcinogenicity study of APD334
in mice. The review analyzes the dose-response relationship of tumor incidences and mortality
(including tumor-related mortality).
For the rat study:
The reviewer’s survival data analysis results showed that the survival analyses didn’t show any
statistically significant dose response relationship in mortality in males and females when
compared with vehicle control. The death rates for the water control group were similar with
that of vehicle control group for both male and female rats.
The reviewer’s tumor data analysis results showed that there were no statistically significant
positive dose response relationships between the treated groups and vehicle control group for
male and female rats. The tumor incidences were similar between the vehicle control group and
water control group.
For the mouse study:
The reviewer’s survival data analyses show a statistically significant dose response relationship in
mortality in both males and females when compared with vehicle control group (Ps<0.05). The
mortality rates in high dose treated groups were statistical significantly higher than vehicle
control group for both male and female mice (Ps<0.05). The death rates for the water control
group were similar as that of vehicle control group for both male and female mice.
The reviewer’s tumor data analysis results showed that APD334 cause a statistically significant
positive dose response in the combined incidence of hemangiosarcoma malignant tumor for all
organs in whole body in male and female mice. For this tumor type, the pairwise comparisons
between the vehicle control group and APD334 mid dose (6 mg/kg/day) group and APD334
high dose (20 mg/kg/day) group were statistically significant. See Table 1 below for tumor
incidences and p-values of the tumor types mentioned above. The tumor incidences were
similar between the vehicle control group and water control group.
APD334 also cause a statistically significant positive dose response in incidence of the
combination of hemangiosarcoma malignant tumor with hemangioma, benign tumor for all
organs in whole body in male and female mice. For this tumor type, the pairwise comparisons
between the vehicle control group and APD334 mid dose (6 mg/kg/day) group and APD334
high dose (20 mg/kg/day) group were statistically significant. See Table 1 below for tumor
incidences and p-values of the tumor types mentioned above. The tumor incidences were
similar between the vehicle control group and water control group.
